# Chapter Summaries

**HOW TO READ THIS FILE. Append-only, one block per volume, in chapter order. **The block that matters to the current phase is *Volume 08, Batch 0003* and within it the last ten entries, which are Chapters 0371 to 0380; the blocks for Volumes 01 to 06 are in `state/archive/chapter-summaries.md.volumes-01-to-06.md` and a writer that needs one should ask for the volume rather than read the file. This line named a Volume 05 phase and had done since Volume 05 closed; corrected on 2026-09-27 by the Batch 0003 review repair pass.** It was over 600 KB; the Volume 08 Batch 0003 review repair pass moved every block before *Volume 07* to `state/archive/chapter-summaries.md.volumes-01-to-06.md` on 2026-09-27, verbatim and with a SHA256 of what it moved. Read one block.**

## Volume 07 — A Word For The Line

**Ten chapters on disk, days 301 to 310, one chapter to one day, and there is no double day in this volume. The chapter number is the day number. The run was recomputed from day 1 being a Tuesday: 301 Monday, 302 Tuesday, 303 Wednesday, 304 Thursday, 305 Friday, 306 Saturday, 307 Sunday, 308 Monday, 309 Tuesday, 310 Wednesday, and the Short Month days 46 to 55, the ordinal being the day less 255. No chapter uses an ordinal for a month. The full record of the ten is `state/batch-summaries/volume-07-batch-0001.md` and the rules they put on a page are at `bible/power-system.md` §41.**

**Chapter 0301 — A Notice In A Hand.** Day 301, a Monday, the forty-sixth day of the Short Month. A lane past two gates, the shut gate from about the seventh hour, then a room over a market to about the ninth. **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register walks up a lane nobody sent her up, and a barrow of boards is standing against the wall and has been standing there for five days counting the day it was put there as one of the five, and the arrangement for taking them up was for a Thursday, and the Thursday is in three days.** She writes a question in her own hand, in writing, to everybody who will be in the room on the fiftieth of that month, four days off, **unsigned, with no name at the top of it**, and leaves it on the gate wall under a stone off a doorstep, and does not call at a door. **The woman of about fifty at the gate, who has not been asked anything by anybody in nine years, is not asked anything, and says four things out loud to a post and to a barrow of boards and to nobody, and one of them is that she has not read the sheet.** At about the ninth hour a slate comes up in the room over the market with three things on it and the third is four lines long and says the ground on that lane has to be settled before the season turns. **The register-keeper says out loud that she cannot say where the line runs and that she is not going to look at it, and nobody in the room asks her why, and a man of about thirty-one who wants to ask her why more than anybody in the room does not.** Change: the volume's central pressure is on a page and nobody in the room can answer it.

**Chapter 0302 — A Slate With Three Things On It.** Day 302, a Tuesday, the forty-seventh. The room over a market, the sixth hour to the fourth. Three things on a slate: the return for the quarter, the case that came up the river in a cart with a docket on it and has not been opened, and four lines about ground. **A man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living is in the room for two sections with a box of nails and twice interrupts by naming a fact about the building — a wet joist at the north corner and a sill that has never been repaired.** A boundary in this world is settled by two parties agreeing where it runs or by nobody walking it, and there is no third way. **The register-keeper is asked the word in the third place of the Crown's page and says before the question is finished that she is not going to define it, and stops there, and the word is not defined in this chapter.** The room does the return and leaves the other two where they are, and the man of about thirty-one puts his hand on the table and says the room has been standing on the wrong side of a thing since before the thirty-ninth of that month, and says he will not say what the thing is until the deed is read. Change: nobody can settle a boundary here with any paper anybody holds, and nobody has said the word out loud.

**Chapter 0303 — The Deed, And A Sheet Attached To It.** Day 303, a Wednesday, the forty-eighth. Two rooms and a stair, the sixth hour to the ninth. **A man of about forty-three who keeps other people's papers takes a pin out of the corner of a deed and separates a sheet that has been pinned to it for as long as he has had the box, which is eleven years.** He reads both out in the local language in daylight with a date in chalk on the door to about nine people, and says what is on them and not what he thinks is on them. The deed is about forty years ago and says where the boundary of what was bought is in the words of a house four hundred miles inland. **The sheet is a clerk's copy of an entry in a public register in a town four hundred miles inland, entered a hundred and forty years before the deed was written, copied about forty years ago, and nobody in this city can go and look at the page it was copied from.** It names nineteen stones; eight of them are different, further down the road and in a different order. **Adrian is in the room once, named once by the woman counting what is read out, reading the clerk's hand over the man's shoulder, and is not asked one question about the boundary.** A woman of about thirty-three asks about the order the stones are in and is told that is the order the page has them in and that nobody knows whose order it is. **The eight names at the foot of the copy are not read, and the chapter is about the not reading, and it does not say why.** Change: both papers are true and the room has been treating one of them as a mistake since it walked in.

**Chapter 0304 — The Thursday, And The Boards That Do Not Go.** Day 304, a Thursday, the forty-ninth, and the arrangement for the boards was for this day. The shut gate and the wall, the sixth hour to the fourth. **A man of about thirty-eight with a barrow of boards comes at the sixth hour and does not take them up, and gives four sentences, and asks in advance not to be thanked, and is not thanked.** The boards are the only thing standing on a road that nine households took back, and if they go there is nothing on the road at all, and a man who takes his own boards home on a Thursday is a man who cannot say afterwards that he had been there. A length of new rope hooked on the barrow handle is not his and he puts it back the way he found it. **The woman of about fifty at the gate works the gate the whole time he is talking, says four sentences to him and three to a post, and is asked nothing by anybody.** The one thing only the two of them know — that she does not know what the line is and told him so — is not said in the chapter and nobody asks him. The boards are still against the wall on the Friday and the Saturday. Change: a man with a barrow decided not to empty a wall and gave a reason that is his, and nobody was angry with him, and he is not a good man and is not a bad one.

**Chapter 0305 — A Tin, And A Question In A Woman's Own Words. The batch's midpoint.** Day 305, a Friday, the fiftieth. A kitchen on that lane, the sixth hour to the eighth. **A man of about thirty-one has put a sheet with nothing on the top of it on a table and gone out of the room and shut the door, and a woman of about sixty-nine has not touched it for an hour, and she asks him to tell her the difference between being asked something and being told something.** There is no name at the top of it, there are four days, and the room is on the fifty-fourth, which is a Tuesday. **She reads the sheet twice, says it is written well, and notices that it says *the holding* and does not say whose holding, and says she has a tin on that shelf with a thing in it that is thirty years old, and that she is not going to open it for a person who came into her kitchen at six in the morning.** She will answer in four days. **The man of about thirty-one visibly decides not to ask her what is in the tin, and says in a mouth that he was not going to, and a person who is not one of the people the question is about cannot be put in a column.** **She writes the question in her own hand on the back of the sheet he left, does not sign it, and he does not ask her to.** Her brother's son, a man of about sixty-three who farms the half, comes in out of a wet ditch with mud to the knee, says four words — the ditch wants water and there is none — and goes out. Change: a question went out of a kitchen in a woman's own hand for a room four days off, and it was not signed, and the four days started on the day it was written.

**Chapter 0306 — A Ditch, A Channel, And Two Pair Of Boots.** Day 306, a Saturday, the fifty-first. Eleven miles of flats and a channel, the fifth hour to the seventh. The light is flat and there is no hill and the cold is out of the north across open ground, and what is under about four pairs of hands is a spade, a line of willow, a bucket and about nine feet of wet silt. **A channel that takes its water from a gate and has had no water since the hundred and ninety-sixth day of this flood is a hole in the ground, and a hole in the ground is where everything goes, and a rack that has not been worked does not come off.** A man of about sixty-three digs; a man of about sixty-four, a neighbour a year apart, is about nine hundred paces off digging a ditch that is not his for a house that is not his, unpaid, unasked, and does not say so. **The ditch goes nine feet down and about as far again as a man can throw a stone and stops, and it stops because the ground went firm, and that is the first time anybody has said out loud that the two parts of one holding are not the same ground.** The silt goes in a barrow to the head of the channel, which is not beside the ditch. Change: a man put nine feet of somebody's ground back in the wrong place and has an opinion about where the line runs and does not say so.

**Chapter 0307 — Four Times A Year Since Before The Water.** Day 307, a Sunday, the fifty-second. A room over a market and then a lane, the sixth hour to the ninth. **A man of about fifty-seven is in the room with a length of new rope he has not used, and he has paid money for half a holding and cannot show anybody where the half is.** He wants one thing on a page that will not come out again. **He says nine sentences about himself and the ninth is that he has been answering a return four times a year since the winter before the water, that he has never been able to read the whole of the question at the bottom of it, that nobody has ever told him what it is for, and that he is asking now about a different thing entirely and did not know until that morning it was the same thing.** He offers to pay for the walking — four men, at a rate he names out loud. **The room does not take the money and does not say no, and the money is still in his hand.** **Adrian is in the room once, named once by the register-keeper, reading the clerk's hand, and is not asked one question about the boundary.** At about the ninth hour, in daylight, in front of about nine people, the man of about fifty-seven asks whether anybody in this city has ever walked a line with a chain; nobody answers, and about four people in that room have worked out that nobody has and that the answer is going to be a yes. He goes down the lane with the rope over his arm, stops at a shut gate and does not go through it, and the woman of about fifty is at the gate and does not ask him one question. **The register-keeper puts her hand flat on the second slate and writes nothing on it, and says out loud that she is not going to write it down, and that it was his and is going to stay his, and that a thing in her hand with her name beside it and not his is a page and not a room, and that she has kept a register in this city for nine years and knows exactly what a page is for — which is the argument she makes to a shut gate in Chapter 0301, in her own words and not in his.** Change: a man who is not angry is harder to say no to than a man who is, and a refusal that is not free costs money that is still in somebody's hand. **The review repair pass on this batch cut a welded restatement of his ninth sentence that had been fused onto the end of the closing narration paragraph, at a point where the chapter has already said he left the room, and rewrote this closing turn so that it is her answer to him rather than a second copy of him. No beat was removed: the money is still in his hand, the four men are still offered, and the chain question is still the last thing he says in that room.**

**Chapter 0308 — Four Hundred And Forty, And The Only Page It Is On.** Day 308, a Monday, the fifty-third. A salt wharf in this city and a stall, the sixth hour to the seventh, and the light is on the water and there is no hill. **A man of about thirty-eight keeps a tally on eleven miles of flats and has put about four hundred and forty in a return four times a year since the winter before the water, and the woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall at that wharf says the number out loud every morning, to nobody.** He is asked, for the first time in nine years, what the return is for, and says he does not know and has never known and that the return has never said. **The number was given to him by a man who is dead or gone, and he has added to it in his own head about four times a year for nine years, and the figure he sends is not the figure he has, and the figure he has was not printed.** The register-keeper answers him in her own words without a slate: the return has a heading, the heading has four places, and there is no place on it for the person the four places are about. **She then says the sentence the chapter is for: a schedule that nobody can fill in will be filled in from the returns, and it will not be anybody's doing, and it will go out correct and be about the wrong people.** He says he is not relieved, says he would like to know what the return is for and is not going to be told, and goes back to the tally, where a figure sits that is not the figure he sends. Change: about four hundred and forty people are on a number in one man's head and on no page anybody else holds, and the only correct paper in this city would fill the Crown's empty place with them without anybody deciding to.

**Chapter 0309 — Six Days Left In It, And Nothing After It. The hinge.** Day 309, a Tuesday, the fifty-fourth. A room over a market, the sixth hour to the fifth. **A sheet comes back with a question written on the back of it in a woman's own hand, and it is answerable, and it went out on the fiftieth and the fifty-fourth is four days.** It is answered on the slate in the form there is, which is the fifty-fourth day of the Short Month, and the register-keeper says out loud to nobody in particular that this is the last answer this city will carry this month's word on. A man of about forty-three says in a mouth that this happened once before, that a woman stood in this room and named a month on the first day of the one that came after the Long Month, that nobody announced it, and **that a form which runs out does not fail, it stops being a form and becomes a thing somebody has to make up, and the person who has to make it up is whoever is standing in the room when somebody asks for a day.** **The Short Month is sixty days, this is the fifty-fourth, the sixtieth is six days off, and the sixtieth is a Monday, and there is no form in four hundred miles for the day after it.** The room does not panic and carries on. **At about the fifth hour a man of about twenty-five, one of four people who came to this city with a question and have not left, asks out loud what the day after the sixtieth would be, and nobody in the room answers him.** Change: this city's calendar has run out and the room found out in daylight with a date on the door, and nothing names what comes next, and the naming is not this chapter's.

**Chapter 0310 — A Word, In A Room, In Daylight, With A Date On The Door. The batch's climax.** Day 310, a Wednesday, the fifty-fifth. A room over a market, the sixth hour to the fourth, and the light comes in over a step at the back with no name on it and stops about the middle of the floor. **A man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall and wrote the ninth line on a wall nine miles up the road in a harder piece of chalk is asked a question about a mark of about a foot and about two inches, and the figures came to the room from a man with a barrow who is not in it.** He is asked to say the word, and **he says it, in one word, in the local language, in daylight, with a date in chalk on the door, and the word is *scar*, and the chapter does not define it and does not say what it means.** He then says four sentences: it is not a path, it is not a watercourse, it is not a furrow, it is not a boundary, the mark is not a pattern, and he is not going to say what it is because he has found out in the last fifty days what a word on a page is for and is not going to find out twice. **He is asked to say it again so the register-keeper can get it right, and does not, and she gets it right, and the chapter says so and does not say how.** The word goes on her second slate in chalk with the date beside it and no name on the line, at his asking, because a line with a name at the top of it is a line somebody can come back to. The chalk that writes it is the piece of chalk nobody was going to use, which has lain on that table since the forty-third day of that month. Nobody in the room asked him about the ninth line. He is frightened and nobody can tell, and at the end he says in a mouth that he has found a way to rub a word out that has nothing to do with a cloth or a wall, and that it is to say it once in a room with a date on the door, and that he has done it about nine times in his own head this afternoon. Change: **there is now a word in this city for a thing about nine hundred paces off a shut gate, and a word in a city is the cheapest thing anybody has ever bought, and the man who bought it does not know what he sold.**

**Chapter 0311 — Nine Places, And A Sheet With No Day On It.** Day 311, a Thursday, the fifty-sixth of the Short Month. A road out of the city, the sixth to the ninth hour, and the hands are on a folded sheet of road with about nine places on it and a lump of chalk carried in one of the folds and nothing else. **Tamsin Quill is on a page for the first time since the seventeenth day of the Short Month, and she is on it working, and she is on a road and not in the room over a market, because the two women of twenty-nine are never in one room.** Her second handle is invented here and in no other chapter: **a folded sheet of road with about nine places on it, folded the same way every time — long way in half, then in half again across, and the corner that came last goes under — and the reason she gives once is that a sheet folded some other way can be taken for a notice, and a notice has a day on it, and hers has not got a day on it and is not going to get one.** She walked nine places in three days asking one question at each, *what is this ground called, and who called it that*, and got eight answers. Seven of the eight are the two names the two documents use, written seven different ways. The eighth is **the nine acres**, and it came from a woman of about thirty-three behind a bread board at a turning where four streets come together, who said that nobody has ever been asked and that it is not a name anybody writes down because there is nowhere to write it. The ninth place has eight houses in it and nobody came to a door and **she did not go back, because going back is how a person turns a question into a pressure.** The ninth line is left empty and not written *no answer*, because an empty line can be filled in and a line that says *no answer* can be argued with. **Adrian is named once, by her, on a road, and is not asked one question, and the consent fracture is not mended and neither of them says a word about why, and the reason is nine years old and it is hers as much as his.** Change: a third name for a piece of ground is on a page in four hundred miles, and it came from a person and not from a document, and the sheet that carries it has no day and no name on it and eight households have asked to keep it.

**Chapter 0312 — The Fifty-Seventh, And A Sheet That Came Down.** Day 312, a Friday, the fifty-seventh of the Short Month. A lane, a shut gate, and **the woman of about fifty at the shut gate is in this chapter and is asked nothing in it and nobody was sent to her.** Her five handles are the same five: a brass key on a tarred cord in the apron pocket, a bar of ash in two brackets, a wooden hook, a piece of chalk, and a date in chalk on the post every morning with nothing rubbed out. The barrow of boards is still against the wall, where it has been since the forty-first of that month counting that day as one of them. The asking the register-keeper put up on the wall on the forty-sixth is still on it, and the room it was written for was on the fiftieth. **The wind took it off the wall at about the second hour of the afternoon and it went into the wet at the foot of it, and she picked it up and read it, having said out loud on the forty-sixth that she had not read it and was not going to, and that reading it would be the beginning of having been asked.** The back of that sheet is blank, and a second sheet in this city has a question on the back of it in another hand, and she has not seen it and is not going to. **She puts it back up and leaves it up and says the two reasons out loud at a post: a paper with nothing at the top of it cannot be taken off a wall on behalf of a person, and there is nobody to take it off for.** The man of about thirty-eight with the barrow comes down the lane at about the fifth hour, does not stop, puts his hand flat on the boards, takes it off, and goes on down. **She says out loud that she has read it and has not been asked and that the two are separate facts and she is keeping them apart on purpose.** Change: the only decision anybody has made about a page on that lane in about a month was made to a post, and it was to leave a thing where it was.

**Chapter 0313 — What A Notary Is For, And What She Is Not Going To Do Today.** Day 313, a Saturday, the fifty-eighth of the Short Month. A room over a market, the seventh hour to the ninth, and **Sera Vail, a notary of fifty, twenty-six years a notary, is in the room and her bag is shut on the table all day.** The register-keeper asks her for **a day** and not a month, and about four people in that room have worked out that the difference between the two asks is the whole of a week. The notary names the cost out loud first, as she does: her name at the top of it, the copy in every room four hundred miles off that has a table and a clerk's habit, and if the days do not stop where the word on it stops then the paper is wrong and she is the wrong; **and the second part, which is that yesterday morning she had a form and this morning she has not, and she has not told anybody that in nine years, and she is telling it because she is about to write a form again.** A man of about forty-three asks the difference between entering a form and making one and is told **twenty-six years and one word**, and that neither is what is being asked, because the day after the sixtieth is the only day a form belongs on and she is not going to make one on a day she has a form for. **She will not spend one of the nine days of writing she has left on the only thing anybody has asked her for this month.** The man of about fifty-seven, with a length of new rope he has not used, says nothing and has been answered at length four days ago and not answered at all this morning. **Adrian is named once by the register-keeper, is reading the clerk's hand on the sheet pinned to the corner of the deed, and is asked nothing.** He says that the copy is the only page in four hundred miles on which anybody has written down the shape of a form and that nobody copied the shape, only the words, and that he is not going to make it an argument because a room where a woman is going to be asked to make one in three days is not a room for his argument. Change: the room has a day to work towards, and the day of the week is a Tuesday, and the notary will be in the room on it and will not say in advance what she wants on the door.

**Chapter 0314 — The Column That Is Not There.** Day 314, a Sunday, the fifty-ninth of the Short Month. A room over a market, the seventh hour to the fifth, and the hands are on a register with nine years in it, a second slate, a box with a deed in it and a sheet pinned to the corner of the deed, and a waxed slate in a case. **The discovery of the chapter is a missing column, and it is found by a person in a room in daylight with a date on the door, and it is about the four days of notice.** The register-keeper opens her book at a page about forty days back on purpose and says she has kept it three columns — what was asked, what came back, and the day — for nine years and has never wanted a fourth and wanted one on Tuesday. **Hettis Roke puts a waxed slate on the table face up and then turns it face down and says the day a sheet went out is not in any of her three columns, and that this is why she cannot check it and he has known it for nineteen years and has never wanted to be able to.** He refuses the room his roll outright, says its shape out loud, and asks that his refusal be entered. The man of about forty-three says that he has the column she wants, in a book, on a shelf, in a room he lets nobody into, **and has never read it back in eleven years, because if he did he would find nine mistakes and then have to decide what to do about nine, and he has never wanted to decide what to do about anything on a Sunday.** Adrian is named once, reading the register over her shoulder, and is asked nothing. The finding itself is the man of about forty-three's: **you are not missing a column for the day the sheet went out, you are missing it because of the four days, and every answer in your nine years is attached to the day you heard it and not the day you asked.** The register-keeper will neither put a fourth column in nor refuse one, and says both out loud. Roke has eight people of his own on no page in four hundred miles, has wanted a page for them for nine years, **does not ask and does not give a reason, and the not-giving is the reason.** Change: a room found out that its own record cannot be checked by anybody including the person keeping it, and that the four days of notice is the only instrument that would supply the missing figure, and the day after tomorrow is the last day anybody in this city can write in a form that exists.

**Chapter 0315 — A Date On A Plain Slate, Read Out Loud.** Day 315, a Monday, **the sixtieth and last day of the Short Month.** A room over a market, before the light to the fifth, and the hands are on a plain slate, a lump of chalk, a cloth, and about nine sheets with nothing at the top of any of them. **A girl of seventeen, on no roll of anything, who has copied every asking anybody has written in this flood and has signed none of them, writes her name at the top of the slate before she writes anything else on it, in the ordinary way, and says the reason out loud: because of the habit, and it is her slate and nobody has ever asked her for it.** The register-keeper, who has not put a name at the top of a sheet for four years, looks at the name and **does not ask for it to be taken off, and never says so out loud to anybody.** The girl copies the last asking, wipes the slate and copies it again, because a thing that is going to be read out in a room has to be read off a copy and not off the paper it came on, and she has never explained that to anybody and nobody has ever asked her. The sixtieth goes on the second slate in chalk and nothing goes under it, and she is asked to read it out, and she looks at the slate for the length of four words deciding whether reading counts as being asked, and decides that it does not, because reading is not answering. **She reads the sixtieth day of the Short Month out loud, and that it is the last one of those, and that there is a Monday on the end of it and the next one is a Tuesday, and she is made to say the second part again and slower, and the register-keeper says she wants the whole room to have it and not just the two of them.** Nobody explained it to her. The man of about forty-three, the only person in that room who could have said something about a name at the top of a page, says nothing at all. She asks the only question she asks in that room — what am I to copy for tomorrow — and **nobody answers, and she is on no roll of anything and there is no address at which a sheet could be put in front of her, and being on no roll of anything is not a qualification and nothing in this month has turned it into one.** Change: the last date this city can write in a form that exists goes on a slate with a girl's name at the top of it, and nobody corrected it.

**Chapter 0316 — A Name, In A Room, In Daylight, With A Number On The Door.** Day 316, a Tuesday, **the first day of a month this city had not got.** A room over a market, the seventh hour to the ninth, and **this is the chapter the whole hinge of Volume 06 was waiting for, and the name goes on a page here for the first time in this manuscript.** The door has no month on it: the register-keeper wrote a count of the days of the flood on it in chalk herself, alone, at the seventh hour, and said the reason out loud before anybody asked — a number a person put on a door is a date until somebody puts a better one on it, and if she is wrong about it then every day after is wrong on the same count, and nobody in four hundred miles can check it. **She gave up a blank space on a door, which is the one thing in this flood nobody has ever had to explain, so that a notary could see what a room looks like when it has nothing.** The notary names the cost again in one sentence, and is told **do it, and I am not going to argue with the cost, because you named it before you were asked.** She rules a line at the top of a sheet and begins. **The man of about fifty-seven stands up off a wall and says one word and refuses her, and not one of his four sentences is about the calendar: a word made in a room is a word somebody can be asked to have made, and he is the man who will be asked, because there is nobody else in that room who could be asked about a boundary and be asked about it honestly.** He says he is not angry and nobody argues with him. She puts her hand flat on the sheet, waits for the light to reach the table, and says, once, in the local language, in daylight, in a room with a number on the door: **the Bare Month.** Nobody asks her a second question about it. He asks how long it is and she declines in front of about nine people and asks for the declining to be entered as hers. The man of about forty-three asks why and she gives a reason that is hers: **she has put a number at the head of a month once, the days did not stop where the number stopped, nobody found that out from the paper, they found it out from the days, and she is not going to guess at a number again in front of about nine people.** What she offers instead is that she will write every day of it that she is asked to write, and that when a clerk writes to her about a date he cannot place she will be able to tell him how many there have been and will not have told him in advance. **The form is five lines with a notary's name under the first, and it is the first page in four hundred miles in about a month with a person's name at the top of it in a room, and the person is a notary of fifty and not a household.** She rubs out the count on the door with a cloth and writes the date in the new word, and the register-keeper writes the same on the second slate. **The man of about fifty-seven does not leave, and at the end asks whether the first day of it is a Tuesday, and is told yes, and says nothing else. He did not know until that morning that the days of the week were still standing, and they are the one instrument in this flood nobody had to make up, and nobody wrote it down.** Change: this city can write a date again, and the month has no number on it, and the only man in the room the new month costs anything to is the man who refused it, and nobody has told him he is wrong. **Adrian is in the room and is in no chapter in any form, and the chapter does not say so.**

**Chapter 0317 — Two Instruments, And A Sheet With A Notary's Name On It.** Day 317, a Wednesday, the second day of that month. A room over a market, and **the sheet with the notary's name on it has been on the table since the seventh hour and nothing has been written on it, and that is the most alarming object anybody has brought into that room in about a month, because it is the first thing in the room that anybody in four hundred miles could send for.** A woman of about thirty-three, who has carried a question about the order of nineteen stones since the forty-eighth of that month, asks the chapter's question: **there is a name at the top of that and five lines and there is nowhere on it to say what a day was for, and that is a form for having a day in and not a form for doing anything**, and she says she has been wrong about this room since the forty-eighth and would like to be told. The man of about forty-three reads the five lines twice, the second time more slowly, and finds a space under the last line about the width of two fingers with nothing in it, and says a space is where a number goes when a person has decided not to write one, and that he has found four of those in nine boxes and two of them are a hundred and forty years old, and that he is not going to make a law of it. **Adrian is named once by the register-keeper, is reading the sheet, and is asked nothing.** The man of about fifty-seven makes a sound with his mouth that is not a word, is asked to say it as a sentence, takes four attempts, and says the chapter's other finding: **a page they can ask to have produced is the only kind there has ever been that settled anything in his life; he has been in a house four hundred miles off twice in forty years and both times a man brought out a book and neither time did the book say where the line ran, and both times the man with the book was right.** The register-keeper does not argue and says the other half out loud because he said his in daylight with a date on the door: **she has kept a register for nine years and put a name at the top of nothing for four of them precisely so that this city would have a thing nobody four hundred miles off can ask to have produced, and as of yesterday morning there are two instruments instead of one, and the one nobody can send for is worth nothing without days to go in it, and the days ran out on Monday.** Hettis Roke comes back, says four sentences, and asks for it entered that a man can refuse a page and be in a room the day after a page is made and both of those are the same man; nobody writes it down. Change: two instruments are not better than one, and the room got the one that can be asked for before the one that cannot, and the man who paid money wants the other kind out loud and is not going to be argued out of it.

**Chapter 0318 — A Man In A Very Good Coat Comes To Say It Is A Problem For Him.** Day 318, a Thursday, the third day of that month. A room over a market, and a man of about forty-one in a very good coat stands for two hours and does not sit down, with a hand going into the coat and coming out again with nothing in it. **He has nine days of a page in that coat and has not shown anybody the front of it and is not going to today, and he came because on Tuesday a woman made a form and he is the only person in four hundred miles already hurt by that happening, and he would rather say it here than have it said for him in about four months by a man who has never met him.** The register-keeper tells him to say it in his own words and not make it small. The man of about forty-three puts the deed and the sheet on the table flat and **Adrian reads the clerk's hand over his shoulder, both columns, twice, and is named once and asked nothing.** The man of about forty-three says the harm is the number that is not on the form, and the woman of about thirty-three says she has been right about it for a fortnight and the day he told them was not the day she was right on, and would rather be right for a fortnight than be told on Wednesday. The man in the coat then says the chapter's finding in four parts: **a form is for the person who has to fill it in later; the person holding the copy in four hundred miles is going to have a day in a word that has no number on it and will have to write the number in himself, and whatever he writes will be wrong and will be his handwriting; a day is not a date; and until Tuesday he could count and could not name, and now he can name and cannot count, and he is the man in the room who has to hold both.** He asks for nothing and says he is not going to be able to say in about four months that nobody warned him. The register-keeper writes the day and puts nothing under it and does not decline. Hettis Roke says one thing to the wall: **a notary's name at the top of five lines is going to be a number at the bottom of something in about four months and there is nothing anybody in this room can do about it.** The man in the coat says he will come back when the room has put something on the form and does not say when. Change: a form two days old is the first page in four hundred miles anybody can ask to have produced, and the first person it costs something to is not in this city, and the room did not amend anything and a woman who has declined four things out loud did not decline this one.

**Chapter 0319 — A Gate, And A Boy Who Says It Back.** Day 319, a Friday, the fourth day of that month. Four streets come together at a gate where the ground has been worn down about two inches in about a hundred years; the hands are on a bar, a barrow with a spade across its handles, a wooden yoke with a strap, and two pails. **A man of about thirty-six, who keeps nothing and holds gates on a turn and is not the keeper of the Redroot gate and is at neither the shut gate nor any other gate in this volume, is at this one, and a boy of about eleven with a yoke and two pails goes through it twice a day and has not stopped there since he was about eight.** The bar is against the post and the bootful is emptied at the near end of the lane before he says anything, and about four people on that road have worked out since that a gate is the only trade anybody in this flood has where the man is better than the page. He says the day out loud to the boy's back — **the fourth day of the Bare Month** — and the boy puts the yoke up on his shoulder and turns round and says it back, and says his mother has had no form for two days and has been doing it out loud at the door. **The man of about thirty-six says that is right, and says he will tell him why she is doing it, and it is that there is nothing to write it on, and that he is saying it at a gate for the same reason, and that it is the entire reason and he is not going to say it twice.** He says it a second time when the boy comes back at the fourth hour and neither of them asks the other anything. A woman of about twenty-eight stops at the gate, which she has not done since the days went back, and neither of them asks the other anything, and she goes through. He then says to the bar, not to the lane, that nineteen years of holding a gate on a turn is that, that nobody has ever put it on a wall, and that he would like the oak to have it. **Nobody in this city has a date on a gate except a woman of about fifty at a shut gate on another road who has written one on a post every morning for nine years, has been asked nothing, has said nothing to anybody about it, and has never met him.** Change: the cheapest instrument in the flood is standing at a gate at the end of four streets, it costs nothing, and no form and no page exists for it.

**Chapter 0320 — A Tin, And A Sheet That Has No Day On It.** Day 320, a Saturday, the fifth day of that month. A room over a market, and a woman of about sixty-nine comes up from the end of the lane at about the eighth hour with a tin in both hands and **nobody has sent for her, because a person who has not been asked anything does not wait to be asked twice.** She puts the tin down and does not open it, decides in front of about nine people that the opening is hers and on her own time, and then opens it. There is a folded sheet thirty years old in her own hand with nothing at the top of it, no day on it and no name on it, and it has been opened and shut about four times in thirty years, **which about four people in that room have worked out is a paper somebody has taken out and looked at and not acted on, thirty years, about nine times.** The man of about forty-three reads it standing up with his hands behind him and does not put it on the table, because a page on a table is a page in a room, and says it is what two households call their own ground and where the water went, and that it is more use than either of the two things on the other side of the table and that it settles nothing. **The register-keeper can now put a date on the outside of it and put the day at the top of an entry, and says so, and that it is the first time in about a month a thing has been able to be said that way and it is the whole of what the form is for, and then names the cost: a page with her hand on it and a date at the top of it is the thing a clerk can send for, and if somebody four hundred miles off sends for it in four years then it goes and does not come back.** The man of about fifty-seven takes his hand off the rope and asks the first question anybody has asked her, in front of about nine people, and says before he asks it that he has never asked her anything. She does not answer straight off. She puts both hands flat on the table on the two sides of the sheet. **She will have it stay on that table: not entered, not copied, not taken out of the room; and if it is ever going to be entered then she is to be asked first and told no if the answer is no, and she is not going to be told afterwards that it has already been done because she was in the room.** Nobody argued with her, and about four people in that room have worked out that a refusal that is not free costs somebody something and that what it cost here was the only page anybody in the room wanted. The sheet goes down about a foot from the second slate and nobody moves it. The man of about fifty-seven says one sentence on his way to the stair about a house four hundred miles off and a book and about forty years, and that he would have given the whole of what he paid for that paper, and that he is not going to say it again and is not going to be thanked for it. **She goes home down the lane with the tin empty in both hands and a paper still on a table in a room over a market, and nothing on it has been entered, and a woman of about fifty at a shut gate on another road has not been told any of it and will not be.** Change: a table over a market now has three accounts of where a line runs on it and one of them has a person in it, and the one with the person in it is the one nobody is allowed to touch, and the form with the notary's name on it still has five lines under it and nothing on them.

**Chapter 0321 — A Rod He Does Not Carry.** Day 321, a Sunday, the sixth day of the Bare Month. Eleven miles of flats from the fifth hour, and then a room over a market. **A man of about fifty-two who digs is at work in the third row, in light flat out of the east and a cold out of the north across open ground and a wind still finding the gaps between the racks, with a spade, a barrow and a bundle of willow about two feet long, and nobody comes out and nobody has sent anybody.** He is setting willow into ground that has gone hard as a road under a rack's leg, and the whole of the method is about nine seconds. **A rod — a length of ash about four feet long with a flat blade ground down on one side — stands on its end in the corner of a hut with a door that does not shut, and it is the only one in four hundred miles and he does not carry it out on any morning, and a rod is a thing a man carries when he is going somewhere to find something and putting things in holes is his trade and finding things is not.** The room over a market, the sixth of that month in chalk on its door, about nine people. The man of about forty-three who keeps nine boxes of other people's papers opens an index in his own hand with four columns including a day, off a shelf it has not been off in eleven years, because there is a reason. **Adrian is in the room once, named once, reading the clerk's hand on the sheet pinned to the corner of the deed, and he says two things: that the room has been treating that sheet as the only account of where the line runs and it is not, and that a woman of about sixty-nine has a paper thirty years old in this city that says where the water went and the only person who could put the two in the same shape is a man who spends his life at the shape of the ground. He is not asked one question.** The man of about forty-three reads three lines, stops with his thumb on the third, and says the name out loud once: **Nev Cutler, fifty-two, in his own hand and not a clerk's, the place the index gives is the flats and not a house, the trade it gives is digging.** The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register writes the day on her second slate and puts nothing under it and says out loud, with a date on the door, **that nobody in this city has ever written down a day on which somebody was not sent for, and the register has a column for days, and this is the first thing in nine years she knows the answer to before anybody asks her.** Nobody was sent. **The line in the ground was not measured, dug, counted, probed or resolved in this chapter, and nobody went to look at it, and the rod stayed against the wall.** Change: the only man in four hundred miles anybody could send for is on a page, and the room has decided not to send him, and he does not know either.

**Chapter 0322 — The Man In The Very Good Coat Comes Back Without A Day.** Day 322, a Monday, the seventh. A room over a market, about nine people, the form with a notary's name at the top of it and five lines under it and nothing on them. **A man of about forty-one in a very good coat does not sit down, comes back on a Monday having said he would come back when the room put something on the form and not said when, and says so out loud before anybody asks him.** The register-keeper does not look up from her register. The man of about forty-three has the box at his feet and does not take the deed out. **A woman of about thirty-three, who has carried a question about the order of nineteen stones since the forty-eighth of the Short Month, asks who the form is for, and the answer is that there is one person in four hundred miles who has to fill one in and he is standing up.** He says the finding in four parts: **a day is not a date; the person holding the copy in a room four hundred miles inland is going to have to write a number in himself, whatever he writes will be wrong, it will be his handwriting, and he will not be able to tell anybody how he got it; and until the first day of that month he could count and could not name, and now he can name and cannot count, and he is the man in the room who has to hold both.** He says the number that would go under the first line is the one figure in four hundred miles this city cannot stand behind, and the woman of about twenty-nine said that out loud on the first day of that month when she wrote a count of the days of the flood on a door in chalk and said if she is wrong then every day after is wrong on the same count. **Hettis Roke, forty-four, nineteen years in, with a waxed slate in a case, puts the case on the table face down and then turns it face up and does not open it, and says he has sent a number four times a year for nineteen years to an office and a clerk wrote it in a book and nobody came, and that he has never once written a figure he was not standing there for, and that he is not going to be the man who puts a number under that line, and he would like it entered that he said so before anybody asked him and not after.** The register-keeper fills the form in herself at about the fourth hour, in her own three columns, and the first of them says what was asked and the second says what came back and the third says the day and the fourth is not in her book, and what came back was nothing, and she says she has never minded that and minded it that morning. Roke fills in a second thing on the back with a gate, a set of days and a number, and there is a space for a person and he does not write in it, and the space is for the man in the office. **Nobody asked him what a keeper is, nobody asked him to define a word that is on a page in this city and on a page in the Marches and has never been defined, and the two rolls of the same gates are not in the room and are not compared and nobody says what the difference is worth.** Change: the form is filled in for the first time in four hundred miles and the space under the first line is still the width of two fingers, and the only person in the volume who is harmed by the form is the man holding the copy.

**Chapter 0323 — Four Ways Of Writing One Day, And No Column For It.** Day 323, a Tuesday, the eighth. A room over a market, and the register open at the back and not at the front, which is the oldest page in it and which she has not opened in nine years. **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register says out loud, in daylight, with a date on the door, that there are four ways of writing a day in that book and no column saying which one any row is in: a day counted from the water; a day counted from the water with a different word in front of it, running on past the number the first one stops at without a mark; a day with a name in front of it, of which there are about nine hundred, in two different names for the same stretch of time; and a day with a name in front of it that is three weeks old and has no figure under the name at all. A person reading the book today cannot tell, and she did not leave the column out, and she has never wanted it for nine years because she has never once needed to know.** The man of about forty-three answers the question she puts to it, and the answer is that the day she heard the answer is the day she wrote and she has never once wanted the day she asked, and that this is the same thing the man with the wax said about a column on the fifty-ninth. She says out loud that there is a worse thing in the same book and they are not going to get to it today. **Hettis Roke is in the room with the case in his hand and says he is not going to tell her how he keeps his and she is not going to ask him, and then says one sentence — a gate, a set of days, and a number, that is what I have, it has been that shape for longer than the oldest thing any of the four of us who keeps it has — and says he is not going to be asked what that is worth, which nobody in that room ever does.** The man of about forty-three reads the clerk's copy out a second time and nobody stops him, and says that the nineteen stones and the eight that are different are further down the road and in a different order and the two documents do not agree and neither was made to do the other's job, and that he is not going to resolve it, and then says what he wants said: **there are four instruments in a book in this room and a clerk's hand a hundred and forty years old on a table in this room, and they have got nothing to do with each other, because they are five instruments and none of the five has ever had to be read by anybody who did not already know which one they were holding.** The second thing found in that book on that morning is found by a woman of about thirty-three who has been right about the room twice without being told: **the rows at the back are nine years old and are in an instrument this city stopped writing in years ago, and she can read every word of what was asked and cannot tell you what day any of it happened on, and those are the only rows in nine years that have a day in them a person could stand behind.** **The eight names at the foot of the copy are not read, Chapter 0314's missing column is not resolved, the second slate lies face down until she turns it over to write the day on it, and the word on the other side of it is not said.** Change: a register that cannot tell a question that took four days from one that took nine cannot now even be read as a record, and the one column in it that works without a date in it is the column for what came back.

**Chapter 0324 — Six Sentences, And The Sixth Is The One That Costs.** Day 324, a Wednesday, the ninth. A room over a market with about nine people in it, and **Tamsin Quill, with the fold: a folded sheet of road with about nine places on it, long way in half and in half again across and the corner that came last under, and the ninth line empty because nobody came to a door and she did not go back.** She says in advance what she is going to say if she is asked. **He is in that room for the whole morning and the name is not on that page: Tamsin Quill says his name once and the chapter does not print it, so he is *he* and the man who brought the plan from end to end, and she then says nothing else about him for the rest of the morning, and he reads the pinned sheet from the top down twice and turns it over to look at the back of it, which is blank, and then puts the plan on the table in about nine words: the line cannot be settled here, the only page in four hundred miles that says where it runs is four hundred miles inland, somebody has to go and get it, there are four people in this city who came here to fetch pages, and he is not asking any of them whether they will go.** Nobody improves on it and the room does not argue with it, because it is correct. The man of about fifty-seven takes the rope off a chair back and puts it over his arm. **She says it in six sentences and the sixth is the one that costs: a man who has paid for half a holding is not going to be answered by four hundred miles and a winter; the people who are going to pay for the not-answering are nine households on a lane and they are the ones nobody in this room has ever had to answer to; a road that a man walks once in a good season is a road those nine households did not give back on the thirty-ninth of that month; so the line is settled here by what is here; what is here is two accounts that disagree and a line nobody has measured and neither is going to change in a season; and the settlement is that it is not settled, and that is a thing that can be written down with a day on it, and a thing that cannot be written down cannot be argued about, and it has been argued about for forty years.** The man of about forty-three sits through four minutes without once putting his hand on the only page in four hundred miles that says where the line runs. Nobody writes it down. **The man of about fifty-seven says the only thing he says in that room: then I have lost a season and I have still got a certainty, and I would like both of those entered in whatever this room writes down, and I am not going to say that anybody in this room is wrong, because there is nobody in this room who is wrong and I have checked. Nobody argued with him and nobody told him he was wrong.** The two women of twenty-nine are never in one room, and on the morning a decision this size was made the woman who keeps the register was somewhere else in the city. Change: nobody goes, and it is said out loud in a room with a date on the door, and the man who brought the plan does not argue it and is not asked one question about it.

**Chapter 0325 — Nobody Goes, And A Yard Eleven Miles The Other Way.** Day 325, a Thursday, the tenth. A room over a market in the morning, and then eleven miles of flats at about the fourth hour of the afternoon. **Nobody had come to the room to be asked anything, and that is the first morning in about a fortnight on which nobody in a room over a market came in order to be asked a question, and the reason is the six sentences of the day before.** Tamsin Quill has the folded sheet on the table with the fold showing and the ninth line is going to stay empty. She says: nobody goes — and she would like it said that nobody going is a decision and not a stopping, and that it is the first decision anybody in that room has made in about a month that anybody can write down, and that she is not going to be the one who writes it down, and that she is telling the room now so that nobody is surprised on Tuesday, when the first of the four rooms is. **A woman of about thirty-three asks what the room is for and is told it is for the four days and for what is here, and the two things are not the same thing and about four of us have been treating them as one for a month.** The instrument is named before the middle of the morning: **four sheets, one for each of the four, in the words of each of the four, in writing, every one unsigned with nothing at the top of it, one a day for four days, the first going out on the morning of the eleventh of that month for a room on the fifteenth — and when the room asks who writes them, the answer is: they do, a question in your own words is worth more than a question in anybody else's, the reason is nine years old and it is the carrier's and not the asker's, and a sheet with my hand on the top of it asking a person to do a thing is a piece of paper and a sheet with their own hand on it is not.** The man of about fifty-seven asks the only question expected of him — is there a day on them — and is told: **there is a day on the room and there is nothing at the top of any of them, and that is four days, and if it were three days it would not be a notice, it would be a delivery.** He says nobody is going to tell him it is the right thing to do, and is told that nobody is going to tell him the other either, and asks for that to be entered in a room and not in a book. **Nobody was asked whether it would go.** Then eleven miles of flats, and the man of about fifty-two who digs with nine willow in and about a hundred yards of row left, and the rod where it had been on the Sunday. **Adrian comes out along the head of the row at about the fourth hour and stops about nine feet off and stands there, and says nothing for about nine spadefuls, and the man of about fifty-two does not say anything either and does not look at him and does not ask him what he wanted, and the not-asking was not arranged by the man who came out and he is the only man in four hundred miles who could have arranged it. Adrian worked it out, standing in the wind for the rest of the afternoon, and the chapter says he worked it out and does not say he was right.** Change: a decision that nobody goes is carried a whole morning and nobody at any point in it thinks to put the four to the four, and the man who is wrong about the cost works the cost out on a flat eleven miles off without asking one question of the only man who could answer it.

**Chapter 0326 — A Sheet With Nothing At The Top Of It.** Day 326, a Friday, the eleventh. A room over a market, and **the first notice goes out this day and it names its morning here: about nine lines of pencil on a single sheet of paper with nothing at the top of it, in a hand that is not the hand of anybody in the room, and nobody in that room had watched it being written.** The woman who reads it out says in advance that she is not going to say whose it is, and that the second she puts a name at the top of it, it is a different object and she is tired of those. **It says what is going to happen in a room on the fifteenth of that month, it says the day, it says that a person is wanted and not a person with a trade, and it says the last thing twice, and the last thing is that there is nothing at the top of this and that if it is lost it is lost.** Tamsin Quill reads it out in the local language, because that is what a sheet gets read for, and it did not have a single word in it that a person could have written for somebody else. The day is said out loud: the fifteenth, this sheet came into this room on the eleventh, at about the seventh hour, and that is four days, and the four days are the whole instrument. **The man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living, who has carried a question up and down four streets in this city for a month and has waited nine days to say a thing he has known since before the water: a notice in a hand is not a page, a hand is not a page, a hand stays in the hand that made it until that person dies, and a sheet with my hand on it asking a person to do a thing can be argued with by anybody who wants to argue, and a sheet with their own hand on it cannot be argued with at all, and that is not a good thing, and it is the only reason this works.** The sheet has a place on it and not a house, and the place is eight houses in a row with a gap for a cart between two of them out past the last named house, and that is the ninth of the nine places on the folded sheet of road, where a woman got no answer a week ago and wrote nothing on the ninth line. **And a place with eight houses in it is a place and a house at the end of a lane past two gates is only a house, and you cannot leave anything at a house without a name.** The man of about fifty-seven asks what happens to it if nobody comes, and is told nothing happens to it, and says that is not what he asked, and is told to ask it in a room on the fifteenth. She folds it once — a woman who folds everything twice — and the man of about thirty-one goes out at about the second hour of the afternoon with it inside his coat, unasked. **Change: a question in a person's own words goes to a place and not to a house, unsigned, with nothing at the top of it, four days ahead, and the price of that is that nobody in this city can ask whether it arrived.**

**Chapter 0327 — Nine Places, And Not Her.** Day 327, a Saturday, the twelfth, and **the second notice goes out this day, four days ahead of a room on the sixteenth, and it names its morning here: at about the seventh hour, in a bag with a piece of chalk and a cloth in the top of it, and the wind going straight through the room because the window on that side has never shut properly.** A girl of seventeen, who is on no roll of anything, has her name at the top of the plain slate before she has anything else on it, which is what she does, and she has copied the second of the four onto it both sides and has signed none of them and was not going to sign this one. **She asks the only question she asks in that room, which is where does this one go, and is told in front of about nine people that it is to nine places, that she is not one of the nine, and that the person answering does not know the answer to the second half of it.** A man of about twenty-five, who was in that room on the fifty-fourth day of the Short Month and asked out loud what the day after the sixtieth would be and got no answer, has been there since before the light; **his left boot is mended at the front with a length of cord that is not new.** He says he wrote something, that he is not going to say what it is and is not going to be asked, and that the reason is that it says one thing about him in about nine words and a thing that says one thing about him is his, and if he says it here then it is in here and it will be in here on Wednesday when he is not able to look at it. He puts the folded sheet on the table with the ninth line uppermost and does not push it towards anybody. **Nobody asked him whether it was going to work.** The bag goes out with the man of about thirty-one, who carried one sheet out on the Friday and carried nine out on the Saturday and did not tell the room he was going. **The nine are one hand nine times over and not nine hands, and the room does not say the reason out loud, and the reason is that there is nobody in four hundred miles with standing to argue with a notice, because the only person who could say what one means is the person whose hand it is and that person is not going to be asked and has not been asked, and a person who wants to be arguing with a sheet has to put a name at the top of it, and a name at the top of a sheet is the only thing on it that would change what it is. A notice is still a notice after it has been copied, and the nine make the agreement testable.** The girl goes out a quarter of an hour after him and goes the other way, down to the low ground, and she is on no list and she was never on one. **Nine places in about six hours: eight of the nine took a sheet, one of them asked whether it had to be answered and shut about half the door, and the ninth is eight houses in a row with a gap for a cart, where a man with a bag put a sheet on the post because the post was the only thing there that was not a door, and there is a nail in that post about four feet up that nobody knows who put there and everybody on that road has known about since they could walk.** Change: the notice reached nine places and did not reach the one person in the room who carried it to the door, and being unfindable is not a qualification, and the chapter does not solve it.

**Chapter 0328 — A Window, And A Man Who Was Not Asked.** Day 328, a Sunday, the thirteenth, and **the third notice goes out this day, four days ahead of a room on the seventeenth, and it names its morning here.** A man of about forty-eight comes in at about the seventh hour and sits down, and about four people in that room work out that a man who has not been asked anything in a long time has learned to sit down the moment he comes in, because standing about is what a person does when they are expecting to be asked. **His second handle is a folding knife with a hinge that has gone stiff and does not spring, and he opens it and shuts it twice with the thumb of the other hand to make the joint take, and then it will hold about nine minutes, and he has been doing that for years and has never mentioned it and is not going to mention it now.** He puts a sheet on the table face down and says, in daylight, with a date on the door, **that he is not going to be asked whether it is going to work, and he is not.** Nobody asked him. Then he gets up and does the window: the catch had been lifting at the top left corner for nine years, the wind came in under it at about a hand's width, and everybody in that room had stopped noticing it the way a person stops noticing a step. **He takes the catch off in about four minutes with a knife and his thumbnail, without asking anybody for a tool and without once being asked, and the draught stops and the wind does not.** He says the reason out loud and then stops: he did it because he was sitting at it; he has been in about nine of these rooms since before the water and every one of them has had a draught in it; he is the only person in this city who goes up a ladder for a living when he can get the work; there is not enough of it. **The man of about fifty-seven says the only thing he has said to another person in that room in three mornings, which is: you are not going to be paid for that.** The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register writes nothing on the second slate, and then asks him the first question anybody has asked him in this city — will you tell me what you are for — and says she would like it noticed that she has asked it and is not going to ask a second one. He answers: climbing, ladders mostly and a bit of shuttering, about nine days of it since the water, about four of them for people who could not pay, and he is not going to tell the room which is which and neither is he going to tell them at a door. The sheet goes out at about the ninth hour. **Change: a window in a room over a market that had not shut for nine years now shuts, and nobody in that room will ever be able to say who did it, and a man of forty-eight is on a page for the first time in this manuscript.**

**Chapter 0329 — She Says It Herself, And Nobody Had Asked Her.** Day 329, a Monday, the fourteenth, and **the fourth notice goes out this day, four days ahead of a room on the eighteenth, and it names its morning here: at about the ninth hour.** The light comes in early and the window on that side of that room shuts for the first time in nine years, which is the weather in a room not being weather. **A woman of about sixty-one comes in at about the eighth hour, puts a sheet on the table face up, and does not sit down, and her second handle is a stick of hazel she carries in her hand and does not lean on.** Nobody in that room asked her anything about it, and a man of forty-eight came in on the Sunday and said out loud that he was not going to be asked, and nobody has asked anybody anything about any of the four sheets in that room. **She says there are four of us, and the room does not improve on it, and about four people in that room work out that she has said the number out loud and has not gone on to say what the four of them are, and that there is no word in this city for what the four of them are, and that about four of them would have used one a month ago and about four of them have worked out in the last nine days that there is not one, and that they have all arrived at it separately and none of them has said so to anybody.** She asks for it to be said out loud in that room that nobody has ever asked her anything at all, and that she is going to say the other thing, and that the other thing is hers and she is not going to be asked for it either. **Then she says it: I am not going. Four words, in a room with a date on the door, before anybody had asked her.** The room does not make a sound and does not argue with it, and nobody in that room is going to be told she is wrong and nobody is going to be told she is right. The man of about fifty-seven says the room on the eighteenth has three people in it, and is told: three is a number and four is a number and neither of them is what we are, and there is no word in this city for what we are and I have looked for one for a long time and there is not one and that is not an injustice, that is just the language. The register-keeper does not write it down. The carrier takes the fourth sheet out and does not look at her while he does it, and the not-looking was careful. **Before she goes she tells the room the whole of what the four of them were told, once, in about four words, by a man who is not in that room, and that they have never once opened anything, and the room did not ask her a single question.** Change: the fourth of the four says no, unasked, in a sentence that is hers, and a room that has been refusing to ask anybody anything for a fortnight is shown doing the one thing it has not done, which is letting a person answer a question that was never put.

**Chapter 0330 — A Room She Was Written Into.** Day 330, a Tuesday, the fifteenth. **A room over a shop on the low ground, which is not the room over a market, and the fifteenth of that month is written in chalk on its door and there is no column of them going up the frame and nothing rubbed out, because a woman of about thirty-three who keeps that room and has a bread board on the sill of the window in it put the day there at about the sixth hour, and nobody in four hundred miles had put a date on that door before, and a door with one date on it and nothing above it is a notice and not a record.** She says: there is a sheet on eight doors on this row and a room on the fifteenth in it, and I have put the day on my door because I am not having a person walk up my stair at the fourth hour asking me what day it is. **A woman of about twenty-one comes in at about the seventh hour and does not stop at the door, and her second handle is a pen with the nib ground narrow, cut down with a knife, because the one she was given was too broad and she is not going to be given another one.** She says, before she has sat down and before she has looked at six of the nine people in the room: what am I for. Nobody answered her. Tamsin Quill tells her there are four of them and about four of us are in this room and the other three are not in this room and nobody is going to tell her where they are. The woman of about thirty-three puts the bread board down at the end of the sill and says she is going to leave the room for a bit because there are nine people in it and a shop, and she was not asked to leave and about four people wanted her to stay and did not say so. **She says yes. It comes out before anybody has finished the question she was not asked.** Then: I will come on the eighteenth as well. And: **you will not — nobody in this room is going to ask you what the eighteenth is for and nobody is going to tell you either, and one of the four of you said yesterday, in a room over a market, that she is not coming to it, and I am telling you that because you are about twenty-one and I am not going to let you promise yourself a winter in somebody's good coat and find that out on a doorstep.** The man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living says, to the room and not to her: you are not going to be asked whether it will go, and it is the rule that room arrived at by itself a fortnight ago, said out loud and on time. **Nobody asked her.** She picks the sheet up and puts it flat and looks at her own handwriting on it for about as long as it takes to fill a pail, checking two things, whether it is still true and whether it is still hers. She says there is no name at the top of it, and is told: **there is no name at the top of it and there never was, and that is not a courtesy and it is not a protection; if it goes down this stair then about nine people can say whose it was, and if it goes down the stair of this building then nobody in this city can, and you have just worked out which of those two things you are in, and I would like you to have worked it out before you came up, and not after.** She had the cut-down pen in her hand and did not write anything with it, because there is nothing on that table for her to write on. **Change: the first of the four is on a page, in a room she was written into four days before it happened by a question in her own words with no name at the top of it, and nobody in that room asked her whether it would go, and she does not know what she is for, and the room has got a woman of twenty-one in it and no word for what she is.**

**Chapter 0331 — Nine Words, And He Does Not Say Them.** Day 331, a Wednesday, the sixteenth. A room over a shop on the low ground, two doors along from the room the fifteenth was in, about nine people, a window somebody painted shut and somebody else scraped most of again, and what is under about four pairs of hands is a folded sheet of road with about nine places on it, a bread board put down at the end of a sill, a single sheet with nothing at the top of it, and a bag with a piece of chalk and a cloth in the top of it. **The man of about twenty-five comes in before the light and sits down, and his left boot is mended at the front with a length of cord that is not new, and nobody says anything for a while.** He puts the sheet face down and will not have it read out, and says that he is not going to be asked about it and would like the room to have heard him say it twice before anybody decides it is a rule. **Nobody argued with him.** Tamsin Quill has her own fold showing and does not open it. He says yes at about the ninth hour and asks that it be entered that he said it and was not asked, and the room does not enter it, and he says the sheet is nine words long and says one thing about him and will not say it. **THE FINDING IS THAT HE WAS ONE OF THE NINE PLACES THE SECOND NOTICE WENT TO.** The man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living, who carried a question up and down four streets for a month and waited nine days to say a thing he had known since before the water, says he carried nine of these out on the Saturday, and the man of about twenty-five says he took one off the post in the gap for the cart and read it and put it back and has had it in the inside of his coat for four days, and has told nobody, and is telling this room. **Nobody improved on that.** Tamsin Quill then says the chapter's other finding out loud, at about the fourth hour, and it is that the four days are two four days and not one, and that the days a sheet is in a bag and the days it is in a coat are the same days and are not the same days, and that about nine people in the room have been careful about the first kind for a fortnight and nobody has been careful about the second. He answers it by saying it is the first useful thing anybody has said in this city about a piece of paper, and that it is nine years old and his, and that he is not going to say it back. Nobody apologised in the fracture and nobody was going to. **Change: a man who was written into a notice is on a page saying yes without being asked, and has told a room of about nine people that he read his own sheet four days ago and said nothing, and the room's own count of its instrument had a half of it backwards.**

**Chapter 0332 — Eight Of Nineteen, And Nobody Can Say When.** Day 332, a Thursday, the seventeenth. A room at the back of a chandlery with a skylight that has been letting weather in through a frame the roof has moved, about nine people, and what is under about four pairs of hands is a folding knife with a hinge that has gone stiff, a catch, a sheet of paper with nineteen things on it in a woman's own hand and no name at the top of it, and about four feet of tarred twine. **The man of about forty-eight comes in at about the seventh hour and sits down, which is the second morning running that a man who has come out of a notice has sat the moment he came through the door, and nobody asked him to.** He opens and shuts the stiff knife twice with his thumb and it then holds about nine minutes, and about four people in that room have worked out since that a man who has to do that before he can use a tool has been doing it for years and has never mentioned it. **He says yes in about nine seconds, before anybody has sat down properly, and says he would like the room to notice the order of those two things.** Then he gets up and does the skylight nobody asked him to do: the catch has not moved since before the water and the frame has, which is what everybody had been calling a draught for about nine days without a word for it, and it takes him about four minutes with a knife, his thumbnail and a length of tarred twine, and he asks for no tool. **THE FINDING IS ABOUT NINETEEN STONES.** The woman of about thirty-three has carried a question about their order since the forty-eighth day of the Short Month and has the order she has walked them in on a sheet with no day and no name at the top of it.
