He tells her, before he says anything else, that eight of them are not the same stone as the other eleven, and how he knows: colour, then tooling, then mortar, and the whole of the method in about nine sentences. **And then he says where it stops.** Nineteen stones came there in more than one time and the eight are not the first eight and are not the last eight, and that is one thing taken off about nine hundred, and one is not the answer; and a mortar tells you how long it has been mixed and how long it has been standing and it does not tell you a year, and a man can put a stone in a gap in an afternoon and a man can put the same stone in the same gap in a week and the wall is the same wall. He then says that it is not settled and is not going to be settled by him, and that he has come because there is a sheet on the table with her hand on it and a day on the door. She writes nothing on her sheet. **Nobody asked him whether it would go.** Change: a man who fixes stone for a living has told a room that nineteen stones standing in one row were not put there in one go, and the order is still where it was this morning, and a skylight at the back of a chandlery that had been letting weather in through a frame the roof had moved now is not, and nobody in that room will ever be able to say who did it. **The window in a room over a market that had not shut for nine years is a different object and a different morning: it is Chapter 0328, day 328, the same man, the same knife, and it is referred to here as the first one and not as this one, and a later chapter may not merge the two.**

**Chapter 0333 — Three People, And A Stick Of Hazel In A Doorway.** Day 333, a Friday, the eighteenth. A room over a bakehouse on the low ground, about nine people, flour dust in the air of it, and what is under about four pairs of hands is a stiff-hinged knife, a sheet with nothing at the top of it, a door with a handle on it, and about nine paces of floor nobody was standing on. **The three of them are in the room before the seventh hour and they have not been in a room together before**: the woman of about twenty-one, who sat down and did not stop, the man of about twenty-five, who put a hand flat on the table and left it there, and the man of about forty-eight, who opened his knife and shut it twice. Nobody arranged it and nobody in this city had noticed it. **Nobody asked her anything.** The woman of about twenty-one has the cut-down pen in her hand and has not written with it in any room. Then she comes to the door. Nobody sent for her and nobody sent anybody to her. **She had the stick of hazel in her hand and did not lean on it, and nobody in that room got up and nobody turned round, and not turning round was the hardest thing any of the three did that morning.** There is a date in chalk on the door and it is the one the room is for, and she read her own date off it, and she said nothing, and nobody spoke to her, and about four people in that room have worked out since that this is the single hardest thing that room has done since the day it decided that nobody was going to be sent, and that it cost nothing, and that the reason it cost nothing is that she did not have to be talked out of anything. Then she went back up the row and the door stood open for as long as it takes to fill a pail and nobody shut it. **The man of about twenty-five has nine words in the inside of his coat for her and does not say them, to her or to the two of them, and about four people in that room worked out that the condition he set for himself has arrived on the day he set it for.** Two later exchanges add no question about her: the woman of about thirty-three tells the carrier out loud that nobody is going to the door and nobody is going to send anybody to the door, and he says he has carried four of these and has not opened one, and the woman of about thirty-three says she would like that noticed in a room with a date on the door and there is nowhere to put it; and the man of about forty-eight says he has watched a stranger hold a pen for seven hours and has not asked her about it. The woman of about twenty-one then announces, in nine hours, that she has stopped waiting to be told what she is for, and that the not-explaining is half of it. **Change: three of the four are in a room with a date on the door and the fourth is in the doorway and not in the room, and neither of those is a failure, and the room holds for seven hours and decides nothing, and a woman of about twenty-one has taken the only thing off the table that was hers to take.**

**Chapter 0334 — A Sheet With A Name At The Top Of It, Refused.** Day 334, a Saturday, the nineteenth. A room over a market, about nine people, the light on the north side and off the low ground by the second hour, and what is under about four pairs of hands is a case that was shut, a length of new rope nobody had used, a copy of four lines in a man's own hand, and a slate wiped and not written on. **Jonah Pike comes up the stair and does not stop on the landing**, and has not sat down in this room before, and puts the copy face up, which is the opposite of what two men in this city did on the same table four days ago. The man of about fifty-seven is at the step at the back and has come in late. **Before anybody has asked him anything he says he is going to be told what to write and that he is going to say no to it before they do, and that he has thought about it for nine days.** Jonah Pike's proposal takes four minutes and it is good and correct: write out in your own hand what you paid for and cannot show anybody, put your own name at the top of it, and the sheet becomes the one kind of page anybody four hundred miles inland can ask to have produced. **THE MAN REFUSES, AND SAYS WHY, AND THE REASON IS HIS.** A house four hundred miles off, twice in about forty years, a man brought out a book, the book did not say where the line runs, and the man with the book was right both times; **and he would not send it, because a page with his name at the top of it is a page somebody can ask for, and then there is a column, and he has been a column twice in his life and both times it was somebody else's.** The finding of that speech is that the only instrument in four hundred miles that has ever settled anything for him is a page anybody can send for, and that he has just given it up, and that both things happened in about four minutes in one room and neither was arranged. **Jonah Pike then proposes the other thing — nine households gave their days back out of a door about forty days ago and did not write a name at the top of anything — and the man answers that the winter is his and he has paid for it and would like it said that both of those things happened in one morning.** The man of about forty-three is wrong about a shut case in that room and nobody tells him, and the case came up the river before the days went back and is still shut. The road north of the Marches is shut and does not open, and the one correct sentence from an office nine hundred miles off has cost the Marches its winter road and has cost that office nothing. The rope has been carried into about nine rooms since the fifty-second day of that month and used in none of them. **Nobody was asked whether any of it was going to work. Change: a man who is right about most of what he says has refused the one page that would have got his winter back, in a room where nobody is wrong, and he does not leave, and the cost of the morning is one winter and it is in his hand.**

**Chapter 0335 — No Form At All, And A Board Off The Wall.** Day 335, a Sunday, the twentieth. **Eleven miles of flats from the fifth hour: light flat out of the east with nothing in front of it, a cold out of the north across open ground and not off a shoulder, a wind behind the light that has not stopped finding the gaps between the racks, and under about four pairs of hands the back of a board off the wall of a hut, a pencil stub about two fingers long, a spade with the handle worn shiny in one place, and a barrow whose wheel has come off twice.** The man of about fifty-two who digs is in the third row and is not digging because the ground is easy, and the rod is standing on its end in the corner of the hut against the north wall where the wall is thickest, and it stood there and it did not stand there any differently. **The man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living comes out along the head of the row at about the seventh hour with nothing in his hands, does not go down the row, stops at the head of the third row about nine feet off the man who is digging, and says his name and then says that nobody is going to be asked anything.** The man works out in about four minutes that he is being told something, goes on putting willow in for about as long as it takes to fill a pail, and asks no question. **THE FINDING IS THAT THERE IS NO FORM.** The only page in four hundred miles with a person's name at the top of it is five lines long with nothing under the first line, it is in a city, **it could have been sent out and nobody sent it, because a page with a name at the top of it is a page and this is not one.** So the man says he is not going to be given one and is not going to be asked for anything, and then he says what he was told — that there is a room on the twenty-fourth, and that there are four days and a date on the room and nothing at the top of it. He then says, out loud on open ground, **that a question in your own hand is worth more than a question in yours and that the reason is nine years old and it is the carrier's and not the asker's, and that he is saying it on a row of racks because nobody in four hundred miles has ever said it in a room where the man it was about was standing nine feet off.** He sits down on the ground at the head of the third row with his coat out flat and his hands in it and writes nine lines on the rough side of a board — not the planed side, because a pencil does not take on planed wood — about as long as nine holes. The carrier will not read it and says there is nobody in four hundred miles who could not read it and him standing there. **It went out on the twentieth at about the fifth hour of the afternoon and the room it was written for is on the twenty-fourth, and that is four days, and the carrier walked eleven miles in the last of the light and did not open it. Change: the fifth and last notice of the volume went out in a hand, unsigned, with nothing at the top of it, written on a board because there was nothing to write on, and nobody was asked whether it would go.**

**Chapter 0336 — A Board, And A Wall Above It.** Day 336, a Monday, the twenty-first. A room over a market with about four people in it and nothing to put on the table, the light lying along a table nobody was sitting at, and under about four pairs of hands a folded sheet of road with a ninth line with nothing on it, a register that is not in the room, a slate, and a length of time with nothing in it. **A room with about four people in it and nothing to put on the table is the ordinary condition of a room the morning after a decision, and the whole of the work a room has on an ordinary day is about four minutes.** Tamsin Quill has the fold showing and a man is at the window who came in with her and has not said a word since the stair. **THE CONSENT FRACTURE IS VISIBLE AND IS NOT MENDED AND NOBODY APOLOGISED IN IT:** she says that he has not asked her for the sheet in nine days and that he did ask on the second day, and that she is not asking him why and would like it noticed in a room with a date on the door, and he says one word, **No**, and the room does not ask either of them a second thing. Then the road up to the store, nine miles, going up the whole way, and at the top a man of about twenty-seven comes out of the door with a pail. **He washes the board and does not wash the wall above it, quickly, the way a man does a thing he has decided not to do.** The board carries the figures of the two hundred and twenty-fourth day of this flood and has not been written on since the day after them, and he starts at the top left corner and goes along the top and comes back along the bottom, and the cloth goes over the figures without pressing on them. Then he says four words into the yard and waits to be asked about them and is not asked. **He gives the reason and the reason is about how a thing looks and not about what is on it:** there is a wall about nine feet off the boards, it is the one people can read from the bottom of the yard and not the top, and about nine feet is a height a man can wash standing on a board and not standing on a ladder, and **he has washed four walls since the water and the bottom half of about nine of them, and he is not going to be the man who makes a wall look like something is being done to it.** When Tamsin Quill says she will tell him about the wall and not what is on it, he says he is not going to start on a Monday on the top of a road, and that there is no date on anything in the yard for him to put it on anyway and he has decided that is the correct arrangement. **She does not ask him.** The queue is about nine men deep and has been running since before the days went back without the man who started it in it. Nothing in that yard is touched: a shut chest, a beam on trestles, nine feet of boards with a sack on them, a pan with a coat over it, two brooms on their boards. **Change: a man has said out loud in the open air on a road nine miles up a hill why he will not wash a wall, the board is not written on and the wall above it is not washed, and a woman who has asked nine doors a question each in three days stood in a yard and did not ask, and the not-asking was hers and lasted about nine seconds and he has not noticed it and there was no date in that yard for either of them to put it on.**

**Chapter 0337 — Eight Doors In A Row, And Not One Of Them Can Show Him The Edge.** Day 337, a Tuesday, the twenty-second. A gap for a cart between two houses out past the last named house, the light coming over eight roofs in one piece and not into the gap until about the fourth hour, and under about four pairs of hands a post with a nail in it about four feet up, a length of new rope nobody had used, a folded paper with a sum on it and a name at the top of it, and a bag with a chalk and a cloth in it. **The man of about fifty-seven has been out since before the light, nine miles, to a place where nine households had already given their days back about forty days ago, and he did not go to the lane past the two gates and he did not go up the road past this city.** The notice from the twelfth is still on the post. **He asked at four of the eight doors where their ground stops, and he stood back off the frame before he did it at every one of them, and nobody knew: one of them said a name, and the name was the name of a piece of ground and not an edge.** The man of about thirty-one comes up the row with nothing in his hands and stops in the gap, and the man tells him the finding before anybody has asked him for it, and says he would rather do it there where it happened. Then a room over a market at about the fourth hour, and **he puts the receipt on the table — the only sheet in four hundred miles with his name at the top of it, a receipt for money out of a house four hundred miles off, and it does not say where the ground is — and says he is not going to be told he is wrong, and wants that said before anything else, because the thing he has just said does not go in a column, it goes in a gap for a cart, and there is no form in four hundred miles for a gap for a cart.** The man of about forty-three has the deed out and reads the clerk's hand out loud for about nine lines twice, and the man who has just come in from a gap for a cart does not hear a word of it. **THE FINDING IS THAT A HOUSEHOLD OUT PAST THE LAST NAMED HOUSE CANNOT SAY WHERE ITS OWN GROUND STOPS, and that nine households on a lane past two gates gave their days back about forty days ago and did not say where their ground was either, and that the two facts are a road apart and have not been put together by anybody and are not going to be, and that the man who said it does not know the second one happened.** Change: a man who paid for half a holding has come back from four doors with the answer to a question nobody put to him and there is nowhere to put it, and the money is in his hand and he is not leaving.

**Chapter 0338 — A Second Bar, And A Boy With Two Pails.** Day 338, a Wednesday, the twenty-third. **Four streets come together at a gate where the ground has been worn down about two inches in about a hundred years, the light comes up the middle of the four of them at about the seventh hour and stops about nine feet short, the wind comes up the fourth street through the gap and does not stop, and what is under about four pairs of hands is a bar against a post, a barrow with a spade across its handles, a wooden yoke with a strap and two pails, and a second bar cut at the near end in the dark by somebody who had a light somewhere else in the yard.** **THE NEAR SILL HAS GONE SOFT ON THE LOW SIDE AND THE BAR HAS BEGUN TO LIFT OUT OF ITS DROP, and that is the whole of the job**: a drop that will not take the bar is a hole that is not a hole yet, and a week later it is a gate that does not shut, and nobody four hundred miles off will ever be told about it. He has the ground out of it before light. The boy of about eleven comes through at the seventh hour and does not stop, and a gatekeeper who is not asking the boy anything has been getting the whole of the water news of four streets out of not saying anything for about nineteen days. **The man of about thirty-six has been saying the day out loud at that gate every day since the fifth day of the Bare Month, which makes this the nineteenth time and not the twentieth, and he says it to the boy's back — the twenty-third day of the Bare Month — and the boy turns round and says it back and then says his mother has not had one since before the water and does it out loud at the door, and that it is four doors now, and that a man with a handcart has heard her and does it at the top of his own road and has not told her.** The man says it is right, and then tells him to say it at the doors and not at the gate, because a gate is a thing you are stood at and a door is a thing you go to, **and that it took nineteen days and a boy with two pails to work out which of the two travels.** He puts the bar back and it drops, and then takes the second bar out of the yard and sets it in the bracket on the far side, **because the fourth street has begun to run across the worn ground where it used to stop, and a second bar is not a repair and is a doubling, and nobody sent for it.** Then a woman of about twenty-eight comes and stands at it, and neither of them says one word, and she goes through, and she says from nine paces into the fourth street and over her shoulder that nobody is going to ask him why and that she has been to about nine doors this week and has not written a name at the top of anything and has now said so out loud in a gate rather than in a room, which is the reason she said it there. **The chapter's finding about the gate itself: this gate is a bar and a keeper and a set of days, and that is the whole of what anybody in four hundred miles can say about it, and there is a different word for that man on a page four hundred miles off, and the two words have never been in one room and are not going to be, and nobody on this road has ever been asked what the difference between them is worth, and the man at the gate has said out loud that he is not going to define either of them and would rather the oak had the other one.** Change: a gate nobody sent for has two bars on it, and a boy who says the date has told a man something the man did not know, and the whole of it is on no page in four hundred miles and cannot be sent for.

**Chapter 0339 — He Will Look At It From The End Of It.** Day 339, a Thursday, the twenty-fourth. A room over a shop on the low ground, about nine people, the light across a sill with a bread board at the end of it, and under about four pairs of hands a case that was shut, a copy of an entry in a clerk's hand a hundred and forty years old pinned to the corner of a deed, a box with nine of nothing in it, and nine paces of floor. **The man of about fifty-two who digs comes in at about the seventh hour and sits down, having put his hands flat on the table and taken them off again inside four seconds.** Nobody asked him anything, in the first four minutes or at all. **Then he says yes, and does not give a day, and nobody asks him for one.** Then he gives the condition: he will look at it from the end of it and will not put his hands on it, and he said it before anybody asked him for it, and he will not add to it or take any off. **THE REASON IS A TRADE AND NOT A MODESTY AND IT IS ON A PAGE:** a man whose whole life is putting things in holes is a man people hand holes to, and a man people hand holes to is not asked what is in the ground, he is asked to make a place for something; and a mark in the ground that was not made by anybody, about a foot wide and about two inches deep, from the near end of a row of trees to a stone nobody put there, is not a hole and is not going to be one, and the trade has nothing to say about it. He says he can tell whether ground is hard at a place from a yard off without a spade and cannot tell anything about the top of a thing he has not stood at the end of, and that he would not want to explain that twice. **HE HAS A ROD AND HE OFFERS THE FACT OF IT UNASKED AND DOES NOT BRING IT, and nobody in that room asked him about the rod.** The man of about forty-three has the deed and the sheet out and the book shut, and says why: the name is on a page, it was read out loud once in daylight in about four seconds, a person wrote it down in their own head and did not put it on anything, **and that is the correct way for a name to be in a book, and he is not going to open it again for about nine people.** Adrian reads the clerk's hand from the top down, both columns, puts a finger a finger's width above the last line and does not touch it, and says one thing and stops: that nobody in four hundred miles has ever agreed a thing with a person who was going to be asked about it afterwards. **THE FINDING IS THAT THE ONLY INSTRUMENT IN FOUR HUNDRED MILES ANYBODY CAN THINK OF FOR A MARK IN THE GROUND IS A MAN STANDING AT THE END OF IT WITH HIS HANDS BEHIND HIS BACK, and that nobody in the room will be able to check any of it, and that every person in the room has known that since the seventh hour and has gone on with the morning anyway.** **The line in the ground was not measured, dug, counted, probed or resolved, nobody went and looked at it, no rod went into anything, and no third hole was made.** Change: the only man anybody could think of sending says yes, with a condition that is his, and the room accepts it in about four seconds, and what the city has got is a man who will look at a thing from the end of it and report nothing that can be checked.

**Chapter 0340 — Four Seconds, And A Name Off His Own Board.** Day 340, a Friday, the twenty-fifth. **The room at the back of the Open Hand has no key and no person owns it, the right of refusal that cannot be bought out is in about nine rooms in this city every day, about nine people were in it, and under about four pairs of hands is a board, a handcart nobody had brought in past the step, a length of rope, and a man's own hand.** A man who trades on the board carries it face up, and about four people have worked out since that a man who has carried a board for about four years has a habit about the underside of it. He begins about a place at the top of a road. **Then the wall says one thing, once, and nobody in the room can be told where it came from:**

> A word may be put to a thing in a room in daylight with a date on it,
> and a word may be taken back the same way,
> and a person may not be taken back at all.

**It is refused before a sentence was finished by a man who trades on the Open Hand board, who has refused a thing four times in four years and once every one of those times a woman who is on nothing was the one who did the refusing, and the fifth time it is his own board that is in the room.** Nobody argued, and the room in this city had spent about a fortnight learning not to argue. **AND THE REFUSAL IS WHAT DOES THE REST OF IT.** The rule is about words, and it says a person cannot come off at all, and he says: **I have put a person on a board and taken them off it, and I did it in my own hand, and nobody in four hundred miles knows that, and I have had it since about the ninth year of this flood.** He tells it in the order he has it in his head, because he has been rehearsing it since the ninth year of this flood. **THE TWO DATES ARE SETTLED AND THEY ARE NOT THE SAME PERIOD: a woman who is on nothing and is named on nothing asked to be added by name at the back of a market about a hundred days into this flood and he refused her in nineteen words before she had finished the sentence, because the board has nineteen rates on it and no names — that is Chapter 0113 and Chapter 0123 and it is a page — and about as long afterwards, in about the ninth year of this flood, a man asked to have his own name put on the board and it was on about nine days;** **and the man whose name was on the board came to her afterwards and told her the board had his name on it and hers was not going to go on, and he was not a fool about it, and he worked out in about as long as it takes to read a line that a board which has refused a person can be shown to have accepted somebody, and he was right, and the man does not think he knew what he was doing.** Then he took it off the next morning with his own cloth. **THE COST IS NOT THE BOARD AND NOT THE CLOTH AND NOT THE NINE DAYS: the cost is that he has said it out loud, in daylight, in a room that cannot be turned out, to about nine people, and that a person was put on a board in this city because of what he did at the back of a market and cannot be put back, and that about four hundred people in this city can see the place on the board where it was and not one of them will ever be told, and that knowing it does not put anybody back.** He says the rule is the only thing anybody has said out loud in this flood that he has not got a way round, and that he has been getting a way round words for nine years, and that this one has a person in it. The man of about sixty with a handcart and a length of rope, who was told no at a gate and said he would come back and has come back, asks at about the fourth hour of the afternoon **whether a person can be put back**, and nobody in that room answered him. **Change: the volume's one panel is on a page, is refused out loud in about four seconds, and the refusal is what makes the man who refused it say out loud the one thing in four hundred miles nobody has ever been told, and nobody in the room can answer the question it raises.**

---

**Chapter 0341 — A Date On The Post, And A Lane Nobody Goes Up.** Day 341, a Saturday, the twenty-sixth. **A gate on a lane past two gates, the twenty-sixth in chalk on the post with the twenty-fifth above it and nothing rubbed out, a bar of ash in two brackets, a wooden hook over its handle, a brass key on a tarred cord in an apron pocket, a piece of chalk, and a barrow of boards against the wall since about the forty-first day of the Short Month counting the day they arrived, with the arrangement for taking them up for the forty-ninth, which was a Thursday.** **Nobody is asked anything, and the not-asking has a cost, and the cost is not hers.** The man of about thirty-eight comes up the lane at about the ninth hour, goes past the gate, puts the barrow down about nine hundred paces off it, stands with his forearms on the handles, looks at one side of the road, does not go in, and comes back down. **In a room over a shop on the low ground in the afternoon he is not asked anything and says the whole of what he did because nobody is going to ask him for it, and he does not say what he stood there for.** The man of about fifty-seven asks the room who is going up his lane, the room does not have it, and there is no form in four hundred miles for a question about a lane.

**Chapter 0342 — Four Sentences, And A Cloth.** Day 342, a Sunday, the twenty-seventh. **THE CLIMAX.** A room over a market, the twenty-seventh day of that month in chalk on the door, about nine people, the second slate on the table, a cloth, a piece of chalk in the inside breast pocket of a man who keeps a stall, a deed with a sheet pinned to the corner of it, and a length of new rope nobody has used. **The word comes off the second slate in chalk with a cloth, by the man who said it, and the chalk he carried for thirty-two days is not used, because a slate is the one surface in four hundred miles a word can be taken off.** He is on no roster in this city and has asked to be on none of them. The register-keeper says out loud that she can do this in a room in daylight with a date on the door and that is the whole of what she can do, and that the third thing cannot be done at all. **His reason is four sentences and it is his: a name is only a removal when the thing named can be found; a line in an orchard cannot be found because nobody is going to it; so a line can be named safely; the moment somebody starts going to it the name becomes a description, and the name has to go.** Three of the four people who came to this city behind a question are standing in the room and the fourth is not coming, and the man of about twenty-five has nine words in his coat and does not say them, and the protagonist is in the room and is not asked one question about it. Nobody was thanked. Nobody was asked whether it will go. **And it is worse: the household that owns that orchard has gone back to having nothing and the man of about fifty-seven is no better off and nobody in that room is wrong.**

**Chapter 0343 — The Table Afterwards, And A Rope Nobody Has Used.** Day 343, a Monday, the twenty-eighth. **Cost and consequence, and the chapter says it is worse and not better.** A yard on the half a man of about fifty-seven bought about forty years ago, a length of new rope nobody has used, a gate latch, a barrow, a receipt with a name at the top of it. **The naming bought that field one afternoon and it cost that field thirty-two days, and he says the arithmetic out loud in a yard and nobody tells him it is a sad one, and a man who carries things for a living says the one sentence that made the afternoon worse and says it to the room and not to him.** In the room over a market in the afternoon the slate is on its shelf with the fifty-fifth day of the Short Month on it and nothing under it, the man of about forty-three has the deed and the sheet out for the third time in about a fortnight and is wrong about why and nobody tells him, and the man of about fifty-seven asks for nothing and is given nothing, and the digger agreed to come and gave no day, and the room finds out on the morning of it.

**Chapter 0344 — A Barrow Against The End Of A Cut.** Day 344, a Tuesday, the twenty-ninth. **Action and physical work, and the finding is a sentence that is not an answer.** Eleven miles of walking, a barrow with nothing in it, a cut in that field that is about a boot wide and about nine inches deep and stops at the stone because the man who cut it ran out of ground, and a mark that is not the cut and does not go near it. **The man of about fifty-two puts the barrow against the end of the cut, stands at the stone, looks along the mark the whole way, and does not touch it, and does not put a foot in it and does not stand over it and comes back four paces at a time.** He says one sentence, to the man with the handcart and not to the field: he cannot tell you one thing about it that he did not know at the seventh hour, and he would like that said out loud before anybody asks him what he saw. The man of about sixty does not ask him for a second sentence. A woman comes out of the second house with a bucket and the man of about sixty will not ask either of the two of them anything, and the man of about thirty-one comes up the road with nothing in his hands and stands at the gate. **Nothing was measured, nothing was dug, nothing was counted, nobody put a rod in anything.**

**Chapter 0345 — A Second Bar, And A Line Nobody Can Administer.** Day 345, a Wednesday, the thirtieth. **Political or procedural.** Four streets come together at a gate, the light stops about nine feet short of the bar, the wind is up the fourth street, and the near sill has gone soft on the low side again. **The man of about thirty-six empties a bootful before he answers, and the bootful goes out before the hard answer and not after.** A second bar in a bracket of his own making, put in by himself on a Wednesday night, and a gate with two bars is one gate and goes on a roll as one gate and the second one is on no roll at all. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall comes up the fourth street with a barrow of pears and asks how a gate with two bars goes on a roll, and **nobody said what it was worth, and the man of about thirty-six said he knows and is not going to say it, and the man of about thirty-four said the same and asked in advance not to be thanked and was not thanked.** A woman of about thirty-three who keeps a room asks him about the wall, and **he refused again, and there is no page in four hundred miles on which anybody keeps a count of the number of times, and the ninth line is not read aloud, not moved, not dated, not taken down, not thanked for and not resolved.**

**Chapter 0346 — A Line In A Register, And Nothing Under The Day.** Day 346, a Thursday, the thirty-first. **THE RESOLUTION.** A room over a market, the thirty-first in chalk on the door, about nine people, a register open at a ruled line, a tin carried a mile in both hands, and a sheet of paper thirty years old lying face up on the table where it has lain since the fifth of that month. **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register rules a line and writes the thirty-first day of that month at the top and, underneath it, that a straight mark in a field may not be dug, with no name on it and no word for the mark and the third column left empty on purpose, and says out loud that she will leave the room rather than fill it.** The man of about fifty-seven is told, in the room, what he has, and says the cost out loud, and nobody tells him he was wrong. **The woman of about sixty-nine's sheet is not entered, not copied and does not go out of the room.** Adrian reads the entry from the top down and reads the empty column as well and does not touch it. **And the woman of about sixty-nine asks the question this volume hands on, once, in a mouth, and nobody answers it: what is a person for, on a page, when the only thing anybody needs them for is a journey.** Nobody was asked whether any of it would go.

**Chapter 0347 — The Boards Come Off The Wall.** Day 347, a Friday, the thirty-second. **Action and physical work.** The light off the west that does not get to the boards, the wind round the corner of the gate, the wet in the bottom two of them, about nine boards on end, a bar of ash in two brackets, a hook, a barrow with a rope hooked back over the handle. **The thirty-second goes on the post and nothing is rubbed out, and a column of dates with nothing under it is the same shape as the entry that went into a book on Thursday, and neither of the two is a record.** The man of about thirty-eight comes up the lane for the first time since the thirty-ninth day of the Short Month, stops at the boards, and takes them off one at a time in the wrong order and loses a quarter of an hour. **He asks her nothing; she tells him nothing; and neither of them offers anything and neither of them is asked.** The man of about thirty-one comes up at the third hour and asks whether it is going to be said anywhere, and the answer is that there is no form for it and the only two things anybody would put on a page are a day and a number of boards, and there is a day on the post in chalk about a foot off. **Nobody made a decision, and the wall is bare, and a wall with a shape on it is not a record and a wall without one is not a record either.**

**Chapter 0348 — Four Feet Off, And A Road Nobody Opened.** Day 348, a Saturday, the thirty-third. **Action and political, and it is the volume's last political act.** A handcart, a coil of rope, a hazel mallet with its head bound in cord, sixteen posts in the cart with their tops out, the light behind the work all morning and the wind up the shut road in pushes. **A man who was told no about a fence across the top of that road has come back four times and has spent a day cutting posts for a fence nobody asked for, and the asking was never the point.** He sets out from the row of trees, because a row of trees is a thing you can see from the road, and he does not measure anything, and **four feet is a number he has in his mouth and not a number he took off anything.** Nobody stops him and nobody is sent. The man of about fifty-seven, who could stop it, does not move out of the way and then moves, and says that he could stop it, and is told that is not the same as being able to say where it is. **About four people have worked out that a fence is the most ordinary thing in the world and the most permanent, and that it is going to be there when neither of the two men who never agreed where the line was is, and that the fence does not care.** The nineteen stones are not this fence.

**Chapter 0349 — A Fold, And A Room With Nothing On The Table.** Day 349, a Sunday, the thirty-fourth. **Recovery.** A room over a shop on the low ground with about four people in it and nothing to put on the table, a window painted shut and scraped, and **Tamsin Quill with her folded sheet of road with about nine places on it, folded the same way every time, and a ninth line with nothing on it.** The consent fracture is visible and is not mended and nobody apologised in it: he says he is not going to ask her for the sheet, and she says she has noticed every day for nineteen days and is not asking him why, and he says he is not going to put it in a room because a room has a date on the door and everything that goes in it is on a page by the fourth hour. **A woman of about twenty-one comes in at about the ninth hour and says out loud that the not-waiting is not a mood and that nobody in the room is going to change it by saying something.** The woman of about twenty-one and the man of about twenty-five have the ninth line of the sheet between them and neither put it there. Three of the four are in the room and the fourth is not coming and nobody is going to say what she said on the fourteenth. **The road north of the Marches is shut and does not open and nobody shut it.** The nine words in a coat are not said.

**Chapter 0350 — Thirteen Posts, And A Monday.** Day 350, a Monday, the thirty-fifth. **THE LAST IMAGE, and it is a fence.** The light in front of the work again, the wind that has been up the shut road since before the water and has never been written down, seven willow posts in a cart, eleven withies cut on the Saturday. **He came up at about the sixth hour and nobody sent him and nobody was waiting for him, and coming back early is not a thing anybody arranged.** Thirteen posts in the ground and three to go, and a man who put nine in the ground on the Saturday and went home with seven in a cart came back on the Monday morning to put four more in. **The man of about fifty-seven says that neither of them ever agreed where it was, and the man with the handcart says the same, and the two of them said it the same way and neither had heard the other say it on the Saturday.** He will not put it in a room. The man of about thirty-one has come up this road four times with nothing in his hands and has not opened one of the five sheets he has carried in this flood. **The wall of the shut gate has nine boards off it and a dry strip of stone along the bottom, and the bar of ash is in its brackets and the thirty-fifth is on the post, and a woman of about fifty has not been asked anything about the boards or the fence and is not going to be.** A man of about fifty-seven put his hand flat on the second post the way a man does to a gate post and did not press on it. **The fence does not care where the mark was, and that is what a fence is for.**

## Volume 08, Batch 0002 — Chapters 0361 to 0370, days 361 to 370

**Ten chapters on disk, days 361 to 370, one chapter to one day, no double day, the chapter number is the day number. The run was recomputed from day 1 being a Tuesday: 361 Friday, 362 Saturday, 363 Sunday, 364 Monday, 365 Tuesday, 366 Wednesday, 367 Thursday, 368 Friday, 369 Saturday, 370 Sunday, and the Bare Month days the forty-sixth to the fifty-fifth, the ordinal being the day less 315. The full record of the ten is `state/batch-summaries/volume-08-batch-0002.md` and what they put on a page is at `bible/power-system.md` §47. These ten entries were written by the second review repair pass, which found that the rolling file in this state layer had stopped at Chapter 0350 and that neither Volume 08 batch had continued it; the entries for Chapters 0351 to 0360 are still missing and are carried as a debt in the same place.**

**Chapter 0361 — A Hurdle, A Bar, And A Man Nobody Sent For.** Day 361, a Friday, the forty-sixth. **The man of about fifty-nine is on a page for the first time in this manuscript.** A road four hundred miles long out past the last named house, the light up the low ground, the wind in the same pushes since before the water, a barrow, four rods in the bottom of it, a hurdle of hazel rods, and a gate with a bar in it that has rotted through. **He put a bar in nine years back and hung a hurdle on it, and he has walked that road four times in nine years and has never once seen anybody else on it.** The man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living comes out past the last named house with nothing in his hands and does not stop, and this is the fourth time up that road, and the barrow man has noticed him four times in nine years and has never asked him why. **The carrier is put on a page here for the first time in this volume and it is established that he has carried nine things in this flood and opened none of them, and that he has not asked one question in four times up this road.** Change: a second handle is invented on a page — a hurdle he made and a bar he replaced — and the volume's last image has a man attached to it.

**Chapter 0362 — A Notice, Four Days, And A Number Said By Somebody Else.** Day 362, a Saturday. **A notice goes out for a room on the fifty-first day of that month, four days, unsigned, with nothing at the top of it, to a place and not to a house.** The register-keeper writes it. The stall-keeper of about thirty-four is in the room, and the woman of about fifty-two is not in it. **The figure the man of about thirty-eight has in his head is said out loud by the man of about thirty-nine in a room and by nobody who has been asked, and the chapter puts a day on a thing that has had no day on it in nine years of mornings.** Change: a notice is on a page with both its morning and its room counted.

**Chapter 0363 — Three Of The Four, And A Question About A Column.** Day 363, a Sunday, the forty-eighth. **Three of the four who have not left are in one room over a market with a date in chalk on the door and about nine people in it.** The man of about forty-eight sits down the moment he comes in, and a knife with a hinge that does not spring is on his knee. A woman of about twenty-one is asked which of the four places under a heading is a person and answers **none of the four, the second one counts them**, in one breath, and **nobody in that room marks the moment.** The man of about thirty-nine is against the wall at the back with his slate flat on his knee and says nothing at the point where a room runs out of forms; the man of about fifty-seven has a length of new rope nobody has used over the back of his chair. Change: the fourth place is refused in a room and the refusal costs nothing and is not recorded.

**Chapter 0364 — Two Men In Front Of It, And No Agreement Anywhere In It.** Day 364, a Monday, the forty-ninth. A shut road above the city, a fence along the top of it, the light stopped at the fence. **The man of about fifty-seven comes up on his own at about the sixth hour, nobody sent him, and counts the fence by touching every post, and says the one sentence he came up the road to say: it is the only thing anybody has put up in this flood that is going to be here after everybody who made it.** The man with the handcart does not take the mallet out and does not say whether he agrees. **Sixteen posts, nine in the ground on the thirty-third day of that month with seven left in the cart, four more on the thirty-fifth, and the last three on the thirty-eighth, eleven withies bound with the same cord, and the counts are said out loud on both sides of the road and the two men never agree about where the ground is.** Change: a man is right out loud about a thing he cannot move and nobody asks him why he is right.

**Chapter 0365 — A Wall With A Shape On It, And A Gate With Two Bars.** Day 365, a Tuesday, the fiftieth. A shut gate at the end of a lane and a wall beside it. **Nine boards came off that wall on a barrow on the thirty-second day of that month and about four inches of grey stone is at the foot of it and the shape is going.** The stall-keeper of about thirty-four comes up with an empty barrow to look at it and does not write on it, and **his piece of chalk has been in his own inside breast pocket since the forty-third day of the Short Month and has not been out once in all that time.** The woman at the gate, who has not been asked anything by anybody since before the water, gives the whole of a roll in four sentences to nine feet of stone and says out loud that she said it to a wall and not to the woman he is. **A second gate four streets off has two bars in it and the second is on no roll at all, and the two gates are not put side by side by either of them.** Change: a refusal and a second refusal, and a thing that cannot go on a page.

**Chapter 0366 — A Task, A Condition Stated First, And A Notice Four Days.** Day 366, a Wednesday, the fifty-first. **A notice goes out for a room on the fifty-fifth day of that month, four days, unsigned, with nothing at the top of it.** **A man of about fifty-two who digs is asked a task and says yes with the condition before anybody puts one on him — he will walk it from the near end to the stone and back and say whether a man at the other end would meet him, and he will not put a hand on it, and he will not put a rod in it, and he will not leave a mark of any kind on it, not a stake and not a heap of stone.** The room accepts it in about four seconds. **He does not offer a day and he is not asked for one, and no rod goes into anything, and nobody asked him whether any of it will go.** Change: the digger's condition is a condition about being findable, and it is on a page in his own mouth.

**Chapter 0367 — A Nib Ground Narrow, And Two People Not Saying So.** Day 367, a Thursday, the fifty-second. A room over a market and a window on the near side that has not shut properly since before the water. **The man of about forty-eight took the catch off that window about a week ago and worked at it and put it back, and about nine people know it and about nine of them are in this room and not one has mentioned it.** He is against the wall with the knife on his knee, opening it and shutting it so the joint takes. A woman of about twenty-one's pen catches in the grain and she asks him two words and **he admits in one sentence that he ground the first inch and a half of it on a step outside a room in about a quarter of an hour**, and says he was not saying it because he had been asked, and she does not thank him. **The man of about thirty-eight says in his own mouth that he has been in about two hundred rooms and has not been asked anything in about nine of them and has never once repeated a thing he heard in one, and that this is a trade and not a favour.** Change: a second handle invented on a page and named out loud once by its owner.

**Chapter 0368 — Three Barrows, And A Figure Nobody Answers.** Day 368, a Friday, the fifty-third. A salt wharf, the only flat thing for about four streets, a wind that does not move the light all morning. **The salt has been coming off a barrow every morning of this flood and going up onto boards at the top end and the stack is higher than a man and there is a strip of clear board left at the bottom, and nothing has come off these boards in a cart since before the water.** Three barrows are on the boards and one belongs to anybody. **The man of about thirty-eight says a figure out loud with both anchors in one sentence — a share with the post taken out has been in the inside of a coat in this city since the thirteenth day of the Long Month, which is the hundred and sixty-eighth day of this flood, and this is the fifty-third day of the Bare Month, and that is exactly two hundred days — about a man in this city who is not on those boards, and he does not say how he knows and nobody asks him.** The man of about thirty-nine puts his board of nineteen rates down flat on a wet board, which nobody in this city has ever seen him do, and says a figure out loud and the wind takes about a third of it. Change: the round figure of this batch lands, and it is about somebody else's coat, and the coat is not opened.

**Chapter 0369 — Four Hundred And Forty, And Nowhere For It To Go.** Day 369, a Saturday, the fifty-fourth. A room over a market with about nine people in it and a form on the table. **The man of about thirty-one comes up the nine steps, sits down at the end of the bench for the first time since the fortieth day of that month, and volunteers the one thing he has seen and says he is not going to be asked where he got it from: nothing has come off those boards in a cart since before the water, the stack is higher than a man, and a man with a tally-board has been cutting it off a barrow every morning of this flood and has not said.** The stall-keeper says **about four hundred and forty** of them, on eleven miles of flats the other way, and the woman who says the same number every morning at the head of a stall is not in this room and is not going to be told. **The man of about thirty-nine says the number out loud from the wall at the back — four hundred and forty people make it, and not one of them has ever been asked anything by anybody in this flood — and then says the other thing: there are nine households behind a wall on the low ground and they were asked on the forty-first day of that month, and they are not one of the four hundred and forty.** **The register-keeper says nothing at all for about two hours and puts none of it on a page, which is the fourth time inside a fortnight.** Change: the largest number anybody in this city can name is said out loud by two men on the same day and nothing is sent.

**Chapter 0370 — A Room Doing The Half Of A Task That Is Not Its Own.** Day 370, a Sunday, the fifty-fifth. **The task is the digger's and the day is the room's, and he is not in the room.** A room over a market with about nine people in it from the seventh hour to the ninth hour of the evening. **The man of about fifty-nine comes up the nine steps and has never been in a room in this city in his life**, and says the whole of why he came up them in four sentences without being asked one question, and it is a thing about himself and not about this city. **He is asked about the man, and the question is not whether it would go, and about nine people agree in about four seconds not to send a man eleven miles on a Sunday, and the digger does not know that and has not asked to be told.** The register-keeper writes the day on the slate in chalk at about the ninth hour of the evening, in front of about nine people, and puts nothing under it, **and that is the fourth time inside about a month.** Change: a room does the half of a task that is not its own, produces nothing, and a man who has never been asked anything tells nine people not to go and look for the one person who has not come. **Nobody asked anybody whether anything was going to go, and there is a sheet of paper in this city with nothing at the top of it that has not been written yet.**

## Volume 08, Batch 0003 — Chapters 0371 to 0380, days 371 to 380

**Ten chapters on disk, days 371 to 380, one chapter to one day, no double day, the chapter number is the day number. The run was recomputed from day 1 being a Tuesday: 371 Monday, 372 Tuesday, 373 Wednesday, 374 Thursday, 375 Friday, 376 Saturday, 377 Sunday, 378 Monday, 379 Tuesday, 380 Wednesday, and the Bare Month days the fifty-sixth to the sixty-fifth, the ordinal being the day less 315. The full record of the ten is `state/batch-summaries/volume-08-batch-0003.md` and what they put on a page is at `bible/power-system.md` §48. The month's name is printed once or twice in every one of these ten chapters, which is the legibility instruction this batch was given and the one thing its two predecessors did not do.**

**Chapter 0371 — A Sheet With A Day On It And Nothing At The Top Of It.** Day 371, a Monday, the fifty-sixth. **The sixth notice of the volume goes out, four days, for the room on the sixtieth day of that month, unsigned, with nothing at the top of it, to a place and not to a house.** The register-keeper rules the sheet into two by hand because there is no form with a room on it, and writes a day and a place and nothing else, and says out loud that the reason there is no name is that if you come to that room on that day then afterward there is nothing whatever in four hundred miles that can say you were there. **Both the morning it went out and the day it is for are in one sentence with the count, and the count is four.** The carrier takes it down nine steps and says he has never opened one of these he has carried. **Adrian Vale takes a sheet folded into four out of the inside of his own coat — a date on the back, two names on the front — reads it from the top down, does not turn it over, puts it back and buttons the coat; neither that fact nor the notice was said out loud by anybody, and the two were nine feet apart on the same table.** The man of about thirty-four has his hand flat on his own inside breast pocket; the man of about thirty-nine is against the wall and says nothing at all for the whole morning. Change: a sheet with nothing at the top of it and a page with two names on the front of it were on one table in one room, and nobody connected them. **The prompt this batch inherited called this notice the seventh of the seven; it is the sixth, and the seventh goes out on day 389.**

**Chapter 0372 — The One He Has Never Filled In.** Day 372, a Tuesday, the fifty-seventh. A room, about nine people, a form on the table with three of its four places in a condition and one in a decision. **The man of about thirty-eight comes up nine steps at about the eighth hour, sits at the end of the bench, and is asked nothing.** The man of about thirty-four says to the table without turning round that a man has not filled the last place in — a statement, not a question. **The answer, in his own mouth: he has filled the first, second and third four times a year since the winter before the water out of what he is given, and has never once filled the fourth, and nobody has ever sent him anywhere to find out how, and he has assumed since the winter before the water that it is a share of something and that a share has to come out of a household, and he has never gone to a household.** He says he is not going to and is not going to be asked. The register-keeper says nothing. The woman of about twenty-one is at a bench with the pen in her apron. The man of about thirty-nine says nothing. **The fourth of the four is not in this city and is not going to be asked about anything by anybody, and a party of four is four, and no days were counted.** Change: the fourth place of the return has a new reason for being empty and the reason is a man and not a household, and about nine people declined to send him.

**Chapter 0373 — Nine Places On A Sheet, And A Crease That Has Gone Soft.** Day 373, a Wednesday, the fifty-eighth. A market that is not a market, about nine stalls down, and **Tamsin Quill sitting on the bottom step of nine steps with the folded sheet of road in her apron.** She takes it out, unfolds it on her knees in the open — one sheet, about nine places, folded in four with the same four creases — and folds it back the same way. **The man of about thirty-four comes past, stops about nine feet off, looks at it, and says that he is not going to ask what it is or how many places are on it, that he counted them from where he was standing, and that he has counted them and has not asked her one thing.** She does not hand it to him. **Then she says out loud that the fourth crease has gone soft, that this is the whole of what has changed on it, and that a crease is a thing about her hands and not about the paper, and that she is the only person in four hundred miles who can tell you which of those two it is.** Change: a page that had not been unfolded in about a month was unfolded once in the open in front of nine people, and nobody asked for it, and one thing about it has changed and it is hers.

**Chapter 0374 — A Room Deciding Not To Say What It Is For.** Day 374, a Thursday, the fifty-ninth, the day before. A room, about nine people, and the whole of the day's work is arrangements: who is on the stair at the seventh hour and who at the eighth, whether the bench is clear or the form is on it, whether the door has a day on it in chalk or a lump of chalk on the sill. The woman of about twenty-one is in the room all day and does not ask one question about any of it. **The man of about thirty-four says out loud, to the bench, that the room is not going to say what it is for, and why: a room which says what it is for has to be right about it, and if it is wrong then the people who came up those steps were sent for the wrong thing, and there is no instrument in four hundred miles that can tell them afterwards that they were not.** The room agrees at once. The man of about thirty-one stands at the end of the bench and says that nobody in this city is going to be told, and names the four people who are coming and the woman at the far end of the bench with a pen she has not taken out, and does not name the other four. **Tamsin Quill is not in that room and has not been told and nobody sent for her.** Change: a room in this city decided out loud, in one breath, not to have a subject, and nobody in four hundred miles was told.

**Chapter 0375 — Whether It Will Go.** Day 375, a Friday, the sixtieth, **the volume's midpoint.** A room over a market, in daylight, with a date in chalk on the door, about nine people. The form on the table is not touched. The man of about thirty-four is at the back with his hand flat on his own inside breast pocket. The man of about thirty-nine is at the back where the light does not reach with his slate flat on his knee and says nothing at the point where a room runs out of forms. The man of about twenty-five has his coat on. The man of about twenty-five has his coat on and nine words of his own in the inside of it that he has not said in three rooms. **The man of about forty-eight and the man of about thirty-four have an exchange about the second slate on the shelf behind the table, which has a date on it and nothing under it and which nobody has picked up in about a month.** **THEN TAMSIN QUILL, AT A BENCH WITH THE SHEET IN HER APRON, ASKS THE WOMAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-ONE, FOUR FEET OFF HER WITH A PEN IN HER APRON, WHETHER IT WILL GO. Two words and a question, said once, in the local language, in daylight, in front of about nine people, and she did not ask it twice.** Nobody asked her anything else. **What she said is not on a page and is not in any file this batch produced.** The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register was not in the room; she came up to the step at the back with no name on it at about the tenth hour, did not come in, and stood there until about the fourth hour, and the two of them were about four paces apart and said nothing to each other. The second slate was on the shelf all day and nobody went near it. Change: **a question that had been available to a room in this city for a month was asked, once, and nobody asked a second one, and nobody after that morning is going to be the second one either.**

**Chapter 0376 — The Order It Is Done In, In A Yard.** Day 376, a Saturday, the sixty-first. **A yard, and the one chapter of this volume that is not a room.** A wall down one side, about nine lines across the other, and the order the nine things are done in is the back first, then along the wall, and the two lines nearest the door last. **Adrian Vale has been in this city for years and has never been in this yard; he stops at a gate, watches about five hours, is asked nothing, and is not asked to.** The man of about fifty-two who digs is eleven miles the other way and nobody in that yard is going to ask him anything. At about the third hour he says the whole order out loud, with his hand going up and down the air. **The woman of about sixty-nine, who has had an empty tin in both hands the whole time and has not opened it, tells him he had it the other way round at the second kettle, that she is not going to tell him which one he had wrong, and that neither is anybody else in that yard, and gives the reason: a man who is told has it right tomorrow and a man who is not told will have it wrong tomorrow and will think about it.** The two lines nearest the door get the last of the light. **He goes back along the lane and does not go up the nine steps, and the chapter says he worked a thing out and does not say he was right.** Change: the order nine things are done in is in one man's head and on no page, and about four people in that yard noticed a thing about one of the nine and are not going to say it.

**Chapter 0377 — A Roof Down By Hand Before The Weather.** Day 377, a Sunday, the sixty-second. A market under sailcloth and sacking on four poles, not a market day, the roof still up. A squall at about the second hour; **four poles out of the sill in about a quarter of an hour, the two ends first because the two ends are where the weight is, and the man who took the near end had a piece of chalk in an inside breast pocket and had both hands on a pole and did not try to get at it.** The man of about thirty-one goes to the far corner and ties a line nobody asked him to. **The woman of about thirty-four says the figure out loud before the man beside her gets to it. He stops. He says that he has got it and she has got it, that he is not going to be thanked, and that he is not going to say he said it first, and that this is so the market does not work it out.** She says it twice more before the light goes, to nobody in particular. Change: the arrangement of about a fortnight is handed back in the open in front of nine people and neither of them says it is over, and a market lost a morning and about nine boards and nothing anybody could put in a column.

**Chapter 0378 — Two Things With Four Places In Each, Not Put Side By Side.** Day 378, a Monday, the sixty-third. A room, about nine people, the return on a bench and the case shut on a table four feet away, both unopened. **The man of about thirty-four puts a hand out and stops about nine inches short of the form and says out loud that he is not going to put the two together, that he has been trying since a Tuesday on a lane, and that he can see he was wrong to try.** He counts eight places aloud: a page with four places under a heading and a docket with four places in it, and not one of the eight the same as any of the other seven; the date on one is not the date on the other; the gate on one is not the gate on the other. **And the third place in that case is a word nobody in this city has said out loud in a room in about nine years, which he has wondered about for a fortnight and has not said and is not going to say, and that is not modesty, that is a woman at a gate telling him the whole of a roll in four sentences to nine feet of stone and refusing to be told where two bars are.** A roll at a gate has a gate on it, a keeper on it and a set of days, and is four streets off and is not in that room and is not going to be brought into it. **The two rolls of the same gates are not put side by side, the case is not opened, the schedule is not filled in and not withdrawn, the word is not said by anybody, and the man of about thirty-nine said nothing for the whole morning.** Change: a room counted eight places across two documents and decided out loud that it is not going to find out, and not finding out is free, and the price of it is the same as everything else here.

**Chapter 0379 — Nine Rows Nobody Can Date.** Day 379, a Tuesday, the sixty-fourth. A room, about nine people, and the register open at the back. It has three columns: the first for what went out, the second for what came back, the third empty on purpose since a Thursday in that month. **The nine rows at the back are in an instrument this city stopped writing in years ago; she can read the hand and every word of what was asked and cannot tell what day any of it happened on, and they have not been looked at since the eighth day of that month, which is fifty-six days back from the sixty-fourth, and the figure is in the sentence that names the day it is measured from.** Nobody copies them. Her reason, in her own mouth: a copy has to be dated to be of any use, and a page that cannot be dated is the only kind of page in this city that no office nine hundred miles off can ask to have produced. The room agrees at once. **The man of about thirty-four had his hand flat on his own inside breast pocket the whole quarter of an hour and took nothing out, and the man of about thirty-nine was about nine feet away with his slate on his knee and said nothing.** Change: a room refused a document on the ground that it could be made to work, which is the first time anybody in four hundred miles has done that, and nothing was entered and no name went under any day.

**Chapter 0380 — Nine Places On A Bench, And About Nine People.** Day 380, a Wednesday, the sixty-fifth. A room over a market, about nine people, and **Tamsin Quill takes the folded sheet of road out of her apron and puts it on the bench and does not hand it round.** She unfolds it in the middle of the room in daylight with the day on the door. **She says out loud that there are about nine places on it and about nine of them in the room, that those are two nines and they are not the same nine, and that she is telling the room so before any of them works it out and starts asking her which nine is which.** Nobody improved on that. **Then she says the sheet is not going to anybody, is not going to be written out on anything, is not going into a form, and is not going to a man who fills in four places four times a year, and that she has been offered a way to give it to somebody since about the time the fence was finished and has not taken it, and the reason is that the moment it is on a page in front of somebody else then the nine places on it are nine places somebody else has.** The room accepts it at once and the sheet goes back into the apron folded in four with the same four creases. The man of about thirty-four sat through it with the chalk in his pocket and was not thanked. **The man of about thirty-nine was at the back and nobody asked him whether a page of that kind should be on a page.** Change: a sheet with about nine places on it was put in front of about nine people and given to none of them, and three of the four who said yes in their own words were in that room, and the fourth is not in this city, and nobody who came down those nine steps counted anything.

## NOTE ON CHAPTERS 0371 to 0380 — REVIEW REPAIR PASS, and it changed no entry above this line

**Every one of the ten entries for Chapters 0371 to 0380 above stands exactly as written.** The prose of those ten files was reworked sentence by sentence on 2026-09-27 to remove a repeated narration frame that restated the sentence immediately before it, and **the set of bolded speech paragraphs in all ten files is byte-identical to the first edition, and the multiset of every digit string across the ten files is unchanged.** So no entry above needed a correction and none was made, which is itself worth recording: **a repair pass that has to correct its own summaries has usually changed something it should not have.** The disposition is `reviews/volume-08/batch-0003.md` and the batch's own half is `state/batch-summaries/volume-08-batch-0003.md` §11. **What a later writer should know and cannot get from the entries above: the observations in those ten chapters are unchanged and are all still on the page, and what was cut was the sentence saying that somebody in the room had worked them out. The chorus frame is still a device of this volume and now stands at thirty-two instances in one hundred and ninety-eight paragraphs, and the rule governing it is at `outline/volume-08.md` §17 item 11a and is a rule about sentences and not about a number.**
