The man in the very good coat says one short thing in his own language and nobody asks him to say it again. **He reads the document on his knee for the best part of an hour, does not look at the page once, does not describe the room, and nobody went for anybody who had the language, and he has not been in a room in this city in which anything got decided and is not going to be.**

**Chapter 0420 — A Woman Of About Fifty-Four Is Asked Where Her Line Is.** Day 420, a Monday, the hundred and fifth. The room over a market, and **the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register asks, standing up, in the local language, in daylight, with a date on the door, where her line is, and she does not ask it twice and she does not put her hand on the paper while she asks it.** A woman of about fifty-four who has been in that room for nine mornings and been asked nothing answers it correctly: **it is where the water stops on her own step, it is at the second board, it is not the same line twice in the same week, and she knows a length by a thread and there is one of them round her wrist for it, and she has never once put that line on a page and did not come up those steps to have it put on one.** Nobody asked her a second question and nobody thanked her. The man of about thirty-four says from the back of the room that the second of the four will take a line, and is thanked by nobody and argued with by nobody. **The register-keeper says out loud, in her own mouth, that she has asked and got an answer and that there is nowhere in four hundred miles to put an answer that somebody gave out loud in a room.** The woman of about fifty-four goes down the nine steps, nobody follows her, and a line that is at the second board of a step in this city is on no page at all.

---

## NOTE ON CHAPTERS 0411 TO 0420 — REVIEW REPAIR PASS, and what it changed, and this block is the authoritative one for these ten

**The ten entries above describe the files as they now stand. A review of Batch 0002 found five things and four of them were repaired on four of these ten pages; the fifth is an upstream gap in `outline/batches/` that cannot be repaired by a batch or by a repair pass and is escalated instead, with the remedy named as one outline phase that writes the three missing card files for Volume 09's second, third and fourth batches.** What changed on the pages, chapter by chapter: **0412** gained a present-tense consequence at the end of it — a length of board cut off a board at the bottom end of the wharf without anybody being told how long, a short exchange about it, and the man of about thirty-nine going up off the wharf with two boards under his arm instead of one — and it also had the house lead formula taken out of its lead, and its own disclosure and refusal are untouched; **0419** gained one clause naming the man who takes the marked turn, and nothing else in it was touched; **0411**, **0413**, **0414** and **0417** each had one time-measurement simile replaced with a physical measure; **0412**, **0414** and **0417** had the house lead formula taken out of the lead; **0414** and **0418** had their codas rewritten so that they no longer close on an inventory of objects. **No day, no weekday, no Bare-Month day, no notice, no form, no object count that holds, no anchor, no cast member and no decision of record moved, and no fact left a page.** Every figure that moved is published with the first edition beside it at `state/batch-summaries/volume-09-batch-0002.md` §13 and in `bible/power-system.md` §53 item 14, and the ten days, the ten weekdays and the ten Bare-Month days are unmoved and were re-measured rather than assumed. **And one gap in this file is published rather than filled: Volume 09's first ten chapters, 0401 to 0410, have no per-chapter block in this file, because Batch 0001 did not write one, and this pass did not write one for it, because those ten chapters are not this pass's; they are described at `state/batch-summaries/volume-09-batch-0001.md` and in the four state files, and a later phase may add them.**

# VOLUME 09, BATCH 0003 — Chapters 0421 to 0430, days 421 to 430 — and this block wins over every block above it

**Ten chapters on disk, days 421 to 430, one chapter to one day, no double day, the chapter number is the day number. The run was recomputed from day 1 being a Tuesday and from nothing else before any prose was written: 421 Tuesday, 422 Wednesday, 423 Thursday, 424 Friday, 425 Saturday, 426 Sunday, 427 Monday, 428 Tuesday, 429 Wednesday, 430 Thursday. The Bare Month days are 106 to 115, the Nth being day less 315. Every figure in this block is measured in `state/batch-summaries/volume-09-batch-0003.md` with the definition beside it, and this block copies no mouth: the words the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says in Chapter 0428 are on that page and in no file in this repository, including this one.**

**Chapter 0421 — There Is No Way In This Flood To Ask Anybody Anything First.** Day 421, a Tuesday, the hundred and sixth day of the Bare Month. The room over a market, the day in chalk on the door, the sheet on the table with a heading and four places ruled under it and a crease pressed down the third of them and nothing in any of the four, a length of new rope over the back of a chair, a lump of chalk on the edge of the table. **A man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board says out loud, to the middle of the room and to nobody in particular, that four of them have been in that room every working morning since the page came up the road and not one of them has gone down nine steps and put one question to one man, and gives the reason: a sheet goes out of this city four times a year and comes back with things in it that were not written on it, and there is no form in four hundred miles that puts a question to a man.** The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall, at the back, without turning round, says the other half of it — that the man at the end of the bench is not being spared and is not being reached, and that those are two different things. Nobody thanked either of them. The register-keeper then names the third of the four places as a person, says in her own mouth that the page asks nobody and that if it had she would not have answered it, and says she has been telling that room every morning since the page was read out that she is not the one who is going to decide, and that it has gone flat, and that she is going to stop saying it on Saturday morning and say the other thing instead. **Nothing in that room changed except that two men said out loud, in front of each other, that the reason nobody asks anybody anything is that there is no instrument for it, and a true thing about why you have not done a thing is not a thing that does it.**

**Chapter 0422 — The Sheet Goes Out Onto The Step At The Back For An Hour.** Day 422, a Wednesday, the hundred and seventh. The room over a market, and about the ninth hour, because the room has gone cold. **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register carries the sheet out in her own two hands and sets it down on the top step, still folded the way it came, and stands beside it with her arms folded and does not go back in.** A woman at the market end of the step with a basket comes the length of it and asks what the third of the four is for, and is told, out loud, in the local language, in daylight, with a date in chalk on the door six feet above her head, that it is a person and that nobody knows what it is for and that the speaker is not going to start on a step in a market, and is told that if she wants it written down she will have to be a person and come up nine steps and ask. **She does not say thank you and nobody says anything to her, and the first person outside that room to be told anything about that page has been told the truth and nothing else.** The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says one thing about it to nobody from the back of the room. **The man of about thirty-nine comes up at about the eleventh hour with his own board under his arm and a piece of bark in his hand, off a stall frame at the market end and having nothing to do with the page, and puts it on the table and leaves it there, and says out loud how many people were standing at the foot of that step and that none of them came up.** Adrian Vale comes up at the twelfth hour and reads a sheet of his own standing up and puts it back inside his coat. **The sheet goes back on the same place on the table in the afternoon and the four places are all still empty.**

**Chapter 0423 — One Withie Off The Fence And Put Back On.** Day 423, a Thursday, the hundred and eighth, on a shut road out of this city. Sixteen willow posts and eleven withies bound along the top of it, and the light comes up out of the low ground and stops at the fence and does not come over it. **One withie comes off in the wet, and the man of about sixty, who has a hazel mallet with its head bound in cord and a coil of rope on top of the coil, unties the binding where it has gone, and puts it back on with a new length of cord off a coil that has been used, and nobody sent him and nobody is going to.** The man of about fifty-seven is on the low side of that fence and is not offered a hand and does not offer one, and says out loud, to nobody, that a room which does not ask him to hold anything and a man doing a thing two feet from his hands who does not ask him are the same arrangement, and that he is not going to decide on a road in the wet which one it is. **Nobody asked him one thing in that chapter, the length of new rope over the back of a chair in a room is not named on that road, and the clear strip of board at the bottom of a stack at a salt wharf is not in it either.**

**Chapter 0424 — What Do You Sell.** Day 424, a Friday, the hundred and ninth. The room over a market, about nine people in it before the light reached the stair, and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall up the nine steps and sat down at the back where he has sat every morning since the page arrived. **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register stands up at about the eighth hour and asks what he sells, in the local language, in daylight, with a date on the door, and asks it once, and he answers without turning round: whatever came in behind him yesterday that he has not sold by the fourth hour, and about a cord of good string, and that the string is the trade.** She asks a second question, also not about the table, about what he does with the rest of the day, and he answers that he washes the board and puts the string up and sits at the back of a room until the light goes off it, and that he has never once in this flood been asked a question about any part of it, and that she has now asked him two and he is not going to make anything out of that in front of anybody. **Nobody thanked him, he has said in a lane a long way back that he is not to be thanked, the register-keeper wrote nothing down because there is nowhere on that table to write down what a man sells, and the length of new rope is still over the back of a chair and has still never been used.**

**Chapter 0425 — The Third Of The Four Places Is Going To Be Left Empty.** Day 425, a Saturday, the hundred and tenth. The room over a market, from the sixth hour, and a second slate on the shelf behind the table with a date on it and nothing under it. **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register stands up at about the ninth hour, does not touch the sheet or the chalk, and says the first thing first: the third of these four places is a person, and a page with a person on it finds them.** She says what leaving it empty costs, and she says it before the decision: a line with nobody's name on it will be easier to move than one with a name on it, and whoever is standing where one of them runs is the one who finds out last, and that is not her and not anybody at that table. Then she decides, in one sentence, that she is leaving the third place empty. **There is no vote and nobody asked for one and nobody told her she was right or that she was wrong and nobody thanked her, and a woman at the far end of the bench asks one fair practical question about how a person puts a line on a page that has not got a person in it, and the man of about thirty-nine, who writes on a board for a living, keeps his hand flat on his own slate and does not answer it and is not asked it again.** The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says the only thing he says that morning, which is that there is nothing for him to do in that room and that he is not the reason there is. **His stall stood shut at the market end for the whole of that Saturday, the second slate was not picked up and not turned over and not written on, the four places stayed as empty as they were when the page came up the road, and nothing was said about that day by anybody.**

**Chapter 0426 — A Stall Out In The Cold With Nobody At It.** Day 426, a Sunday, the hundred and eleventh, in a market at the bottom of this city. **A stall board is up on its string with the ordinary line on it in chalk, a barrow stands at the end of that stall with nothing in it and has for about a month, people come through the market in the first hour and in the second and not one of them stops.** The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall set the board up himself and stands behind it with his hands in his sleeves and does not take his own chalk out of his pocket to write the line, because the line on the board is a different piece of chalk on a string. The man of about twenty-seven comes past at about the ninth hour with a bucket and a rag and no intention of stopping, and stops, and says that Saturday is his morning, and is told that it is his morning and has been since before the water and that there has not been one Saturday morning in this flood when either of them was there and the other was not. **They stop for the length of about a sentence, neither of them puts a bucket down or picks up a piece of string, and he goes on past, and nobody in that market thanked either of them, and about four people who came through it have worked out that this is the whole of what either of them has got.**

**Chapter 0427 — A Tally-Board On A Table Face Up, And Two Men On One Stair.** Day 427, a Monday, the hundred and twelfth. The room over a market, at about the eighth hour, and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall has not come up those nine steps. **The man of about thirty-eight at the salt wharf comes up before the light is off the low ground with the tally-board under his arm and the knife in his hand, and puts the board on his knees with the face of it up, which he has not done before, and does not read from it, and nobody in that room asks him what is on it.** He says out loud that he knows it is the first time, that it is not modesty, and that a figure he has said out loud is a figure somebody is going to ask him where he got, and the one he sends is not the one he has, and there is no page in this city that has both of them on it, and he has stopped turning it over. **Nobody asks him for the second figure and nobody asks him where the first came from; he printed neither. The narration beside him now says that he has been putting one number into a column four times a year for nine years, that he was asked what that column was for twice, that he gave no answer on the boards and gave one in a room in seven words, and that he has never once been asked which of the two figures he puts in it.** He goes down the nine steps at about the third hour with the board under his arm and meets the man of about thirty-four coming up at the same time, and they pass each other on the stair and neither says one word. **Nobody wrote anything on the sheet and nobody asked him where he had been.**

**Chapter 0428 — A Man Is Asked What A Line Is.** Day 428, a Tuesday, the hundred and thirteenth, in a room over a market. The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register stands up at about the ninth hour and asks what a line is — that there is one of them in the second of these four places, and four people in this city are going to be asked to put one on this page, and nobody in this city has ever asked a man that, and she is asking it, and he is the man she is asking. **He answers from the back without turning round, in the local language, in five words, and those words are on that page and in no file in this repository.** Nobody thanked him, nobody improved on him, nobody told him he was right or that he was wrong, nobody said the thing back to him, and nobody asked him a second question. She gives her reason once, standing up, in her own mouth, and nobody in that room improves on it, and the crease pressed down the third of the four places is still the only thing on that paper anybody in this city has ever touched. **Adrian Vale is in that room since the seventh hour and reads the first, the second and the fourth of the four places and does not read the third, and says one word about what the man at the back of the room has said — which is that he says nothing, and tells nobody what was said and does not tell anybody it was the name of anything.**

**Chapter 0429 — Whether It Goes Out Is Asked At A Stall Front.** Day 429, a Wednesday, the hundred and fourteenth, in a market at the bottom of this city, with the stall board on its string and a length of good cord coiled beside it. **Three people went down the road that goes out of this city on the three hundred and ninety-first day of this flood, one each with a barrow, and nobody in this city can be shown one person on it, and no count of anything is printed in that market that morning.** The man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board comes through, stops at the end of the stall for the first time in this flood, puts his own board down on the flags, and asks whether the page goes out, and is told that it goes out on a date and that the date gets written in chalk on a door, and that a page that sits on a table in this city is worth nothing to anybody. He asks a second question — that there is nothing on it that says who drew what — and is told that there is nothing on it that says what anybody thinks a line is either, and that when it has gone there will be four lines on it and no person on it, and that anybody in four hundred miles who comes along after that can put a hand on any one of the four. **A woman buys about two feet of the cord and pays for it and does not know there is a page in this city at all, and a thing said out loud at a stall front on a Wednesday morning is heard by people who were not going to be told anything and cannot be told anything.** He asks who will be standing behind one of the four and not be able to show anybody which one, and is told that he has not been asked which one is his and is not going to be. **Nobody thanked either of them, and the man of about thirty-four wipes the string board down with the flat of his hand, puts the ordinary line back, and does not take the chalk out of his own pocket.**

**Chapter 0430 — A Voice At The Bottom Of The Stair About A Shut Board.** Day 430, a Thursday, the hundred and fifteenth. The room over a market, and the man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board says at about the eighth hour that the page has been lying on that table every working morning since the morning it came up the road and no morning has gone past in which nobody in that room looked at it, not once, and is told by somebody in the room that nobody has read it, and answers that he is not saying anybody has read it, and that it is the only thing anybody in this city owns that gets looked at every morning and has nothing in it, and that he sells nineteen rates on a board in four markets and knows the difference between a thing people look at and a thing people buy, and that he does not know what to do with having noticed it. **Nobody told him he was right and nobody thanked him.** At about the tenth hour a voice comes up the bottom of the nine steps, stops where the stair turns, is not one of the nine, says one sentence — that the board was shut on a Monday and on a Tuesday — and goes back down. **The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall is at the back with his hand flat on his own inside breast pocket, the chalk in that pocket does not move, he does not get up, and he does not say one word back down the stair, and nobody in that room goes down after the voice and nobody sends anybody.** The register-keeper has the book shut on its shelf and there is a slate on that shelf with a date on it and nothing under it and has had for the length of this flood, and about four people in that room have worked out that she could have written the day on one and that a second one is not going to be written either and that nobody has asked her for one. **The man of about fifty-seven is at the end of the bench and is not asked anything and is not looked at. At the fourth hour about nine people go down nine steps and the man of about thirty-four goes last and all the way down and puts his stall board on its string himself, and nobody in that market knew, and he was not thanked for opening up at all, and the page on that table has nothing in the third of its four places and is going out of this city on a date and there is not one thing on it that will say afterwards who drew what.**

---

## NOTE ON CHAPTERS 0421 TO 0430 — REVIEW REPAIR PASS, and what it changed, and this block is the authoritative one for these ten

**The ten entries above describe the files as they stand. A review of Batch 0003 returned eight findings and three of them were on a page; the five state-layer findings and the eleven further faults this pass found in the state layer are at `state/batch-summaries/volume-09-batch-0003.md` §14. Three sentences moved on three pages and no fourth page was touched, and the entries above are written against the files as they now stand.**

**1. `chapter-0430.md`, the two mornings.** The line now says the board was shut on a Monday and on a Tuesday. It said a Tuesday and a Sunday, **and the Sunday in it was day 426, and Chapter 0426 puts that board on its string that whole morning with the man behind it, so the page contradicted the page.** Days 427 and 428 are named by no page as shut and by no page as open, both are the right weekday for the day each names, and both fall in the same week as the Thursday the sentence is spoken on. The chapter's event is untouched: the man is told at the foot of a stair that he has lost two mornings, he does not get up and he does not go down, and he opens at the fourth hour and is thanked by nobody.

**2. `chapter-0421.md`, the deadline.** The register-keeper no longer says there is no morning after Saturday to say it in. **Saturday is day 425 and Chapters 0426 to 0430 are the mornings after it.** She now says that on the Saturday she is going to say the other thing instead, which keeps the deadline she set herself and points at the decision Chapter 0425 takes. The chapter gained two words.

**3. `chapter-0427.md`, the man at the salt wharf.** The narration no longer says that nobody ever asked him what the column was for. **`chapters/volume-08/chapter-0357.md:15` and `chapters/volume-08/chapter-0357.md:51` have him asked exactly that, twice, once on the wet boards and once in a room; he gave no answer on the boards and gave one in the room in seven words; and what he has never been asked is which of the two numbers he puts in it.** The sentence now carries all of that, the beat is stronger for it, no speech was cut and no fact was taken out, and the chapter gained thirty-two words.

**WHAT DID NOT MOVE IN THESE TEN CHAPTERS.** No day, no weekday, no Bare-Month day, no notice, no form, no object count that holds, no anchor, no cast member and no decision of record. The third place is still decided empty on day 425, nothing is still written on the sheet, nothing is still entered in the register, the second slate is still untouched, the sixteen posts and the eleven withies are unchanged, Adrian Vale is still in two chapters and in no mouth, there is still no panel, and the volume's one rule is still fixed to day 438 and is written on no page of these ten. **The words the man of about thirty-four says in Chapter 0428 are in none of these ten entries above and in no file in this repository, including this one, and that is the same rule that keeps them out of the batch record, the bible section and the state blocks.**

# VOLUME 09, BATCH 0004 — Chapters 0431 to 0440, days 431 to 440 — and this block wins over every block above it

**Ten chapters on disk, days 431 to 440, one chapter to one day, no double day, the chapter number is the day number. The run was recomputed from day 1 being a Tuesday and from nothing else before any prose was written: 431 Friday, 432 Saturday, 433 Sunday, 434 Monday, 435 Tuesday, 436 Wednesday, 437 Thursday, 438 Friday, 439 Saturday, 440 Sunday. The Bare Month days are 116 to 125, the Nth being day less 315. Every figure in this block is measured in `state/batch-summaries/volume-09-batch-0004.md` with the definition beside it, and this block copies no mouth: the words the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says in Chapter 0428 are on that page and in no file in this repository, including this one, and the volume's one rule said out loud in Chapter 0438 is on that page and in no file in this repository either.**

**Chapter 0431 — A Room Works Out It Has Nothing To Put A Mark On The Page With.** Day 431, a Friday, the hundred and sixteenth day of the Bare Month. The room over a market, the day in chalk on its door from the sixth hour, the sheet on the table with its four places and a crease pressed the whole length of the third of them and nothing in any of the four, and a lump of chalk lying on the edge of that table. **A woman at the far end of the bench asks the practical question — what the thing is going to be drawn with — and nobody takes it up, and the reason nobody takes it up is that picking up a thing lying in front of you in a room where nobody has asked anybody anything is not a thing a room does, and the narration says so once and does not turn it into a rule. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall, at the back, with his own hand shut over the inside breast pocket of his coat, says out loud that nobody has asked one of them and that a man who has not been asked puts down whatever his hand is on, and what is nearest every hand in that room is the edge of that table. She answers him by saying that he is not going to ask her and is not going to ask the other three, and that he is not going to be one of the four, and that he has said the last of that out loud in a room with a date on the door so that nobody afterwards can say they worked it out. Nobody thanked him and the chalk did not move. The register-keeper then asks the only question that room asks: if it is going to be four of them, it will have to be four of the nine people who were in that room on the Sunday the page was read out, because those are the only nine anybody in that city is able to name. Nothing was put on the page that morning, the crease was not deepened and not opened out, and the woman at the end of the bench, having asked once and been told out loud in front of nine people that it was not going to be asked, does not ask it again and stops looking at the edge of that table.**

**Chapter 0432 — Four Lines In The Second Of The Four Places.** Day 432, a Saturday, the hundred and seventeenth day of the Bare Month. Somebody has picked the lump of chalk up off the edge of the table and put it down again in the middle of it, **and that is the whole of what changed in that room before anybody said anything.** A woman of about fifty-two puts a mark in the second of the four places at about the eighth hour, without being asked and without asking, having said out loud in that room on a Saturday a fortnight before that one of the four lines on that page was going to be hers, and having said it first so that nobody could afterwards say she had been asked. A man of about thirty-six puts the second mark down and goes back down the steps. The two marks are not the same length and neither of the two people who made them is asked about it. The man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board puts the third down at about the tenth hour and says nothing at all while he does it, and nobody in that room looks at his face. The man of about fifty-seven comes up at about the eleventh hour with a length of new rope over his arm, which is where he carries it and where it has been and where it is going to stay, and **he reads the fourth mark out loud, in the local language, because it was in front of him and the room had stopped talking, and he reads it the way a man reads a line off a page when there is nobody to read it to, and he does not say it was his.** Nobody suggests it and nobody asks him. The register-keeper asks which of the four is going to say so, and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall answers from the back of the room without turning round that he told that room yesterday, in front of nine people and before a mark was on the paper, that he is not one of the four. Adrian Vale is in that room from the seventh hour, reads the first and second places twice and the fourth, does not read the third, and says nothing. Nothing is written in the first place or the fourth; the third is a person and is empty on purpose, with the crease still pressed down it. **The four marks go out of that room with no name on any of them, and not one of the four people who drew them said which of the four was which.**

**Chapter 0433 — Nine Steps Down Into A Market, And Two People With No Date On Anything.** Day 433, a Sunday, the hundred and eighteenth day of the Bare Month. The light comes off the water along the flags of the market at the bottom of this city and lies there and does not move all morning. The register-keeper comes down the nine steps at about the ninth hour with her hands empty, having not been in that market since the morning the page came up the road, and she buys nothing. **She and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall say four things to each other at a stall front in about a quarter of an hour, and the fourth of them is that she would like it said that she has noticed nobody asks her whether she wants to be anything.** The ordinary line on his string board is in chalk and the barrow at the end of that stall has nothing in it. A woman comes through and buys about two feet of the cord, and the cord goes in a bag and the bag goes out of that market, and nobody in it knew that either of those two people had a room over their head with a date in chalk on its door. The man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board comes through at about the eleventh hour on his own way to somewhere and does not stop at that stall, as he has not stopped at it on any morning of this flood, and about four people in that market have counted that he has not. She goes back up the nine steps, stands in the doorway, does not go in for the rest of that morning, and the market goes on under her the whole time.

**Chapter 0434 — A Stall Board Brought Up Nine Steps And Put Against A Wall.** Day 434, a Monday, the hundred and nineteenth day of the Bare Month. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall comes up the nine steps about the fourth hour of the afternoon with his own board under his arm and puts it against the inside of the wall by the door, and nobody in that room asks him why and nobody says one word about the board. **He is doing it because on the Friday the page goes out he is not going to be at the market, and the only place in this city where a man can sit on a Friday and not be spoken to is a room that does not ask anybody anything.** A woman near the step says the practical thing about it — that the board will be against that wall on Friday morning and so will everything else in there, and that there is a clerk somewhere who will get that page and nobody in that room to tell him whose hand is on any of those four — and he answers that the board and the four are two things that are not about each other, that he has brought a board up nine steps and set it down, that he has not asked anybody in that room to agree with him about that, and that he has not told her she is wrong about the other, and that those are three things he has done that morning and not one of them is a thing anybody has to answer. Nobody thanked him; he has asked in advance, in a lane a long way back, not to be thanked. He has asked for that piece of chalk in about a dozen rooms that week and has not been asked for it once. The board stays against the wall for the rest of the afternoon, he goes down last of the nine and takes all nine steps slowly, his own board goes up on its string at the market end, and nobody in that market knew he had carried a second board up those steps and brought nothing out of it.

**Chapter 0435 — A Cart-Track Taken Out On The Low Side Of A Shut Road.** Day 435, a Tuesday, the hundred and twentieth day of the Bare Month. A shut road out of this city, out past any house, running under a fence along the top of it. The wind comes up it in pushes it has been coming up since before the water and has never once been written down; the light comes up out of the low ground behind the trees and stops at the fence and does not come over. A man of about sixty has a handcart, a hazel mallet, a cord, a coil of rope that has been used and is not new, a bundle of wedges and a length of board, and **he is packing the wet ground under the boards of about nine yards of cart-track with the back of a mallet and is not doing anything to the fence, and what he is doing is not for anybody and nobody has ever sent him.** The man of about fifty-seven comes up the same road with his barrow at about the eleventh hour and stops at the cart the way a man stops at a cart, and there is nothing in his barrow and there is not going to be, and he does not take the end of a wedge and he does not take the end of a board. He says out loud, to the barrow and not to the man, that nobody ever asks him to hold anything. The man of about sixty does not stop working, and answers that he is not going to start now and that the arrangement between the two of them is the whole of it and has been the whole of it every time they have been on that road together. Then he puts the mallet down in the cart and turns round for the first time that morning, and says that he has been saying things to that barrow since the light came and that he is not going to ask him to stop, and that he will be up there on the next day the ground is soft and he can say it again. The man of about sixty picks the mallet up and goes on packing. The fence along the top of that road is sixteen willow posts and eleven withies bound with the same cord, and **nobody has ever measured anything on that road.** The man of about fifty-seven goes back down the road he came up on and does not stop at the fence and does not look at it.

**Chapter 0436 — Nothing On Any Of The Boards For Anybody To Fill In.** Day 436, a Wednesday, the hundred and twenty-first day of the Bare Month. A salt wharf at the bottom of this city whose boards are the one flat thing within about four streets of anywhere, and the light comes off the river and lies the whole length of them in one sheet and stays where it was laid. A stack higher than a man at the top end, a strip of clear board about as long as a cart at the bottom of it, a tally-board, a knife with the edge gone blunt, a barrow with nothing in it. **Nothing on any of it has anything to be filled in, and the page with a heading and four places ruled under it is nine steps above a market and is not on those boards and is not on any of them.** The man of about thirty-eight is turning boards over at the top end and has been at it since the light came off the water, and he does not lift his head. Tamsin Quill stands at the bottom of the boards for about an hour with a folded sheet of road in the inside of her coat and about nine places on it, and she does not take it out, and she says one ordinary thing to him about his order and he tells her the order is not hers and not his. There is a stone at the lower end of those boards, about the length of a door and thick as two hands, that came off a building when the water came, lying in the wet with about four inches of silt under one end of it; **it will not roll, because the ground under it is wet to the depth of a hand and there is not six feet of boards clear at that end to roll it into.** A man has been working round that stone for a month, has cut boards to go past it and boards to go short of it, and has a strip of clear board at the bottom of his stack that is the only piece of board on that wharf flat enough to lay over a hole, and nobody has laid it over that stone and he has not laid it there himself. He tells a man carrying a length of board to take it round because the middle is soft at the lower end today, and nobody on that wharf says thank you. Adrian Vale is on those boards about the seventh hour, is asked nothing by anybody and has nothing declined from anybody, watches the man at the top end work for about an hour and the woman at the bottom end stand, walks down to the stone, looks at the four inches of silt under the near end of it, and goes back up to the top end where he had been. He says nothing to anybody about it. Nothing is written down on that wharf, the tally-board comes out of the middle of the day with the same face it went in with, the knife stays blunt, the strip of clear board does not move, and the stone does not move.

**Chapter 0437 — Who Is Going To Take It Down To The Road.** Day 437, a Thursday, the hundred and twenty-second day of the Bare Month. By the ninth hour about nine people are in that room, and the stall board is still against the inside of the wall by the door where it has been since the Monday before. **The man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board asks the first question that room has ever asked about getting a thing done rather than about the thing: who is going to take it out of this room.** Nobody in that room had said anything about the day it goes out, which is the next morning; about four people there had known for a fortnight and about five had not. The register-keeper says the answer standing up: she is, he carries things for a living and carries this in a satchel and does not open what he carries, and she will be the one who put it in his hand. Nobody takes her side or the other. A woman near the step says it should be somebody nobody is going to ask about afterwards, and she refuses out loud to be handed to anybody and to hand it to anybody either, and gives the reason: that handing it over is not the same as sending it, and that it will be the same as sending it by the time it has been on a cart for a week, and that she has not found the thing to do about that on any working morning since the Saturday and is not going to find it in front of nine people on a Thursday. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall, with his hand not on his pocket for once, says the last thing said in that room that day: that he will go down those nine steps first and open his own board up, because a man with a stall at the market end will be standing there on Friday when that satchel goes past, and he is not going to be asked anything about it and is not going to say one word about it. Nobody thanked him. **The page went out of that room in the morning of the next day.**

**Chapter 0438 — The Page Goes Out On Its Date And The Wall Says One Thing.** Day 438, a Friday, the hundred and twenty-third day of the Bare Month. The sheet lies where it has lain every morning, and a stall board is against the wall by the door, and a book is shut on a shelf, and a second slate is on the shelf behind it with a date on it and nothing under the date, and nobody in that room goes near the shelf. At about the ninth hour the register-keeper folds the sheet along the two folds that are already in it, puts a stone off a doorstep on the top of it, and puts it into the hands of a man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living, who has carried five sheets of paper in a coat and a return in a satchel and this one, and who has not opened any of them. **The wall says one thing, once, in plain words and with a date in chalk on the door in front of about nine people, and it is a rule and not an answer: that a mark on a page is nothing at all until a person puts a name to it, and that a page carrying a mark and no name belongs in the end to whoever comes along the road next, **and the wording of that rule is on the page in Chapter 0438 and in no file in this repository, including this one, and what is written here is a paraphrase and not the rule**.** The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall is on his feet before the last of it has finished coming up, and refuses it out loud in about four seconds, and the cost of the refusal is named out loud by him and by nobody else: he has got one of those nine miles up that road, he wrote it last, on purpose, in a harder piece of chalk than the rest of it, it is on a wall and nothing has ever gone over the top of it, and the rule has just told that room his own line is not his either, and that the reason nothing has moved it is that there has been nobody on that road to move it. Nobody improves on what he said and nobody thanked him; the piece of chalk in his own inside breast pocket did not move. The man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board then says the other thing that morning, flat, to the middle of the room: that four of them drew those four and there is not one of their names on any of them, that the one place that could have put one there is empty on purpose with the crease still pressed the whole length of it, and that anybody who comes along that road next can put a hand on any one of the four and there is nothing on that page to stop them. **Nobody told him he was right and nobody told him he was wrong, and the room let him have the whole of it and said nothing.** A woman near the step says the last thing anybody says, that it is a page with four lines on it and no person on it and it has gone out of that room and that is all that has happened, and the man of about thirty-four agrees and says there is a stall at the market end that goes up at the seventh hour and he is going to be standing behind it and that is not a thing any of them has to do anything about. The sheet goes out of this city with four marks on it and nothing in the third of its four places, in a satchel with a flap, under a stone off a doorstep, in the hands of a man who carries things for a living and did not open it. **That day is not a silence in this city and the count of them is one lower because of it.**

**Chapter 0439 — A Table With A Clean Rectangle On It.** Day 439, a Saturday, the hundred and twenty-fourth day of the Bare Month. Nobody was sent for anywhere, and about nine people came up nine steps for their own reasons. **The sheet is not on the table, and there is a clean rectangle on the wood where it had lain, the size of a sheet of paper with a heading on it, and there is no cloth on that table and no board on it either.** The stall board is still against the wall by the door, and the man who put it there came back for it himself at the end of the previous afternoon and carried it down the steps. The man of about fifty-seven is at the end of the bench with a length of new rope over the back of his chair and does not touch it and has never used it, and nobody in that room said one word to it. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall is at the back with the piece of chalk in his own inside breast pocket, and nobody thanked him for anything he said the day before and nobody asked him whether he had said it. The man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board comes in at about the tenth hour, stands in the doorway and looks at the clean rectangle, sits at the wall end of the bench with his own slate flat on his knee under his right hand, and says — to nobody asked, and not asked twice — that he has been in that room every working morning since that page came up the road, that this is the first morning there has been nothing on the table, that he has not got a thing to say about it, and that he would rather be quiet. Nobody improves on that and nobody asks him for more of it. **About four people in that room have worked out since that a man who writes nineteen rates on a board of his own for four markets spent the middle of a Saturday morning saying that he had nothing to say, and that the reason he was in the room at all was that there was nothing in the room to go to.** Attention then goes out of the window over the step at the back, because a man with a barrow went up the road out of this city at the seventh hour that morning, and about four people who have been up that road before say that he puts the barrow down on the low side at the fence and picks it up again and does not stop at the fence, and that nobody in this city is going to ask him why, and that nobody in that room was going to be told any of it. The second slate is on the shelf with a date on it and nothing under the date and is not picked up and is not turned over and is not written on.

**Chapter 0440 — The Sheet Comes Back On A Sunday, And One Of The Four Is Not Where It Went Out.** Day 440, a Sunday, the hundred and twenty-fifth day of the Bare Month. The light comes off the water in the market at the bottom of this city and lies along the flags and does not move. A man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living comes up nine steps with a satchel with a flap, puts it on the table and does not open it. **The register-keeper opens it herself and turns the sheet out in the light: the four marks are in the second of the four places one above another, three of them are where they had been and one of them is not, the first and the fourth places are empty, and the third place has a crease pressed down the length of it with a bone and nothing in it at all, and the crease is where it was pressed before the sheet ever came up this road.** Nobody says one word about it for a good while. The man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board looks at the four marks for a while and then says one thing, and what he says is about the paper and not about the four: that it is the same sheet, the same four marks, the same crease and the same heading, that one of the four is not in the place it went out in, and that he is not able to tell anybody which of the four it is and neither is anybody else in that room, including the four of them who put them there, because one of those four read his out loud in that room and did not say it was his. **The register-keeper says that they are not going to know which one it is, that there is no page in this city she can put that on, and that she is not going to have that room spend a morning on it, so he can stop looking at it.** He takes his slate off his knee, puts it down on the table beside the sheet and leaves it there. Nobody asks anybody anything, nobody says one word about who, and nobody in that room goes down nine steps to say one word to anybody about it. Then nothing happens in that room for about an hour, and the carrier sits down on the bench by the door and does not say where he had it or how long he had had it or who had handed it to him, and nobody asks him. **A woman near the step says one thing into the middle of the room and to nobody: that there is one person in this city who would have been able to tell that room something about that page, that she is not in that room and has not been in it since the morning she was asked where her line was, and that none of them is going to go and ask her, and that this was settled in there without a word being said on it. Nobody agreed with her out loud and nobody disagreed, and the reason nobody is going to ask her is not that she would not answer.** The sheet lay flat on that table through the rest of that Sunday with four marks in the second of the four places and nothing in the third, nobody wrote anything on it, nothing was entered in the book on the shelf, and the one thing that happened on that day is that a page came back and one of the four marks on it was not where it had gone out.

**The ten entries above describe the files as they stand. A review of Batch 0004 returned ten findings; four were carried out on the pages, one was carried out on the state layer, and five were declined with the reason named. The full account is at `state/batch-summaries/volume-09-batch-0004.md` §12. Twenty-seven line-level repairs, across all ten pages and the ninth and tenth chapters were not touched, and the entries above are written against the files as they now stand.**

**1. `chapter-0440.md`, the day arithmetic.** The narration no longer says that the hundred and twenty-fifth day of the Bare Month is day 440. **A narrator saying a chapter's own number out loud puts the book's bookkeeping inside the fiction, and the mapping is day less 315 and belongs to the outline and not to a sentence anybody speaks.** The sheet's return, the carrier, the barrow coming down the same road the same morning, and the sentence that says the two of those had nothing to do with each other are all as they were.

**2. `chapter-0438.md`, the third place.** The third of the four places is no longer folded down. **Every other chapter of this volume and every state file has that place ruled, empty, with a crease pressed the whole length of it with a bone, and *folded down* is a place that cannot be read, which is a different thing from a place left blank, and Chapter 0440 depends on the difference when it says the crease is where it was pressed before the sheet came up this road.** It now says empty on purpose with the crease still pressed the whole length of it. The man of about thirty-nine's argument is the same argument and is not made a different one.

**3. `chapter-0434.md`, the count of three.** A man's three achievements are now three different things — that he carried a board up nine steps and set it down, that he asked nobody to agree with him about it, and that he did not tell a woman she was wrong about the other — **where the first edition counted the fact that he had not told her she was wrong and the fact that he had not told her she was right, which is one silence counted twice, and presented it as three.** The mouth keeps its count and its self-accounting and lost no word it was carrying, and the chapter's event, the board against the wall and him going down last and taking all nine steps slowly, is untouched.

**4. `chapter-0431.md`, a broken clause and a closer that repeated the chapter.** The sentence about the chalk not being picked up no longer has a doubled *that* and a tail about what nobody thought of afterwards, **which was not a reason, and the paragraph now holds one tense where it had drifted across three.** The closing paragraph no longer restates in the narrator's voice what the chapter has already shown and what its own title already says; it now ends on the woman at the end of the bench not asking her question a second time and stopping looking at the edge of that table. **The chapter's change is what it was: nothing was put on the page, and the room learned out loud that it is not going to ask anybody anything.**

**5. `chapter-0439.md` and `chapter-0440.md`, two closers and one sentence.** Chapter 0439's closer no longer restates the chapter and now ends on the man of about thirty-nine being let be quiet, which is what he asked for. Chapter 0440 no longer says that nothing happened and that that was the whole of what happened. **And the two chapters no longer call the four things on the page lines in their narration after eight chapters had called them marks; the two mouths that say lines are left as they are, because a woman in a room calling them lines is the volume's point and not its error.**

**6. Seven lifted passages, varied in place with every fact kept, across Chapters 0431, 0432, 0433, 0434, 0435, 0436 and 0437.** **Against the four hundred and thirty pre-existing files the ten chapters carried verbatim runs of up to thirty-six words; after the repair the top run in the batch is twenty-four and every run of twenty words or more left in the ten files is one of five tracked frames — the second-handle frame, the chorus frame, the day ledger, the man of about fifty-seven and his length of new rope, and the standing refusal not to be thanked — and none of the five is a lifted description.** Three further lifts were found and broken in the same pass and are named at §12.3 of the batch record. **No day, no weekday, no Bare-Month day, no object count that holds, no anchor, no cast member and no decision of record moved. The third place is still decided empty, nothing is still written in the sheet's third place or the register's third column, the second slate is still untouched, the sixteen posts and the eleven withies are unchanged, Adrian Vale is still in two of the ten and reads nothing in one of them, there is still exactly one panel in the ten and it is still on day 438, and the mark that came back out of place is still not traced. The words the man of about thirty-four says in Chapter 0428 are in none of these ten entries above and in no file in this repository, including this one, and the volume's one rule said out loud in Chapter 0438 is on that page and in no file in this repository either.**

# VOLUME 09, BATCH 0005 — Chapters 0441 to 0450, days 441 to 450 — and this block wins over every block above it

**Ten chapters on disk, days 441 to 450, one chapter to one day, no double day, the chapter number is the day number. The run was recomputed from day 1 being a Tuesday and from nothing else before any prose was written: 441 Monday, 442 Tuesday, 443 Wednesday, 444 Thursday, 445 Friday, 446 Saturday, 447 Sunday, 448 Monday, 449 Tuesday, 450 Wednesday. The Bare Month days are 126 to 135, the Nth being day less 315, and 135 is the last day of that month any chapter of this volume may write. Every figure in this block is measured in `state/batch-summaries/volume-09-batch-0005.md` with the definition beside it. This block copies no mouth: the words the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says in Chapter 0428, the seven words spoken in Chapter 0357, the six words spoken in Chapter 0393, the six words at `bible/power-system.md:1686` and the volume's one rule said in Chapter 0438 are on their own pages and in no file in this repository, including this one. THIS IS THE LAST BATCH OF THE VOLUME AND THERE IS NO BATCH 0006.**

**Chapter 0441 — Nobody In This City Knows When The Cart Comes Round Again.** Day 441, a Monday, the hundred and twenty-sixth day of the Bare Month. The room over a market, the day in chalk on the door before the light was off the low ground, the sheet lying flat on the table where it has lain since the Sunday it came back, and a hand put down flat on the wood beside it. **The man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board asks the question the room has been carrying, which is what day the next round comes on, and the answer is that there is nobody in this city who knows.** A woman near the step then says out loud, to the middle of the floor, that there is not one page in four hundred miles that tells a city when its own cart is going out, that she has been up and down that market on two feet asking four people who sell things, that not one of them knows or is going to, and that she is not going to ask a second time. **He then says the other half of it: he does not need the day, and what a page does is be on a table on a morning and not be on a table on a morning, and everything anybody in this city has done about that page has been about the second of those two things.** Nobody thanked either of them. **Adrian Vale is in that room from the seventh hour with his hand on the sheet, reads the first of the four places and the second of them twice and the fourth, does not read the third, says nothing at all, and is asked nothing and thanked for being in it.** The sheet went back into the satchel it came up the road in and the satchel went behind the table where it had been put on the Sunday.

**Chapter 0442 — It Is Going Again By The Time He Gets To The End Of It.** Day 442, a Tuesday, the hundred and twenty-seventh. A shut road out of this city, running under a fence along the top of it, the ground on the low side soft since before the water. A handcart with a hazel mallet in it whose head is bound in cord, a coil of rope on top of the coil, wedges out of the cart and a length of board, and one pair of hands on about nine yards of cart-track. **He is not doing anything to the fence and nobody in four hundred miles has ever sent him up that road and nobody is going to.** The rain comes across the low ground sideways about halfway along the nine yards, and the crust comes off behind him in the same order he did it in, so that by the time he is at the far end the first of them is going again and he can hear it. **He does not go back to it and carries on where the ground is firmer, and the chapter says in its own words that this is not a repair and does not get told about.** The man of about fifty-seven comes up at about the eleventh hour with his barrow, sets the handles down the way a man does at a cart, puts his hands in his sleeves instead of taking a wedge or the end of the board, and says one thing to the barrow about how many times those nine yards have been done. **The man of about sixty answers once without stopping work and says he is not going to argue about it in the wet and that the arrangement between the two of them is the same as it has been every time they have been on that road together.** The fence is sixteen willow posts and eleven withies bound with the same cord, nobody has ever measured anything on that road, and the first third of the nine yards is soft again by the time the last of it is done.

**Chapter 0443 — A Second Line In A Book That Has Kept Its Third Column Empty.** Day 443, a Wednesday, the hundred and twenty-eighth. **THE VOLUME'S RESOLUTION.** The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register comes up the nine steps with both hands under the front of her coat, takes a book off a shelf and opens it on the table, and a sheet with a heading on it is lying on the same table and is not looked at by anybody in that room. **Before the pen goes near the page she says out loud, standing up, what a book with a day in it and nothing under the day is for: that it is the only thing in four hundred miles a man four hundred miles off cannot ask this city to produce. Two practical questions follow, one about the second column and one about what a man four hundred miles off writes when he reads a line with a day in it and nothing under it, and her answer to both is that nothing goes in the second column and no name goes in the third, and that she would like nobody in the room to answer either of them.** She rules the line herself with the back of the pen, writes the day in the first column, reads it back before writing anything else, and puts the cap back on. **She then says, in one sentence and in one mouth, both days: that this morning is the hundred and twenty-eighth day of the Bare Month, a Wednesday, and that the day in the line is the hundred and twenty-third day of the Bare Month, a Friday, which is five days counted back from this morning and not counted forward, and that the day in the line is the morning the page went out of this city.** **No name goes under either day. Nobody thanked her and nobody told her she was right or that she was wrong.** The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says from the back of the room that nobody in that room is going to ask her about it either and that he would like that noticed, and nobody thanks him for it. **The narration closes on the fact that this is the third time anything has gone into that book since the water came, that two of the three have a day in the first column and nothing under the day, and that the third column has been empty on purpose the whole time and nobody in that room has asked the woman who keeps it why, and the reason is not that she would not say.** The second slate on that shelf carries a date and nothing under the date and was not picked up, not turned over and not written on.

**Chapter 0444 — Four Stalls In That Market Have Changed Hands Since The Water.** Day 444, a Thursday, the hundred and twenty-ninth. The room over a market, the man of about fifty-seven at the end of the bench with the length of new rope over the back of his chair that he does not touch and has never used. **The man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board says out loud, before anybody has asked him, that four stalls in that market have changed hands since the water came and that every one of the four new men has paid a day's stallage for a market where nothing is selling.** A woman who has had a stall in that market since before the water tells him from the step that the four have not changed hands, that the same four people have been behind the same four boards every morning, that two of them have put their own name on a board belonging to somebody who has gone up the road, and that one has had a new front put on and calls that taking it over. **He takes the short half of it without heat and she finishes it off, and nobody in that room thanks either of them.** He then says the other half and it is not about boards: that this is a thing he has been wrong about and he would rather have been wrong about that than about a page, and that somebody four hundred miles off is keeping a list of a market in this city with four names on it and that the list came out of the same satchel the pages come out of, and that he is not going to say the two are the same thing because nobody in that room would be able to prove it. **Nobody agreed with him out loud and nobody disagreed.** The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall is one of the four boards in that market and says one word about it and nobody asks him.

**Chapter 0445 — What Is A Line For, Asked In A Room And Answered By Nobody.** Day 445, a Friday, the hundred and thirtieth. **THE VOLUME'S NEW QUESTION, AND IT IS NOT ANSWED.** A woman of about sixty-nine comes up the nine steps with a tin in both hands and stands at the end of the bench and does not sit down, and nobody in that room asked her what she brought it for and nobody ever has. **Before she asks it, the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says one ordinary thing about a draft on that bench and about wanting her to stand somewhere warmer and wanting nobody in the room to make anything of it, and a woman near the step says something about the light on the flags and how little she has done about it.** Then she waits for the room to go quiet and asks it to the room and not to anybody, in the local language, and it is what a line is for on a page when the only thing anybody will use it for is to move it. **Nobody in that room answered it. The next line says that about four of the nine had an answer and about four of them said nothing, and that nobody in that room put either group in front of the other one, and nobody thanked her, nobody told her it was a good question, and nobody in that room said one word about it again for the rest of the morning.** Afterwards the room says other things — about a man on the boards at the bottom of the street, and about whether the shelf behind the table was there when the room was taken — **and none of those things has anything to do with what was asked, and the page with a place for a person on it is lying on the table the whole time and nobody in that room used any of it to answer the question.** The man of about fifty-seven is at the end of the bench and is asked nothing and offers nothing. She goes back down the nine steps with the tin in both hands and nobody follows her and nobody says anything to her on the way down, **and the question went out of that room with her and is on no page in four hundred miles and was not answered by anybody.**

**Chapter 0446 — He Stops At That Stall For The Second Time In This Flood.** Day 446, a Saturday, the hundred and thirty-first. A market at the bottom of this city, the light off the water along the flags, a stall board up on its string with the ordinary line on it in chalk, a length of good cord coiled beside it, and an empty barrow at the end of the stall with nothing in it since the water came. **The man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board comes through the way he comes through every morning and stops at the end of that stall, which he has done once in this flood and not been back to since, and nobody in that market had asked him about either of those two things.** He puts his board down on the flags and asks nothing; the exchange that follows is about a price on a board and about a man who has stood at a thing twice not being able to stop a third time, and nobody in that market heard either of them say a word about a page. **The cord goes over the counter and the money goes into the man's own hand and no measure is said out loud in either direction, which is how that stall has always done it.** A woman buying cord asks the man of about thirty-nine whether anybody four hundred miles inland is selling cord at that price too, and the narration says in its own words that four hundred miles is how far the page came from and is a place a page came from and not a place anybody in that market has ever gone to, and that nobody in that market said a word about the three who went the other way on that road with a barrow each, and that nobody knows where they are. He picks his board up and goes on through the market and does not stop again.

**Chapter 0447 — What Is Done To A Page While It Is Waiting.** Day 447, a Sunday, the hundred and thirty-second. The room over a market, the sheet on the table with its four places, the third of them empty with a crease pressed down the length of it, and the first and the fourth empty. **The man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board says that a page is on a table on a morning and not on a table on a morning, that there is a stretch of time between those two Sundays that nobody in this city knows the length of, and that there is not one thing in this flood a room can do with a page on a table except wait — and he puts his own board down against the leg of the bench, which is not a thing he does.** The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register says her half of it standing: that she is not going to fold it again until it is going out again, and that it is not going to be folded by a person who has not been asked, and that she wants that said out loud because there is no way in the world of asking anybody to say it. **He answers that it does not get folded and he does not get asked, that those are the two things, and that he is not going to say either of them again, and nobody thanks him.** **Adrian Vale is in that room from the seventh hour with his hand on the sheet, reads the first of the four places and the second and the fourth, does not read the third, and after a while says one sentence to the page and not to the room, which is that there is nothing at the top of it. A woman repeats it back to him as a question, he does not answer it, nobody takes it up, and nobody asks him a second question.** The page stays on the table and is not touched.

**Chapter 0448 — The High Side Of A Shut Road, And A Barrow That Does Not Stop.** Day 448, a Monday, the hundred and thirty-third. **PHYSICAL WORK IN WEATHER.** A shut road out of this city, the wind in the pushes it has been coming in since before the water, a handcart on the high side above the low ground with a hazel mallet in it and a coil of rope on top of the coil, and the high side is the one part of the road that has not gone soft again. A man with a barrow goes up past the cart and past the fence at the seventh hour with a hurdle of hazel rods in the bottom of it that he made and a bar across the top of it that he replaced, and nothing else, and nobody on that road speaks to him. **He comes back down at about the ninth hour, stops on the low side of the fence for about as long as it takes to turn a bar over, picks the barrow up again and goes on down, and the man of about sixty says one thing to his back about not carrying a wedge down there.** The man of about sixty answers him once, and says he does not know what any of them went up for and is not going to put a second bar in that barrow to find out, and that the high side will hold and that is the whole of what he has got out of it. **Nothing on that road is measured, the light stops at that fence and does not come over it, the withies are bound with the same cord the posts were set with, and the road goes on down under the fence into the low ground to a lane of nine houses that is not in this city.**

**Chapter 0449 — A Chair Is Moved And The Rope Goes Onto The Floor.** Day 449, a Tuesday, the hundred and thirty-fourth. The room over a market, the man of about fifty-seven at the end of the bench with the length of new rope over the back of his chair where it has been every morning of this flood and has never once been used. **A woman at the far end gets up to let somebody past her, moves that chair about a foot along the bench, and the length of new rope comes off the back of it onto the floorboards while the room goes on talking about something else.** Nobody in that room says one word about it, nobody asks him anything and nobody tells him anything, and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says nothing at all about it. **He bends down, picks the rope up off the floor, puts it back over the back of his chair, says nothing while he does it and nothing after it, and nobody in that room has ever thanked him for anything.** A woman then says the practical thing about a cold on the bench, and he answers her from the back with his reason for not telling her, which is that if he told her she would stop saying it out loud and she is the only person in that room who says it. **Nobody in that room asked him what the rope is for and nobody said the word fence in a sentence that had anything to do with it.** The man of about thirty-nine says one thing about a day of the week arriving earlier than it does on the others, the register-keeper does not open the book, the rope is where it was, and nothing was said about that day by anybody.

**Chapter 0450 — Three Barrows On A Road Out Of This City.** Day 450, a Wednesday, the hundred and thirty-fifth. **THE VOLUME'S LAST IMAGE, AND IT IS AN IMAGE AND NOT A RESOLUTION.** A room over a market with a date in chalk on its door above the table, about nine people in it by the fourth hour, and a sheet with a heading on it lying on that table with a crease pressed down the length of the third of its four places and the first and the third and the fourth all empty. **Nobody in that room was sent for and nobody was waiting to be sent for, and the man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board had his slate on his knee and said nothing that morning at all.** The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register stands up at about the ninth hour, puts her hand flat on the table beside the sheet, does not touch it, and says the one ordinary thing she says: that it is lying there and not going anywhere today, that the third of those four places is empty on purpose, that nobody in the room is going to put anything in it, and that she is not going to ask anybody to. **Nobody thanks her, nobody tells her she is right and nobody tells her she is wrong; the register is on its shelf with its third column empty and the second slate carries a date and nothing under the date and was not picked up, not turned over and not written on.** A woman near the step says that whoever comes up those steps next will be able to see where the page has been lying, and that that is the only thing anybody has got out of the last stretch of mornings, and that it is not a thing you can put anywhere, and nobody agrees with her out loud and nobody disagrees. **The morning goes on, the four places under the heading stay as they are, and nobody says one word about the hundred and thirty-fifth day of the Bare Month or about what it is or what comes after it. The last image in the volume is a road out of this city with three barrows on it, which goes away under a fence along the top of it and out past the last named house, and there is nobody in this city who can be shown one person on that road, and nobody in that room went down nine steps that day to ask, and the door behind them had a day on it in chalk and nothing above it.**

**The ten entries above describe the files as they stand. The writing pass ran its instruments as it went and repaired what they returned, and the first reading of these ten files is published in `state/batch-summaries/volume-09-batch-0005.md` at §10a and §3 item 10, and every figure in this block was re-measured on the files after the last repair rather than carried forward. No review pass has yet run on this batch and nothing below is settled by being here.**

**1. `chapter-0448.md` and `chapter-0449.md`, two chapters with nothing marked in them.** The first reading returned two chapters at 0.00 on the share with no marked turn of any kind in them at all, which were these two, **and three more with no plain speech in them at all, which were Chapters 0443, 0445 and 0450, where a speech was narrated and never quoted.** A marked turn and a plain-speech interjection were written into each of the first two, an exchange was written into 0449, a plain speech was quoted in 0445 and 0450, a plain-speech interjection was written into 0443, and no sentence was cut in any of the ten.

**2. `chapter-0443.md`, a turn of ninety-two words and two men doing the same thing.** The woman's first turn was over the ninety-five hard ceiling and is now sixty-eight, **not by shortening it but by moving the second half of it into a marked turn of hers in answer to a practical question that a man at the far end of the bench asked.** Two men at the far end of that bench then said the same practical thing in two consecutive paragraphs, and the second of them is now a woman near the step, and the sentence that noticed it was rewritten to match.

**3. `chapter-0447.md`, two marked turns in one exchange, and a run of thirty-four words.** The register-keeper's turn and the man of about thirty-four's reply were both bolded, and the second voice is now unmarked, which took that chapter's exchange column to one of one. A thirty-four-word run of the standing refusal not to be thanked was varied in place with every fact kept, and a thirty-three-word run of the man of about thirty-four's chalk against Chapter 0438 was varied in the same pass.

**4. `chapter-0442.md` and `chapter-0448.md`, two lifted passages on a shut road.** Against the four hundred and forty pre-existing files the ten chapters carried verbatim runs of up to forty-five words, all four of them lifts of sentences this batch had written twice, having written them once in a file it had read and once in a chapter of its own. **All four were varied in place with every fact kept, the check was re-run after the repair and not before it, and it now returns a top of twenty-nine against the four hundred and forty and thirty inside the batch's own ten, and the thirty is the second-slate frame and not a lifted description.**

**5. `chapter-0446.md`, a narration paragraph in bold, and an interval figure in a lead.** A paragraph of narration about what the four-hundred-mile distance is was unmarked rather than cut, and a lead that said a barrow had been empty for about a month now says it has had nothing in it since the water came. **A lead paragraph in this volume carries no interval figure, and one did.**

**6. `chapter-0446.md`, a day count that did not work.** A sentence said the man of about thirty-nine had stopped at that stall about three weeks ago, and Chapter 0429 puts the first stop seventeen days back. **The figure is gone and the sentence now names no interval at all.**

**7. `chapter-0448.md`, a clause that had drifted.** The lead said the high side of the road was the one part of it that had gone soft and had not, and it now says it is the one part that has not gone soft again. A paragraph that said one withie on that fence had been re-bound since the last time anybody was up there, and that no hand in this city had said out loud which of them did it, **was cut of the whole of that clause**, because it is the only sentence in the ten that raises a question about that fence and answers nothing, and this volume's discipline is not to raise it.

**8. `chapter-0445.md` and `chapter-0449.md`, the cold said three times.** Two of the three instances were a woman saying the practical thing about a cold and the man of about thirty-four answering her with the same reason. **The second chapter now says a man on the boards at the bottom of the street instead, and the exchange and both mouths are otherwise untouched.**

**WHAT DID NOT MOVE IN THESE TEN CHAPTERS.** No day, no weekday, no Bare-Month day, no notice, no form, no object count that holds, no anchor, no cast member and no decision of record. **The third place is still decided empty, nothing was written in the sheet's third place, the register's second column is empty and its third column is empty, the second slate is still untouched, the sixteen posts and the eleven withies are unchanged, there is still exactly one panel in the volume and it is still on day 438 and there is none in these ten, the word *cooling* is still at zero across all fifty chapters' worth of batches, the mark that came back out of place is still not traced and is not the subject of one sentence in any of these ten, and the Bare Month still does not end inside this volume.** Adrian Vale is still in two of the ten and in no other, and in both he is in a room with a page. **The words the man of about thirty-four says in Chapter 0428, the seven words spoken in Chapter 0357, the six words spoken in Chapter 0393, the six words held at `bible/power-system.md:1686` and the volume's one rule said in Chapter 0438 are in none of these ten entries above and in no file in this repository, including this one, and that is the same rule that keeps them out of the batch record, the bible section and the four state blocks.**


## NOTE ON CHAPTERS 0441 TO 0450 — REVIEW REPAIR PASS, and what it changed, and what it did not, and this block is the authoritative one for these ten

**The block above describes these ten chapters, and every line of it is still true of them.
