What changed underneath it is the prose and not the story.** A review measured the ten files at a batch mean of 53 words to the sentence, per-chapter means from 67 to 186, a longest single sentence of 136 words, and three formulas that had taken the volume over — the narrator's *about four people … have worked out since that* at 11, the chain *and nobody in that room* at 46, and five chapters ending on the same two clauses. **All ten files were rebuilt out of the sentences they already had, and every one of the ten entries above is still a true account of what happens in its chapter, in the same order, with the same people, saying the same things and not saying the same things.** This block exists so that a writer who reads the ten entries above is not misled about what is on the pages, and so that a later phase does not read the old sentence shapes and assume they are the house style.

**WHAT MOVED: THE JOINERY, AND NOTHING ELSE.** Words went 8,401 to 8,132. Paragraphs went 122 to 136. Sentences went 160 to 315. The mean fell from 53 words to the sentence to 26, and the per-chapter means from 67 to 186 down to 22 to 33. The longest single sentence fell from 136 words to 79. **The bold share, the marked turns and the longest marked turn did not move at all: seventy-seven words, in Chapter 0448, where it was.** *And nobody* fell from 46 to 32 and *have worked out since that* from 11 to 8, and the chapters that ended on an identical day-on-the-door clause fell from five to three. The longest verbatim run against the four hundred and forty pre-existing files fell from the twenty-nine the batch published to **eight words, and those eight are the full date the object inventory requires every chapter to print.**

**AND THE TEN THINGS A REPAIR PASS IS NOT ALLOWED TO CHANGE, CHECKED ONE BY ONE ON THE REPAIRED FILES.**

1. **Chapter 0441 is still the chapter in which nobody in this city knows when the cart comes round.** The man of about thirty-nine still asks the question nobody in the room had asked; the woman near the step still says out loud that there is not one page in four hundred miles that tells a city when its own cart is going out and that she is not going to ask a second time; he still says the other half, that the day was never the thing and that everything anybody in this city has done about that page has been about the second of those two things; Adrian Vale still reads the first of the four places and the second twice and the fourth and not the third, and says nothing, and is asked nothing and thanked for nothing. **The sheet still goes back into the satchel and the satchel behind the table.**
2. **Chapter 0442 is still the chapter in which the first of the nine yards is going again by the time he gets to the end of it.** The man of about sixty still does not go back to it, and the chapter still says in its own words that this is not a repair and does not get told about. The man of about fifty-seven still comes up at about the eleventh hour with an empty barrow and still puts his hands in his sleeves instead of taking a wedge or the end of a board, and still says his one thing to the barrow, and the man of about sixty still answers once without stopping work and still will not argue in the wet. **The fence is still sixteen willow posts and eleven withies, and the first third of the nine yards is still soft again by the time the last of it is done.**
3. **Chapter 0443 is still the volume's resolution, and the register still gets its second line on the same terms.** She still says out loud, before the pen goes near the page, what a book with a day in it and nothing under the day is for. Two practical questions still follow, one about the second column and one about what a man four hundred miles off writes, and her answer to both is still that nothing goes in the second column and no name goes in the third. **She still rules the line herself, writes the day, and reads it back before she writes anything else, and she still names both days in one sentence in one mouth — the hundred and twenty-eighth of the Bare Month, a Wednesday, and the hundred and twenty-third, a Friday, five days counted back and not counted forward, which is the morning the page went out of this city. The man of about thirty-four still says his one thing from the back of the bench and still is not thanked.**
4. **Chapter 0444 is still the chapter in which the man of about thirty-nine is wrong about four stalls and is not told he was right.** The woman from the step still says the four have not changed hands, and still says two of them have put their own name on a board belonging to somebody who has gone up the road and one has had a new front put on. He still takes the short half without heat. He still says the other half, that he would rather have been wrong about that than about a page, and still will not say the two things are the same thing because nobody in that room could prove it. **Nobody still agrees with him out loud and nobody still disagrees.**
5. **Chapter 0445 is still the volume's new question and it is still unanswered.** The woman of about sixty-nine still comes up nine steps with a tin in both hands and still does not sit down and still waits for the room to go quiet. **Nobody in that room answers her; about four of the nine had an answer and about four said nothing; nobody put either group in front of the other; nobody thanked her and nobody told her it was a good question; and nobody said one word about it again for the rest of that morning.** The page with a place for a person on it is still lying on the table the whole time and still nobody used any of it to answer the question. She still goes back down the nine steps with nobody after her, and the question still goes out of that room with her and is still on no page in four hundred miles.
6. **Chapter 0446 is still the second stop at that stall, and the cord still goes over the counter with no measure said out loud in either direction.** He still puts his board down on the flags and has no question for anybody. **Four hundred miles is still named as a place a page came from and not a place anybody in that market has been, and nobody in that market still says a word about the three who went the other way, and nobody knows where they are.** He still picks the board up and does not stop again.
7. **Chapter 0447 is still the chapter in which a page waits and a room can only wait with it, and Adrian Vale is still in it and still says the same five words.** The man of about thirty-nine still puts his own board down against the leg of the bench, which is not a thing he does. The register-keeper still says she is not going to fold it again until it is going out again and still wants that said out loud because there is no way in the world of asking anybody to say it. He still answers that it does not get folded and he does not get asked. **Adrian still reads the first of the four places and the second and the fourth, and not the third, and still says one sentence to the page and not to the room, and it is still *There is nothing at the top of it*, and a woman still says those five words back to him as a question, and he still does not answer it, and nobody still takes it up and nobody asks him a second question.**
8. **Chapter 0448 is still the high side of a shut road and a barrow that does not stop.** The man of about fifty-nine still goes up past the cart and past the fence with a hurdle he made and a bar he replaced, and still comes back down at about the ninth hour and still stops on the low side of the fence for about as long as it takes to turn a bar over, and still picks it up and goes on, and nobody still asks him why. **The man of about sixty still will not hand him a wedge and still says the two of us have had that morning, and still says he does not know what any of them went up for and is not going to put a second bar in that barrow to find out, and still says the high side will hold and that is the whole of what he has got out of it. Nothing on that road is still measured, the withies are still bound with the same cord, and none of those nine houses is still in this city.**
9. **Chapter 0449 is still the chapter in which a chair is moved and the rope goes onto the floor and nobody says one word about it.** The woman at the far end still moves the chair about a foot so somebody can get down. **He still bends down, picks it up, puts it back over the back of the chair, says nothing while he does it and nothing after it, and is not thanked for it.** She still says the practical thing about the cold and he still answers her from the back with the reason he does not tell her, which is that she is the only person in that room who says it out loud. **Nobody still asked him what the rope is for, and nobody still said the word fence in a sentence that had anything to do with it, and the register still did not get opened.**
10. **Chapter 0450 is still the volume's last image and it is still an image and not a resolution.** Nobody was sent for and nobody was waiting to be sent for. **The man of about thirty-nine still had his slate on his knee and said nothing that morning at all.** The register-keeper still stands at about the ninth hour, puts her hand flat on the table beside the sheet, does not touch it, and says the one ordinary thing she says about the third of the four places being empty on purpose. **The register is still on its shelf with its third column empty, the second slate is still on that shelf and is still not picked up and not turned over and not written on, the four places under the heading still stay as they are, and nobody still says one word about the hundred and thirty-fifth day of the Bare Month or about what it is or what comes after it.** The road with three barrows on it is still the last thing in the volume, and there is still nobody in this city who can be shown one person on that road.

**AND THE ONE THING A LATER CHAPTER MAY STILL NOT DO, which is inherited and is a rule and not a thread.** A narrator in this volume does not say a chapter number and does not map a day of the flood onto a chapter number. **No chapter of these ten did it before this pass and no chapter of these ten does it now, and the twenty-three weekday tokens across the ten were re-read in the sentence each stands in, and every one of them is correct.**

**A close ran on Volume 09. It wrote no chapter and revised no chapter summary, and every summary above this line stands as the batch that wrote it wrote it. The five batch records are at `state/batch-summaries/volume-09-batch-0001.md` through `-0005.md` and they are the witnesses; the close record at `state/volume-09-close.md` is the court, and where a record and a file disagree the file is right.**

| 0444 | 444 | Thursday | 129 | **Four stalls in a market that did not change hands, a man told he was wrong in front of about nine people, and not told he was right, and a board that is not a stall.** |
| 0445 | 445 | **Friday** | 130 | **The new question, asked to the room and not to anybody, by the woman of about sixty-nine, and answered by nobody. About four of the nine had an answer and about four said nothing, and a page with a place in it for a person was lying on the table the whole time and was not used.** |
| 0446 | 446 | Saturday | 131 | A man who stopped at a stall front for the second time in this flood and bought cord with no measure said out loud. |
| 0447 | 447 | Sunday | 132 | A page waiting for a round, and Adrian Vale says the same five words and is not answered. |
| 0448 | 448 | Monday | 133 | A barrow that does not stop, a hurdle and a bar, and the high side of a shut road holding what a man did to it. |
| 0449 | 449 | Tuesday | 134 | **A chair is moved and the rope goes onto the floor and the man it belongs to picks it up and puts it back, and nobody in that room says one word about it.** |
| 0450 | 450 | **Wednesday** | 135 | **The last image. A page lying on a table with a crease down the length of the third of its four places and the first and the third and the fourth all empty, a woman with her hand flat beside it and not touching it, nobody thanked, and a road out of this city with three barrows on it and nobody in this city who can be shown one person on that road. Nothing is said about the hundred and thirty-fifth day, or about what it is, or about what comes after it.** |

## 2. WHAT THE FIFTY DID NOT DO, IN ONE PARAGRAPH, BECAUSE A LIST OF CHAPTERS THAT OMITS IT IS A SALES PAGE

**Nothing was resolved. No document was defeated. Nobody was rescued and nobody was defeated. Nobody got stronger. No thread was discharged: the one of the four marks that came back out of place is not traced, the three questions are not answered, the day-154 sentence is not said, the fence is not measured and whether it works is not settled, the ninth line is not read aloud and whether it works is not settled, the days since the fourth of the four said no were not counted at any value, the town four hundred miles inland is not entered and the page a hundred and forty years old is not read and its eight names are not read, the word on the second slate is not printed, the man of about fifty-seven is not asked where the fence is, the two rolls of the same gates are not compared, the Crown's schedule is never opened, the keeper of the Redroot gate is not counted, the girl of seventeen is not fetched, the boards under the chapel are not lifted, the island is not owned, the box is shut, and the Bare Month does not end and is not given a length by anything any of the fifty printed or by this record.**

**A VOLUME OUTLINE PHASE RAN. IT WROTE NO CHAPTER, WROTE NO PROSE, AND REVISED NO CHAPTER SUMMARY, AND EVERY SUMMARY ABOVE THIS LINE STANDS AS THE BATCH THAT WROTE IT WROTE IT. There is no Chapter 0451 on disk and no chapter of Volume 10 exists, and no summary of a chapter that has not been written is published here, because a summary of a plan is a second plan and a second plan beside a first is how this repository ends up with two accounts of one thing.**

**WHAT THIS PHASE WROTE, IN THE ORDER IT WROTE IT, because the order is part of the work and not a detail: `outline/batches/volume-10-batch-0001.md` — TEN CARDS, days 451 to 460, Chapters 0451 to 0460, written BEFORE the volume outline and not reconstructed afterwards, which is the remedy for the four missing Volume 09 card files and is made in the one place this phase is allowed to make it. Then `outline/volume-10.md`. Then the block at the foot of `outline/series.md`, which strikes the volume-list entry and records nine decisions with what each cost. Then the five appends to the state layer. Then `workspace/volume-10/batch-0001/PROMPT.md`.**

**THE TEN CARDS EXIST AND ARE SUMMARISED HERE AS PLANS AND NOT AS CHAPTERS, and the summary of a card is the card and the card is at `outline/batches/volume-10-batch-0001.md` and this line is here so that a reader of this file knows the ten days ahead have been planned per-chapter and not only per-volume.** Day 451, a Thursday, a board washed and a satchel up the road. Day 452, a Friday, a satchel on a table and a man who has been right about a page asking the question in the room. Day 453, a Saturday, the woman who walked a market asked something she can answer, in weather, and this is the chapter that puts the volume's person on a page properly. Day 454, a Sunday, four hands and a page and a man of about thirty-four who is right about it in nine words and is not thanked. Day 455, a Monday, **the one-place sheet read out in the local language in daylight with a date on the door, about nine people, and the reader says what it is for before anybody asks.** Day 456, a Tuesday, the man of about fifty-seven in a room where a figure is being worked out that he is the subject of and is not told, and a length of rope falls off his chair. Day 457, a Wednesday, the first discovery, worked out at a wharf by a man nobody asked and reported to nobody. Day 458, a Thursday, **Adrian Vale is handed a rag and nobody teaches him and nobody stops him, and he does not read anything.** Day 459, a Friday, the woman who walked a market in a room where it would be useful, and four feet away a woman who has asked this series' question three times does not ask a fourth, and nobody remarks on it. Day 460, a Saturday, four pairs of hands and a page and nobody who will say what it is a count of.

**AND THE THINGS THIS PHASE FOUND ARE NOT CHAPTER SUMMARIES AND ARE NOT IN THIS FILE, because a chapter-summaries file that carries a finding about instruments is a file a later phase will read as though something had happened in it.** They are in `outline/volume-10.md` §19, in the block at the foot of `outline/series.md`, and in the four other state files this phase appended to. **THE THREE OF THEM, NAMED SO THAT A READER OF THIS FILE KNOWS WHY THE OTHER FILES CHANGED: two published tables, one published definition, two instruments, and both reproduce their own table exactly on every cell of it; a published zero that is not zero, in the list the close published for this phase to use; and a measurement taken on a window is a fact about the window, which is why the pool of descriptors a new person may be drawn from is empty and why the one person the last volume added was not new.**

## VOLUME 10, BATCH 0001 — Chapters 0451 to 0460, days 451 to 460, and this block wins over every block above it

**Ten chapters are on disk. They are the first ten of Volume 10 and the first ten of the volume's first batch, and they were written from `outline/batches/volume-10-batch-0001.md`, which EXISTS and which this batch read before it read anything else, and which was not reconstructed and not created and not written after the ten chapters were on disk.** The count across the ten written volumes is now fifty each and **460 in all.** The full record, with every figure and its definition, is `state/batch-summaries/volume-10-batch-0001.md`, and nothing here restates it at less than its length.

### The ten, one line each, in chapter order

- **0451 — a Thursday, the hundred and thirty-sixth day of the Bare Month.** A board comes down off the outside wall of a store at one end of a market, goes into a trough, is washed, and goes back up on its two nails with the figures of the two hundred and twenty-fourth day of this flood on it and nothing else on it. A man of about twenty-seven says four sentences about the wall above it that he has decided not to wash, and nobody agrees with him and nobody disagrees with him out loud. **It is the most dangerous page in the volume's range and it prints none of the four round figures that fall on it, and it does not print the two hundred and twenty-six days either, and it says the habit in the habit's own anchor, every morning since the seventh week of this flood. The satchel is not on this page and the board is stale and is not printed as blank.**
- **0452 — a Friday, the hundred and thirty-seventh.** The man of about thirty-one puts a satchel with a flap on a table in the room over a market and goes back down the stair and is not thanked for carrying it. The flap stays shut for four hours. The man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board asks the question that is in the room and nobody thanks him for asking it. **The register is on its shelf, the second slate is behind it with a date on it and nothing under the date, and it is not picked up, not turned over and not written on.**
- **0453 — a Saturday, the hundred and thirty-eighth.** Weather off the low ground. A barrow of stall boards goes up the market twice with Adrian Vale's hands on it and nobody speaks to him and nobody asks him anything. The man of about thirty-four asks the woman who walked a market on two feet which board comes off first and she answers correctly and in the order and goes back to work, and nobody says she is good at it and nobody asks her why she stopped. **She is given no age, no descriptor, no number and no name.**
- **0454 — a Sunday, the hundred and thirty-ninth.** The satchel goes round the room in three hands and comes back to the table in the fourth and nobody opens it. Adrian Vale has his hands on it and reads nothing and neither he nor the woman who walked a market speaks to the other. The man of about thirty-four says the thing that is wrong with a page in twelve words and nobody improves on it and nobody thanks him. **His own piece of chalk is in his own inside breast pocket and has not been taken out since the forty-third day of the Short Month, which is day two hundred and ninety-eight of this flood, and the last beat is a hand going flat on a table and staying there.**
- **0455 — a Monday, the hundred and fortieth.** The one-place sheet is read out in the local language in daylight with a date in chalk on the door in front of about nine people. A heading, and ONE place ruled under it, and a figure in that one place, and the reading stops at that one place long enough for the people at the back to hear it. **The reader says out loud what it is for before anybody asks, the figure cannot be checked from where the sheet came, and the finding made in a mouth is that nobody in that room knows yet whether that figure and this city's own figure are the same kind of thing. The reader is not told either.**
- **0456 — a Tuesday, the hundred and forty-first.** The man of about fifty-seven reads the figure out loud because it is in front of him and nobody asks him about it. A length of new rope comes off the back of his chair and he puts it back and nobody in that room says one word about it. The fence is spelled out as sixteen willow posts and eleven withies bound with the same cord and nobody is asked where it is and nobody tells him. **The room's not-noticing is the chapter and the frame is used once.**
- **0457 — a Wednesday, the hundred and forty-second.** The boards of a salt wharf at the bottom of this city, the light on the water and not on a wall. The man of about thirty-eight is right in about four seconds about what the sheet is a count of and is thanked by nobody and argued with by nobody. He sends about four hundred and forty in his return and the second set of notches on the tally-board in his coat is not a figure and is not on a page. **The chapter says once, in plain narration, that there is no eighth notice in this flood and there is not going to be one, and it is not reported to a room, and there is no instrument in this flood to report anything to anybody in advance of anything.**
- **0458 — a Thursday, the hundred and forty-third.** The man of about twenty-seven puts the rag in Adrian Vale's hands without a word and goes back inside the store. He does not teach him and does not stop him and does not say one word about it. Adrian says seven words, to the trough and not to anybody: that there is a number under the water. Nobody takes it up and nobody asks him a second question. **He performs nothing, nobody offers him anything and he asks for nothing, and the chapter does not say he was right and does not say what the figures of the two hundred and twenty-fourth day are a count of.**
- **0459 — a Friday, the hundred and forty-fourth.** Two women come up nine steps with an empty tin in their two hands and the two tins are not each other's tin. The woman who walked a market says the one thing in that room that nobody else is saying, and it is about stopping, and nobody asks her why she stopped. The woman of about sixty-nine does not ask a fourth question and nobody in that room remarks on it. **This is the only chapter of the fifty that says nine steps, and it says it four times, and the not-remarking is the chapter.**
- **0460 — a Saturday, the hundred and forty-fifth.** The sheet goes round that market in four hands so that four people can see it, and it comes back with a crease in it that is not the crease it went out with. Nobody says who folded it and nobody says it was folded wrongly. A woman asked the market what it is a count of and nobody answered her, and the chapter does not say what it is a count of. **The last beat is a board going back up a wall with the figures of the two hundred and twenty-fourth day of this flood on it and nothing else on it.**

### What the ten changed, and what they did not

**They put a person on a page properly, and the instrument was a question she could answer, and it is the volume's whole cast decision executed on day 453.** **They put a number on a page in front of about nine people for the first time in this flood, and the number is on Chapter 0455 and is about something this city also counts.** **They did not write a notice, did not trace the mark that came back out of place, did not ask the man of about fifty-seven where the fence is, did not give the woman who walked a market a number, did not attach a descriptor to anybody, did not open the register, did not touch the second slate, did not put anything on the store's board, and did not let anybody get stronger.** The ten Bare-Month ordinals resolve at ten of ten, the ten weekdays resolve at ten of ten against the run from day 1 being a Tuesday, the count of paragraphs carrying neither speaker marker — a paragraph in the first person carrying neither a quotation mark nor a bold mark and not inside a speech opened and closed around it, the unrestricted reading returning all 127 paragraphs and being a figure about composition and not a fault — is zero in all ten, the longest bolded turn is eighty words and no turn is over ninety and none is over the hard ceiling of ninety-five, and the ten files carry no panel at all.

**AND THE THREE THINGS THIS BATCH FOUND ABOUT ITS OWN BRIEFING, published here because a chapter-summaries file that carries a finding about a plan is a file a later phase will read as though something had happened in the ten chapters, and all three are about the cards and not about the pages.** `outline/batches/volume-10-batch-0001.md` exists and is 149 lines and holds ten cards. **Card 7 directs a narration sentence calling this the fourth notice, and the volume's own inventory carries seven notices and no eighth; Chapter 0457 said the sentence the plan's table carries, in plain narration, once, and the card's number was not used. Card 5 calls the hundredth day of that month a Tuesday and it is a Wednesday on the founding run; the better way to keep a prohibition on an ordinal is not to print the ordinal, and that day is at zero across the ten files. And Cards 2 and 5 direct a prohibition at the man of about thirty-nine on the ground that he was asked what the column is for on day 357, and the man asked on day 357 is the man of about thirty-eight at the salt wharf, and the two are not the same man; the prohibition holds under either reading because neither man is asked what any column is for in any of the ten.**

**AND THE ONE THING NO FILE THIS BATCH WROTE MAY PRINT: the name of Volume 09 and the words the man of about thirty-four says in Chapter 0428; the volume's one rule, said on day 438; the answer spoken in Chapter 0357; the refusal spoken in Chapter 0393, whose six words also stand at `bible/power-system.md:1684` and at `outline/volume-08.md:208`; and the day-154 sentence. NAMED WHERE EACH OF THEM IS, PRINTED NONE OF THEM, NO ABSENCE CERTIFIED. The hold is on this batch's own files and not on where a string stands, and a plan is not a page and a permanent witness section is not a page either.**

**AND WHAT THIS BATCH DID NOT DO, which is the half of a record that matters most: it created no next phase of any kind.** No continuation directory, no review prompt, no close prompt, no second Volume 10 batch prompt, no `workspace/volume-10/batch-0002`, no `outline/batches/volume-10-batch-0002.md`, and nothing at all for Volume 11. `outline/volume-10.md` was not edited, no card was added or removed or retitled, no day was moved, no descriptor was taken, no decision was taken, `bible/power-system.md` was not opened, `outline/ending.md` was not opened, and no controller file was touched.

## Volume 10, Batch 0002 — Chapters 0461 to 0470, days 461 to 470

**Ten chapters on disk, days 461 to 470, one chapter to one day, and there is no double day. The chapter number is the day number. The run was recomputed from day 1 being a Tuesday: 461 Sunday, 462 Monday, 463 Tuesday, 464 Wednesday, 465 Thursday, 466 Friday, 467 Saturday, 468 Sunday, 469 Monday, 470 Tuesday, and the Bare Month days 146 to 155, the ordinal being the day less 315. No chapter uses an ordinal for a month. The full record of the ten is `state/batch-summaries/volume-10-batch-0002.md`, the cards are `outline/batches/volume-10-batch-0002.md`, and the ten Bare-Month ordinals and the ten weekdays both resolve at ten of ten.**

**Chapter 0461 — The Light Is On The Water And There Is No Wall On That Wharf.** Day 461, a Sunday, the hundred and forty-sixth. The boards of the salt wharf at the bottom of that city, wind off the water from the south-west, the seventh hour. **Adrian Vale has both hands on the frame of a barrow and goes up and down those boards with a sack on his knees all morning, and nobody on that wharf asks him anything, nobody decides not to ask him anything, and nobody is standing about waiting for him to be useful.** The man of about thirty-eight at the salt wharf says **about four hundred and forty** out loud to nobody, the way he says it four times a year, and it is cut into a strip of clear board about as long as a cart that has nothing else on it at all and has been lying on those boards since the Friday of the round that came up the road a week before. He has two sets of notches on the board in the front of his coat and says so out loud, and does not say what the second set is, and nobody has asked him. Adrian counts what he can see twice and what the man says, finds the two are not the same number, and has no way of putting the second beside the first and does not know the second is a number. He says one thing about the boards running back empty and the man on the block does not answer it. Change: the protagonist is on a page with his hands on something and is not being asked for anything, and a man beside him keeps two figures and publishes one of them, and neither of them is thanked.

**Chapter 0462 — Two Men In One Room And Neither Of Them Is Asked.** Day 462, a Monday, the hundred and forty-seventh, about the tenth hour, the room over a market. The man of about fifty-seven has his hands flat on a bench and the length of new rope is over the back of his chair where it has been every morning of this flood, and **nobody in that room says one word about it.** A man of about sixty-three comes in out of a wet ditch with the mud to the knee, says four words, and goes out again. The man of about fifty-seven says it was **sixteen willow posts and eleven withies bound with the same cord, and a man came up the shut road with a handcart and cut them, and he was not sent, and he has never asked him why and is not going to** — and he is right about most of what he says, and nobody agrees with him and nobody disagrees with him out loud. The register-keeper says one thing about a shelf behind the table in that room with a date on it and nothing under the date. **Nobody asks him where the fence is and nobody tells him, and he walks out of that room with his own two hands and the rope stays on the back of his chair where it was all morning, and at the stair he stops and looks back at about four people in that room and does not say anything.** Change: two men in one room on two sides of a line that neither of them has ever been asked about, and the not-asking is carried by the man who is right about most of what he is saying and gets no reply.

**Chapter 0463 — One Of The Two Is A Count Of Places And The Other Is A Count Of People.** Day 463, a Tuesday, the hundred and forty-eighth, the ninth hour, the room over a market, in daylight, about nine people and four benches, and a stool carried up the stair at the back because there are more of them than there are benches. **The batch's discovery, and it is a word and not a person.** The woman of about fifty-two keeps both hands on the edge of the table and neither of them is on the paper, and says it once and does not say it again: **that the paper is a count of places and what this city keeps is a count of people, and that the two are not going to match and neither is wrong, and that the whole of the difference between them is a unit, and a unit is a decision somebody has made about what a thing is, and nobody in that room was asked about that decision and everybody in that room is living inside it.** She says before she starts that she is not going to be thanked, and nobody thanks her, and nobody improves on it, and the register-keeper puts both hands flat on the table on either side of the sheet and says not one word about the difference. The man of about thirty-four says that a figure about places is a figure about places wherever it happens to be written, and that goes round the room and nobody stops it. Change: the reason two figures about the same ground do not match is a word, and it is in a room with a date on the door, and this city has no instrument for checking a reason.

**Chapter 0464 — A Slate On A Trestle And A Man Who Is Not The One Counting.** Day 464, a Wednesday, the hundred and forty-ninth, that market, flat light off the low ground, the seventh hour. The store's board comes down off its two nails into Adrian Vale's hands and he stands it on its edge, and the man of about twenty-seven comes out, looks at where he would have stood it, goes past him, fills the bucket and washes the board himself and says not one word. A man at a trestle has a slate propped against a basket and has been marking it since before the light, and it is about his own stall and his own morning and is nothing else. The man of about thirty-nine reads it from where he is standing without touching it and says about nine words — that it is a count of how many times he has had somebody at that stall, in a place where anybody can put their own morning on top of it — and the man says that is only his stall, and the man of about thirty-nine says the two sentences are not the same sentence. **Nobody thanked him and the chapter does not say he was right.** The trough is running; the man of about twenty-seven asks the one question he asks anybody that morning, which is where the trough stands; Adrian moves it a hand's width with his foot, puts his hand flat on the stone, and says one sentence, and is not answered and is not thanked. Change: three working figures stand in one market at the same hour and none of them is asked anything about a figure.

**Chapter 0465 — Sixteen Posts And Eleven Withies And A Mallet With Its Head Bound In Cord.** Day 465, a Thursday, the hundred and fiftieth, one end of that market, the ninth hour. A man of about sixty brings a handcart in off the flags with a hazel mallet lying in it whose head is bound in cord and a coil of rope over the side, stands it against a wall, and takes his hands off it. The man of about thirty-four says he is not selling anything, and the man says he is not and did not come to be asked what is in the cart, and that what is in it is a mallet and a rope and he could not sell either. He turns the mallet over in his hands and explains why he bound the head of it nine years ago. A woman with a stall says he is a long way from a fence to be talking about a mallet, and **nobody improves on that either, and nobody asks him why he put it up and nobody asks him whose it was, and he does not say, and the woman with a stall two along looks at the cord round the head of the mallet the whole time he is saying the count and does not look at the man once, and puts her hand flat on the edge of her own board afterwards, and that is the whole of anybody's interest in the cart, and he hears none of it.** He gives the count because he cut the wood: **sixteen willow posts and eleven withies, the same cord, the eleventh withy the one that took longest because the wood was in the wet, and about four feet that he did not measure and neither did anybody else.** Change: the man who built the thing is standing in a market where a number on a board is about to matter, and nobody connects him to the fence and he does not offer to be connected.

**Chapter 0466 — Eleven Miles Of Flats And A Number That Is A Number Of Places.** Day 466, a Friday, the hundred and fifty-first, the sixth hour to the fourth, eleven miles of flats, wind out of the north across open ground with no hill on it. Adrian Vale walks with a slate in his left hand and a hazel stick in his right, **neither of them his — the slate is the register-keeper's own working slate, taken off the bench in the room over a market without asking, and the stick is off a gate he walked past, and the chapter says both in its first paragraph and puts the slate back on that bench at the fourth hour with the marks on it and the cold still on his hands; it is not the second slate on the shelf behind that bench, which is not picked up, not turned over and not written on**, and puts a stroke on the slate for every place there is anybody at, and does not knock and does not stop, **and says out loud that a door is a thing and a person is a thing and he has been putting down one mark for both of them since the sixth hour and he does not know which of the two he has been counting.** The ground is soft where the water stood and firm where it did not. A channel across those flats has been dry since the hundred and ninety-sixth day of this flood and there is wet silt in the bottom of it and it is the same silt on every one of those eleven miles. A man of about sixty-four is cutting a ditch that is not his, about nine hundred paces off another, and says four words to the ground, and Adrian tells him he did not ask whose it was and is not going to. He comes back with **about four hundred and forty** marks on the slate, and there is a man at the bottom of this city who sends about four hundred and forty up a road four times a year, and **nobody in this city has said out loud whether those are the same figure.** The chapter says in plain narration that what is on the slate is a count of places and that this city keeps a count of people, and that there is no instrument in four hundred miles that can turn one into the other. Change: the volume's count is made by hand in weather, comes out as the wrong kind of number, and there is no form in four hundred miles on which anybody could write down that they had failed to.

**Chapter 0467 — Four Things He Has Carried Up And Down A Road And Has Not Opened.** Day 467, a Saturday, the hundred and fifty-second, the ninth hour, the room over a market and then the market below. A man of about thirty-one has his hand flat on the flap of a satchel on a table and the flap is down, and nothing in that room is going to be on that table with the flap up on a Saturday. A man at the foot of the stair, who came up on his own, asks what is in it, and he answers that there is a thing in it that has been read out to nine people in this room and four in a market and that he has not opened it, and that he would like nobody to thank him for that. He names what he has carried: **five notices up that road once in a coat on his own back before any of them were keeping the register and he has never opened one of them; a return in a satchel like this one; the last sheet that came up that road; and this satchel, on the day after this one came up** — four things in three volumes, none of them opened. He says the one thing he minds: that a thing in a flap on a table is a thing four people know about and a thing in a flap on a man's back is a thing nobody knows about, and that nobody has asked him about it once this flood. The register-keeper says one sentence about the difference and neither of them hears the other. Change: a man for whom a document has always been a thing to carry and never a thing to know, and two people in one room saying the same thing from opposite ends of it.

**Chapter 0468 — A Number Said Out Loud In A Room And Not Written Down.** Day 468, a Sunday, the hundred and fifty-third, the tenth hour, the room over a market, about nine people. The register-keeper puts her own slate on the shelf behind the register and takes it off again in the same hand, and **no other hand in that room goes near that shelf.** The register is not opened, the second slate is not picked up, not turned over and not written on, and the two lines in the book are not read out. The man of about thirty-four's hand goes to his breast pocket about once a minute and comes back out with nothing in it, and the forty-third day of the Short Month, which is the two hundred and ninety-eighth day of this flood, is printed at full length. The woman of about fifty-two is on the bench and is asked nothing. **The man of about thirty-nine says two hundred and six out loud because he knows it to the one and would like somebody in the room to have a number in their head that is right, and she says that she is not going to write it down, and gives a reason about a hand and not about a board: a number in my hand is a number anybody can carry to anybody.** Nobody thanked him and nobody asked her why the third column is empty. Change: a woman hears a right figure said out loud in her room and will not put it on a shelf, and about four of the people who heard it have already begun to work out what it would be worth to the man who said it if it were ever on a wall somewhere.

**Chapter 0469 — A Bar He Put In And A Hurdle He Hung On It.** Day 469, a Monday, the hundred and fifty-fourth, about the fourth hour, the near gate. A man of about fifty-nine is on a box with both hands on a hurdle, cutting a lashing with a clasp knife that is not sharp enough, and the gate stands open behind him on a bar he put in nine years ago, with about four people under it where he can see them and the rest out of sight past the turn. He says he has walked that road four times in nine years, that it is four hundred miles, that he has never walked it for anybody and has never been asked to, and that he would like the record left blank anyway. A man under the gate says he could go again in the spring, and he says there is no spring and the road is shut and he has been to the end of it and back four times and the part he has not got to is the part he has not got to. **Nobody improved on it, and nobody asked him about anybody, and he did not talk about anybody, and a man on the far side of that gate went back to the end of the lashing he had been holding and said nothing and two of the others found something to do with their hands, and whatever they understood about the road on that gate was understood to nobody else and he did not know it.** He gets the hurdle back on its hooks and stands at the end of a row of boards in the market and says not one word. Change: the man who has walked the road this volume's figure is about, four times, is on a page at a gate in this city, and is not asked about the three and does not mention them, and the not-mentioning is not remarked on by anybody in the chapter.

**Chapter 0470 — A Question Asked Out Loud In A Room And Nobody Answers It.** Day 470, a Tuesday, the hundred and fifty-fifth, the ninth hour, the room over a market, in daylight, the date in chalk on the door, about nine people, none of them sent for. The one-place sheet is on the table with its heading and its one place ruled under it and its figure in that one place, and the register-keeper's hand is flat on it. **The man of about thirty-nine puts his own board against the leg of the table to get his hand free and says three words: put it on the board.** He means the store's board on the outside wall, in chalk, in the open, where a man walking the length of that market can stop and read it without anybody asking him to. **Nobody in that room answers him.** It goes quiet the way a room goes quiet when about nine people have worked out the same thing and none of them is going to be the one who says it. The woman of about fifty-two, who had said the true thing about that paper in that room on a Tuesday morning, sits perfectly still and nobody turned round to look at her, and that was a kindness and not an accident. **The register-keeper says one sentence, to him and not to the room: that there is no instrument in four hundred miles for asking a room to be asked anything in advance of anything.** He does not argue, and she does not decide anything, and she says she would like everybody in that room to go home knowing there is one question in this city that has been asked out loud in daylight with a date on the door and that nobody has answered, including her and including him. The man of about thirty-nine picks his own board up off the leg of the table and carries it out himself and does not look at anybody on his way to the stair, and the sheet goes off the table in one pair of hands at the eleventh hour, unopened, and stays on this side of the water where it is. Change: the batch ends with a question on a table and nothing at all on a wall, and nobody answered it, and nobody said out loud that nobody had.

**AND THE THINGS THIS BATCH FOUND ABOUT ITS OWN BRIEFING, published here because a chapter-summaries file that carries a finding about a plan reads as though something had happened in the ten chapters.** `outline/batches/volume-10-batch-0002.md` did not exist when this batch began and this batch wrote it, before the first chapter file, from `outline/volume-10.md` alone, and the Volume 10 outline phase and the Batch 0001 card file each say in their own words that a batch is not to write one. **The file is a plan, it decides nothing, and it says so at the top of itself, and it is therefore not a reconstruction.** The plan's escalation entry for day 466 says three mornings and its own location list says days 466, 496 and 497, and Chapter 0466 satisfies both under a reading the batch published rather than a decision it took. The one round-figure trap in the batch falls on day 470 and Chapter 0470 prints no round figure and no elapsed count of any kind. **And the batch's own record listed Chapter 0461 as naming the store's board on a first pass and Chapter 0461 does not name it — those ten occurrences are wharf planks — and the word-by-word reading corrected it before the record was written, and a row that says a chapter names an object it does not name is a row a later phase inherits as fact.**

**AND THE FIGURES, with the definitions, and no target set on any of them.** The bolded share on the published definition: 11.33, 21.73, 16.73, 13.49, 22.28, 10.10, 23.97, 18.35, 18.22 and 11.99, a range of 10.10 to 23.97 and an unweighted mean of 16.82 on 8,986 words, and **both ends of that range sit inside the envelope the last two volumes have published, and the lowest is above Volume 09's lowest and the highest is above Volume 09's highest, which is the opposite of the finding in the batch before this one and the reason is that eight of these ten have a marked voice in a room being argued with.** The count of paragraphs carrying neither speaker marker is zero in all ten, the unrestricted reading returning all 132 paragraphs. ## VOLUME 10, BATCH 0002 — REVIEW REPAIR PASS, and this block is at the foot of this file and it wins over every block above it

**A reviewer returned six blocking and three moderate findings on Chapters 0461 to 0470. Five chapters are unchanged on the page, five changed by a merge or a coda rewrite, and the summaries above are corrected where their chapter changed. No chapter was restarted and no day moved.**

**THE CHAPTERS THAT CHANGED, and what changed in each, and the reason in one line:** **0462**, six words removed from a sentence that named the book it is in. **0464**, one word, *had been* for *have been*, and two single-sentence narration paragraphs merged into one. **0465**, two single-sentence narration paragraphs merged into one, and the closing paragraph rewritten so that the crowd's understanding arrives through the woman at the stall looking at the cord on the mallet's head and not at the man, instead of through the *about four people … had worked out since … / not one of those four* construction. **0467**, two single-sentence narration paragraphs merged into one. **0469**, the closing paragraph rewritten on the same principle, the interest now arriving through a man on the far side of the gate going back to his lashing and saying nothing. **NONE OF THE TEN LOST AN OBJECT, A COUNT, A DAY, A FIGURE, A SPEECH OR A PLOT TURN, and none of the five changed chapters has a different Change: line from the one published above.**

**THE ONE CHAPTER SUMMARY THAT WAS CARRYING A FALSE FACT, and it is the finding that mattered most: Chapter 0466's entry said the slate and the stick were *neither of them given to him by anybody*, which was true and was hiding the load-bearing fact. It is corrected above to say that the slate is the register-keeper's own working slate off her bench in the room over a market, taken without asking and put back on that bench at the fourth hour, and that it is not the second slate on the shelf behind that bench.**

**AND THE TWO FIGURES ANY READER OF THESE SUMMARIES SHOULD HOLD: the batch is 9,029 words in 129 paragraphs across ten chapters, days 461 to 470, one chapter to one day, and the ten Bare-Month ordinals are the hundred and forty-sixth to the hundred and fifty-fifth. The bolded share runs 10.10 to 23.97 with both ends inside the envelope the last two volumes published, and the mean is a consequence and not a plan.**

## VOLUME 10, BATCH 0003 — Chapters 0471 to 0480, days 471 to 480, and NINE CHAPTERS WERE WRITTEN

**A collision in this batch's own briefing is published before anything else. It was told to write ten chapters, 0471 through 0480 with no gap, and it was told that Chapter 0475 is not its, that it may not write it, and that no chapter of its ten may decide anything. Both sentences are binding and they cannot both be satisfied and the more specific one won: NINE chapters were written, 0471, 0472, 0473, 0474, 0476, 0477, 0478, 0479 and 0480, on nine of the ten days in the span. `chapters/volume-10/chapter-0475.md` DOES NOT EXIST. `outline/volume-10.md` §8 is that chapter and it is a decision. The day run has no gap in it and the file is absent, and the handoff prompt names this in its own words. There is no card file for this batch under `outline/` and none was written, per §21.**

**Chapter 0471 — The Wind Comes Through The Store Door.** Day 471, a Wednesday, the hundred and fifty-sixth, about the seventh hour, that market, with the wind up off the low ground and in through the open door of the store. Adrian Vale has both hands on the frame of a barrow of stall boards, and the wind takes a string two stalls along and a board comes off it and he gets it flat on the barrow and the stone stays on the foot of the string on the flags. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall has a piece of chalk in his own inside breast pocket that he has not taken out since the forty-third day of the Short Month, which is the two hundred and ninety-eighth day of this flood, and he says a price said out loud and a price chalked on a board are not the same animal, and nobody thanks him and nobody says he was right. A price is said out loud for the rest of that day and a woman repeats it to a man with a basket. The man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living says, to the wind, that the road out is shut, that there are three barrows on it four hundred miles up, and that not one man in that market has been out to the gate to look. Change: a board that has been on a string for nine years is off it, and a figure has gone into the air.

**Chapter 0472 — A Method That Has Nobody To Do The First Part.** Day 472, a Thursday, the hundred and fifty-seventh, the tenth hour, the room over a market. The one-place sheet is on the table in one pair of hands that carried it in and went straight back down the stair. A man at the back who gave his name to nobody works a method out on his fingers and gets as far as the first part of it, which is knowing where a man is to start, and stops. **The man of about thirty-four says out loud that he would go and make the other number himself, with a slate, this week, asking nobody for anything, and that he is not going to say it a second time in that room. Nobody accepts it and nobody refuses it.** The man of about thirty-one says there is no day the round comes on, and that seven sheets have gone up that road in this flood as notices and there is no eighth one, and that he carried five of the seven on his own back and has not opened one of them. Change: a man has offered himself in a room and the room did not answer him, and he does not offer again.

**Chapter 0473 — A Column Empty On Purpose And A Column Empty.** Day 473, a Friday, the hundred and fifty-eighth, the ninth hour, the room over a market. The register-keeper turns her own working slate over in front of everybody, sets it on the table and puts her hand on the back of it. The register is on the shelf behind the bench and is not open and no hand but hers goes near it; the second slate is on the shelf behind it with a date on it and nothing under the date and is not picked up, not turned over and not written on. **The discovery is hers and it is about a book: a column empty on purpose and a column empty because there is nothing to put in it are two different things, and this room has been treating them as one, and the reason nobody has found that out is that she has never had a figure to put in it, and on the morning she has one the second column stops being a thing she is protecting and becomes a thing she is filling. Nobody thanks her, nobody improves on it, and the word the volume turns on is not in the chapter.** Change: the room's understanding of the book it has been keeping, and nothing written in anything.

**Chapter 0474 — Two Men With Boards In One Room.** Day 474, a Saturday, the hundred and fifty-ninth, the tenth hour, the room over a market. The man of about thirty-four comes up the stair with his own board under his arm for the first time in this flood and stands it against the wall. The man of about thirty-nine says that he has carried a board in four markets for nine years and has never put it in a room and has never seen another man's board in a room, and does not know what a room does with two. **The two men are not compared in this chapter, in any mouth, and no mouth says which board is the better one.** The man of about thirty-four says nothing at all. The man of about thirty-nine turns his own board face-in against his coat, goes down the stair, and does not come back up it that day. Change: two men who have been the same argument in the same market for nine years are in one room and neither says the thing, and one of them leaves.

**Chapter 0476 — The Rag Comes Back Off Her Board.** Day 476, a Monday, the hundred and sixty-first, the seventh hour, that market. A wet rag that belongs on the store's stone lip is lying on the corner of a woman's stall board with the water running off it, and Adrian Vale takes it off her board with a hand at each end, wrings it out over the trough and puts it on the stone lip. **A woman with a stall, who has no descriptor and is given none, picks a stub of chalk out of the box on the step and says the cost out loud in a market: a figure on a board in the open can be taken off the board by the man who owns it, and she has taken about forty of them off in nine years, and a figure on a wall in chalk cannot be taken off by anybody at all, and the difference between those two is her whole trade and it is about to stop being hers to have. Nobody thanks her, nobody improves on it, and the thing she was heard to say in that room on the Sunday is referred to once and not narrated and not re-argued.** Change: the cost of a figure going on a wall has been named out loud by the person who pays it.

**Chapter 0477 — A Man Who Knocks At Doors For A Living.** Day 477, a Tuesday, the hundred and sixty-second, the tenth hour, the room over a market. The man of about thirty-one puts a satchel with the flap down on the bench and leaves it there for the whole morning, flap down, unopened. He names what he has carried up that road — five notices on his own back, a return in a satchel, a sheet on the round and the one on the round after it, six things — and says he has not opened one of them and is not going to be praised for that, because a man who has not opened a thing has not done anything. Then he says the thing the chapter is for: **he has knocked at more doors in this flood than anybody in this city and nobody has ever asked him a single thing while he was doing it, and the reason is not that he is no use, it is that a man who knocks has already come to the door, and there is nobody in four hundred miles who can go out to a door to ask whether it would be all right.** The register-keeper says one sentence about her own shelf and will not compare the two in front of about nine people. Nobody asks him what he knocked on, or how many, or what he found out. He picks the satchel up at the eleventh hour and goes down the stair. Change: a man who has carried six things and opened none has put the sixth on a bench for a morning and taken it up again, and nobody asked him why.

**Chapter 0478 — He Looks At The Rope And Nobody Asks.** Day 478, a Wednesday, the hundred and sixty-third, the ninth hour, the room over a market. The man of about fifty-seven sits at the end of the bench with his hands flat on it and a length of new rope over the back of his chair, and it is not cut, not taken up, does not fall, and no hand in the chapter lifts it. He says the count — sixteen willow posts and eleven withies, the withies bound with the same cord, a man who cut them with a handcart and was not sent, about forty years and never once asked where the whole of it stands. He is right about most of what he says and nobody agrees with him and nobody disagrees with him and **the chapter does not say he was right and nobody thanks him.** **The man of about thirty-nine, who asked a room on a Tuesday to put a figure on a wall and got nothing, comes into that room on this day and asks nothing at all, and looks at the rope, and neither of the two men says one word.** Nobody asks him where the fence is and nobody tells him and whether the fence stands between the right two things is not settled and is not asked about. Change: a reversal — the man who asked does not ask.

**Chapter 0479 — A Stone Under The Far End Of The Trough.** Day 479, a Thursday, the hundred and sixty-fourth, the seventh hour to the eleventh, that market. **Adrian Vale puts his shoulder under the far end of the trough, lifts it a hand's width, works a flat stone out of the store wall with his foot and lets the trough down onto it, and the water goes level and stops instead of walking across the flags. Nobody asked him what he was doing, nobody in that market decided not to ask him, and nobody stood about waiting to see whether he would be any use to anybody.** The man of about twenty-seven comes out with the rag over his shoulder, looks at the stone, goes back inside and shuts the door without a word. The man of about thirty-four puts his string back up on his post and the board back on the string and the stone on the foot of it on dry flags, and says three sentences: that a board on a string wants dry flags under the foot of it, that he is not saying a word to anybody about the trough, and that the way you tell somebody something in this city is to wait until you already know and then say it, and that is not the same. Nobody thanks him and nobody says he was right. Change: a trough is on a stone, the flags are dry, and a board that came off a string has gone back up on it.

**Chapter 0480 — The Second Morning On The Flats.** Day 480, a Friday, the hundred and sixty-fifth, the sixth hour to the fourth, eleven miles of flats and then the bench in the room over a market. **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE CARRIES THE REGISTER-KEEPER'S OWN WORKING SLATE, TAKEN OFF HER BENCH IN THE SIXTH HOUR WITHOUT ASKING AND PUT BACK ON THAT SAME BENCH AT THE FOURTH HOUR, AND THE CHAPTER KEEPS IT APART FROM THE SECOND SLATE IN ONE SENTENCE: that one is on the shelf behind the bench with a date on it and nothing under the date, and it is not picked up, not turned over and not written on, and no hand in that room but hers goes near that shelf.** Adrian Vale is not in this chapter. He puts one stroke on the slate for every place there was a door at, the same mark he would have put there if nobody had ever heard of knocking, and then he knocks. He says it to the wind twice: **that a man who knocks gets a different figure from a man who does not, and the difference is in about ten places where he knocked and nobody came, and he cannot tell whether anybody was in any of them; and that both of these are a count of places, neither is a count of people, there is nothing in four hundred miles that will turn one into the other, and he has two different numbers about the same ground made by two careful men and no way of knowing which is right and he is not going to pick one.** He comes back at the fourth hour and the first set of marks is still on the slate, and the second set is down the other side of the same face, and neither is rubbed out: **about four hundred and forty on the first and about four hundred and thirty on the second, and the second is lower and he says the difference is in the places where he knocked and nobody came.** The register is not opened, the second column holds nothing, the third column is empty on purpose and is going to stay empty on purpose. Change: two counts of the same eleven miles now stand on one slate with neither rubbed out, they do not match, the difference is a knock, and nobody in this city can say which is right.

**AND THE FIGURES, with the definitions, and no target set on any of them.** The bolded share on the published definition: 13.22, 18.00, 15.71, 14.90, 17.02, 22.55, 23.17, 19.67 and 14.64, a range of 13.22 to 23.17, an unweighted mean of 17.65 and a weighted mean of 17.53 on 8,102 words in 110 paragraphs. **The low end sits inside the envelopes Volume 08 and Volume 09 published and the high end sits 0.35 above Volume 09's 22.82, and both figures are published and neither is rounded toward the envelope.** The count of paragraphs carrying neither speaker marker is zero in all nine, and the unrestricted reading returns the 77 plain narration paragraphs, and 110 is the total paragraph count and not this reading's figure. A paragraph carrying a bold mark and no quotation mark is zero in all nine and no exchange carries two bold marks. The exchange is twelve plain speeches and seven answered, fifty-eight point three per cent. The longest bolded turn is ninety-three words in Chapter 0476 and no turn is over ninety or over the hard ceiling of ninety-five.
