## VOLUME 10, BATCH 0004 — Chapters 0481 to 0490, days 481 to 490, ten chapters

**Ten chapters were written, 0481 through 0490, on ten days, one chapter to one day, no double day, and `chapters/volume-10/chapter-0475.md` is still not on disk and is not any batch's. There is no card file for this batch under `outline/` and none was written, per `outline/volume-10.md` §21; the ten cards are at the head of `state/batch-summaries/volume-10-batch-0004.md` and were written before `chapter-0481.md` existed.**

**Chapter 0481 — The Bucket Goes Up The Row Twice.** Day 481, a Saturday, the hundred and sixty-sixth, the sixth hour, that market. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall goes up the row with the store's bucket and comes back with it full and goes up again and comes back again, and puts both loads in the trough against the wall under the store's board, which has the figures of the two hundred and twenty-fourth day of this flood on it and nothing else, and the wall above the board is not washed. **The woman at a stall two along, who has no descriptor and is given none, has been carrying that water up that row in that bucket at the sixth hour for the whole of this flood and nobody has asked her to and nobody has thanked her, and on this morning she comes out with her own pail, gets as far as the edge of her own board, looks at the trough, stops, and puts the pail down under her board with its mouth up.** He says a bucket is a thing anybody can pick up and a trough is a thing anybody can fill and neither is a favour, and would rather nobody said a word about it, and in particular would rather the man who washes that board did not find out from her. Change: a pail is not carried up that row again, and the trough is filled by a man nobody asked, and nobody on that row has ever said out loud that it was carried up it at all.

**Chapter 0482 — A Door That Will Not Shut.** Day 482, a Sunday, the hundred and sixty-seventh, the ninth hour, the room over a market, the hundred and sixty-seventh in chalk on the door and no name on it, about nine people. The door will not shut: the damp has come up the stair and swollen the bottom rail until it stands a hand's width proud of its own frame. **Adrian Vale gets a bar off a leaning pole, works it into the gap, and prises a piece of slate out of the top step, and sets it on the flags where the door has to lift itself over it, and the door stands open a finger's width on its own.** Nobody asks him what he is doing and nobody stops talking. The register is on its shelf with its spine to the wall and is not opened, and the second slate lies face in behind it with a day across the head of it and nothing under that day and her hands stay off it all morning. **She says a room is a thing a door makes and a date makes, and a door standing open on a piece of a step is the end of a market with a bench in it, and about four people came up those stairs who had not come up them before the water came, and she is not going to send them back down and she is not going to write their names down either.** A woman comes up that stair with a slate of her own under her arm and a tin in her other hand, stands at the back of the room with her back to the wall, is asked nothing by anybody, gives her name to nobody, and goes down the stair again. Change: a door that stands open all morning, about fourteen people where there are about nine, and one of the five who came up it written down nowhere.

**Chapter 0483 — The Back Of The Sheet.** Day 483, a Monday, the hundred and sixty-eighth, the tenth hour, the room over a market. The one-place sheet has been lying face up on that table since the morning it was read out: a heading, one ruled place under it, a figure in that one place, folded twice with a bone. **The man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living turns it over, and the back of it is not blank: there is a figure on it in a hand that is not the hand the front is written in, and the two figures are of the same kind of thing and are not the same figure, shown by their length and by the strokes not being made the same way, and there is no day and no name and no place on either side of it and nothing that says what either is a count of.** He says once, in his own mouth, that a person can say the same about two numbers that are not equal without meaning anything false by it, and that he has never had to say the same about two numbers. **Nobody thanked him for turning a sheet over, nobody argued, nobody asked who had written the other side, he did not say, the man of about forty-one in a very good coat was not in that room and was not spoken of, and the room did not go looking for anybody.** The register-keeper says the only person in four hundred miles who can say which of the two that office means is that office, that there is no day on either side of the page to say which went out first, and that she is not going to put either of them in a book. **Neither of the two figures is printed in this chapter or in any other of these ten.** Change: the office has two figures about the same kind of thing and neither can be checked from this city.

**Chapter 0484 — A Man At The End Of A Lane.** Day 484, a Tuesday, the hundred and sixty-ninth, a lane past two gates with about nine households and a wall with nine boards gone off it. A man goes up the lane with a stick putting a notch on every door-frame, the same cut on every frame, on no page and on nobody's tally. Tamsin Quill stands on the bottom step with the fold of her sheet of road showing in the inside of her coat and does not take it out, and says once, in her own mouth, to his back, that **a figure that says how many of something there are is the plainest instrument of that claim this flood has produced, and that it is plainer than a notice and plainer than a name, and that the figure is the one that counts and the name is the one that finds people.** He does not answer. He cuts seven notches and stops at the eighth frame because there is a name cut into the wood with a knife, low down, where a child could reach it and where a grown person stooping for a bucket would put a hand. **There is room above it for a notch and he does not cut the notch above it; he puts the ninth mark on the ground in front of that door instead, and the water that comes down that lane after rain takes it before he has gone the length of two houses, and he does not come back.** The register-keeper is not in that lane and the man of about twenty-seven is not in it. Change: eight notches in nine frames, a name still there, a mark gone.

**Chapter 0485 — Round A Board On The Flags.** Day 485, a Wednesday, the hundred and seventieth, the eighth hour, that market. Adrian Vale pushes a barrow of stall boards down the row and back and a woman has to lift the foot of her own board to get the wheel past. **The man of about thirty-nine who trades nineteen rates on a board comes along the same row and has to walk round the end of the barrow, and while he is walking round it he says, in about the time it takes to say it, that a figure on a wall anybody can read is a figure the first man through who disagrees with it can change, and that a man who goes out onto eleven miles of flats with a slate and knocks comes back with a different figure from a man who goes out and does not, and that there is a slate in that room with the two of them on it.** Nobody thanked him. Nobody argued and nobody improved on it. **The man of about thirty-four, two stalls along, heard all of it, said nothing, and has not offered himself to anything since the hundred and fifty-seventh day of the Bare Month; his board also carries an unfilled space where a name used to be, taken off in his own hand in about the ninth year of this flood, and about four people looked at the face of that board and not one of them looked at the space.** Then a woman with a stall asks him a question for the first time in as long as he has carried a board — whether she should say her prices out loud instead of chalking them — and he says he does not know and that there is not one person in that market who does, and she will not ask him anything else. Change: the standing offer is spent, and the man who is right cannot answer.

**Chapter 0486 — The Man Who Washes The Board.** Day 486, a Thursday, the hundred and seventy-first, the tenth hour, the room over a market, the date in chalk on the door and no name on it, about nine people and none of them sent for. **The man of about twenty-seven who washes the store's board comes up the stair with a bucket and a rag, sets the bucket on the step, folds the rag on the sill, wipes his hands down his coat and stands at the back of the room.** The register-keeper says once, in her own mouth, that nobody has ever asked him what he is doing to that board and that she is going to, and that she is asking because a figure is going on that wall and because he is the only person in this city who has been at it every morning since the seventh week of this flood, and that there is no instrument in four hundred miles that gets a person in front of a wall. **He answers, once, in his own mouth, out loud, in that room, in that light, in the local language, in four to eight words, and it is the name of this volume, and the words are on this page and in no file in this repository. Nobody thanked him for it, nobody asked him what he meant, nobody improved on it, nobody said it back to him to hear how it sounded, and not one of the nine wrote a word of it on anything.** She says a man did not catch it because she is not going to say it a second time and neither is he, and that nobody in this city is going to be able to ask him what he meant after today either. He picks the rag up and goes down the stair. Change: a man has said out loud what he has been doing to that board, and no instrument in four hundred miles will ever have told anybody which of the two things he came up that stair to do.

**Chapter 0487 — The Offer, Withdrawn.** Day 487, a Friday, the hundred and seventy-second, the ninth hour, that market. The man of about thirty-four takes his hands off his string, holds them out and turns them over, and says to the man of about thirty-one, in his own mouth, that he is not going to be the man who goes out and makes that other number, and that the reason is that **he cannot knock, and he has not knocked at a door since he was a boy in this market, and a man who sells off a board has a hand that has never been on a door, and if he went out onto those flats he would have stood in a gateway and counted and come back with the same figure the first man has and had to say so in front of about nine people.** The man of about thirty-one says that is the end of it, that he is not going to say he was right or wrong, and that the two counts on that slate were made by two men and neither of them was him. A man at the end of the row asks whether it was going to sound well, and he says it is going to sound well and is not going to be well. Nobody improved on either. **Change: the offer is withdrawn by the man who made it, for a reason nobody had thought of until he said it, and a hand is put flat on his own board and left there until it goes cold on it, and not one person on that row is going to be the one to say why.**

**Chapter 0488 — A Plank Against A Shed Door.** Day 488, a Saturday, the hundred and seventy-third, the seventh hour, the boards of a salt wharf at the bottom of this city, weather coming up off the water, a stack higher than a man. **Adrian Vale has both hands under the end of a plank going down a wet chute faster than the man above it wants, and there is a shed door standing open with its frame square to the run of the chute and its middle rail about where a plank of that length arrives. He gets one hand off the plank and one hand under the frame, lifts, and the plank goes into the gap and stops there.** Nobody on those boards asks him what he is doing, nobody stops and looks, and nobody is standing about waiting to see whether he will be any use. The man of about thirty-eight at the wharf looks, says a plank into a door frame is a thing he has seen hold and a thing he has seen not hold, that he will not say which this is until the fourth hour, and that he is not going to say anything to the man who put it there. **They take the door off its hinges, carry it across six boards and put it back on the other side of the opening, and it goes crooked and will only shut if you pull it, and that shed has not shut since, and a man has to walk round the outside of it to get at boards he wants.** The tally-board still has about four hundred and forty on it and has not been taken down and put up again, and the barrow at the end of the wharf still has nothing in it at all. Change: a shed door on the wrong side of its own opening, permanently.

**Chapter 0489 — The Shut Road, On A Sunday.** Day 489, a Sunday, the hundred and seventy-fourth, out of this city, at the top of the shut road, where the fence is sixteen willow posts and eleven withies bound with the same cord. **The man of about sixty stands at the ninth post with his hand flat on the cord; his handcart is in the lane with the bar down, with a coil of rope in it that has been used and a hazel mallet with its head bound in cord, and he touches neither.** He goes down the line counting the posts, finds that the eleventh withy has come loose at the top, lifts it, looks at the wear on the cord and on the wood, lets it go, does the same thing again with the other hand and twice more, and then takes his hands off the fence. He says it out loud, to the fence, with nobody on that road to hear it: **sixteen willow posts and eleven withies and the withies bound with the same cord, and he put it up with a handcart and nobody sent him, and the eleventh is loose and he has looked at it four times and has not bound it up again and is not going to, and there is nobody within four hundred miles of that lane who could come and tell him which two of those posts this argument is about.** The only other man in this city who has ever said those numbers out loud paid for half a holding about forty years ago, was not asked about them and has not said them again, and the two of them have never seen one another. He shoulders the cart back down the lane until the wheel comes up against the bar. Change: two men have said that fence aloud without being asked and the two counts are the same, and nobody in four hundred miles can be shown where any of the sixteen is standing.

**Chapter 0490 — A Figure Chalked On The Board.** Day 490, a Monday, the hundred and seventy-fifth, the ninth hour, that market, about nine people at that end of it. **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register has the store's own chalk out of the box on the step and writes this city's own figure on the board on the right of the old figures and a hand's width clear of them, with a stroke in the second place of it, and she does not rub anything out, and the figures of the two hundred and twenty-fourth day are on that board when she starts and when she finishes and there is no place on that board that has been wiped.** The wall above the board is not washed. About four people read it and about four of the nine who read it worked out that the two sets do not agree, and none of the nine can say which is right, and the reason none of them can is a word, and the word is not on the board. **The man of about thirty-nine comes down the row, stops, reads it, and says that the figure is a count of places and there is not one person standing in that market it is a count of, and that every person standing there is going to read it as though it were a count of them, and that is what a public figure does, and it does it in about a morning. Nobody said he was right and nobody said he was wrong and he did not take his own board down.** **THE WALL SAYS ONE THING, and it is this volume's one panel, and it is the only panel in these ten chapters.** The man of about twenty-seven, standing at the trough with the rag in his hand, refuses it in about four seconds in a handful of words: **he has washed those figures every morning since the seventh week of this flood, he has not asked anybody to check them and nobody else has either, and the reason they have stood up on that wall is not him and is not his washing, it is that he has not washed the wall above them.** Nobody improved on it and nobody thanked him and he has asked in advance not to be thanked and has not been. **No chapter of these ten connects that refusal to day 486 and no chapter of these ten makes the two sets of figures on that board the same figures.** The chalk goes back in the box and the lid comes down on it, and the board is taken down at the fourth hour and washed, and both sets come back up out of the wet exactly as clean as they went on. Change: this city's own figure is on a public wall in chalk and it stays there, and it does not agree with the other figures, and nobody can say which is right.

**AND THE FIGURES, with the definitions, and no target set on any of them.** The bolded share on the published definition: 18.19, 17.09, 21.21, 15.62, 21.81, 22.43, 23.60, 13.66, 13.39 and 14.24, a range of **13.39 to 23.60**, an unweighted mean of **18.13** and a weighted mean of **18.46** on **9,600 words** in **138 paragraphs**. **The low end sits inside the envelopes Volume 08 and Volume 09 published and the high end sits 0.78 above Volume 09's 22.82, and both figures are published and neither is rounded toward the envelope.** The count of paragraphs carrying neither speaker marker is **zero in all ten**, and the unrestricted reading returns the 71 plain narration paragraphs, and 138 is the total paragraph count and not this reading's figure. A paragraph carrying a bold mark and no quotation mark is **zero in all ten** and no exchange carries two bold marks. The exchange is **twenty-one plain speeches and fifteen answered, seventy-one point four per cent.** The longest bolded turn is **eighty-five words in Chapter 0486**, and no turn is over ninety and none is over the hard ceiling of ninety-five, and no turn was cut to make a number. Long sentences of forty words or more: **92 on reading A and 84 on reading B, and the two readings DIFFER in all ten of these files**, on a batch mean of **33.5** words to the sentence on reading A and **29.3** on reading B, longest single sentence 79. The longest run of speech paragraphs is **four in Chapter 0483, at the ceiling of four**, and two or fewer in the other nine. **The paragraph composition, published as four separate counts and never as their sum: one hundred and thirty-eight paragraphs are sixty-seven speech, twenty-one one-sentence narration lead-ins, seven free-standing one-sentence narration paragraphs, and forty-three of three or more sentences, and a paragraph of three or more sentences carrying a physical action is at least one in every one of the ten, per chapter 4, 5, 3, 4, 5, 4, 3, 4, 2, 6. Nine of the ten files carry no panel and the wall said nothing on all nine of those days, and the one panel in the ten is on day 490 in Chapter 0490. Thirteen Bare-Month ordinal phrases stand across the ten and every one of them is its own chapter's day, and nineteen weekday tokens stand and every one of them is the correct weekday for the day its sentence names, and the interior tokens that name a different day are published in the batch record at §2.**

**AND THE WORD-RUN CHECK AT TWO THRESHOLDS, because one threshold would have certified a batch with nine clones in it.** At forty: **zero within the ten and zero against the four hundred and seventy-nine pre-existing files.** At sixteen: **zero within the ten and three maximal runs against the pre-existing files in seven distinct texts, and all three are required declarations — the full form of day 298 in Chapter 0485, the fence count in Chapter 0489, and the date-on-the-door frame in Chapter 0482. THE FIRST EDITION RETURNED NINETEEN MAXIMAL RUNS IN TWENTY-NINE DISTINCT TEXTS AND TWELVE WITHIN THE TEN, AND TWELVE OF THOSE WERE CLONES OF SENTENCES IN THE BATCHES BEHIND — A SHEET'S DESCRIPTION, A TABLE AND AN HOUR, A STAIR AT THE TENTH HOUR, THE BOARD'S STATE, A MAN'S POSITION ON A ROW, A MAN DOWN A ROW, A LID ON A BOX, A HAND ON A POCKET, THE CARRIER'S UNOPENED THINGS, THE SECOND SLATE'S THREE FACTS AND ITS THREE NEGATIONS, AND A FLAG — AND ALL TWELVE WERE REWRITTEN BEFORE THE RECORD WAS FINISHED. No fact, no day, no count, no object and no line of dialogue was added, removed or moved by any of the rewrites.**

**AND THE ONE THING NO FILE THIS BATCH WROTE MAY PRINT: the name of Volume 09 and the words the man of about thirty-four says in Chapter 0428; the volume's one rule, said on day 438; the answer spoken in Chapter 0357; the refusal spoken in Chapter 0393, whose six words also stand at `bible/power-system.md:1684` and at `outline/volume-08.md:208`; the day-154 sentence; and the words the man of about twenty-seven speaks in Chapter 0486. NAMED WHERE EACH OF THEM IS, PRINTED NONE OF THEM, NO ABSENCE CERTIFIED. The hold is on this batch's own files and not on where a string stands, and a plan is not a page and a permanent witness section is not a page and a batch record is not a page either.**

**AND WHAT THIS BATCH DID NOT DO: no panel except on day 490, no notice written, no tracing or guessing at anything, no question asked of the man of about fifty-seven — he is in none of the ten — and no use of his rope, no elapsed count of days anywhere, no answer to any of the three questions and no preview of the fourth, no word on the second slate, no chapter opening the register, no third line entered or named, no joining of two of the four documents, no visit to the town four hundred miles inland, no mention of the three on the road, no room count and *dozen* at zero, no ordinal for a month, no number for the woman who walked a market, no new descriptor, nobody made stronger, and no controller file touched.** The volume's plan was not edited, no file under `outline/` was written or edited, `bible/power-system.md` was not opened, `outline/ending.md` was not opened, and no new final enemy was introduced. **Chapter 0475 was not written and this batch does not own it. The next phase is `workspace/volume-10/batch-0005/PROMPT.md` and it writes Chapters 0491 to 0500.**

## VOLUME 10, BATCH 0004 — REVIEW REPAIR PASS, and this block is at the foot of this file and it wins over every block above it

**A reviewer returned eight blocking findings on the ten pages and eleven on the batch record. Eight chapters changed by a sentence, a figure, an attribution or a closing paragraph. No chapter was restarted, no day moved, no card edited, and nothing under `outline/` was written, edited or renamed. The full index of both sets of findings is §14 of `state/batch-summaries/volume-10-batch-0004.md`. Only the summaries that changed are given here, and the summaries of the two chapters that did not change are not repeated.**

**Chapter 0481 — The Bucket Goes Up The Row Twice.** Unchanged except the closing paragraph. A woman at a stall two along from the man who keeps a stall has carried that water up the row in her own pail at the sixth hour for the whole of this flood and nobody has asked her to and nobody has thanked her, and on this morning she comes out with it, gets as far as the edge of her own board, looks at the trough, stops, and puts the pail down under her board with its mouth up, and she has not carried it up that row since. **THE CLOSING PARAGRAPH NO LONGER SAYS THAT NOBODY ON THAT ROW EVER SAID OUT LOUD THAT IT HAD BEEN CARRIED UP, WHICH FOUR EXCHANGES IN THE SAME FILE DENY, AND NO LONGER SAYS THAT ONE PERSON IN THIS CITY KNOWS IT FOR CERTAIN, WHICH THE MAN WHO CARRIED THE BUCKET CONTRADICTS WITH HIS OWN TWO TRIPS.** It says what the file had not said: the man who keeps the bucket carried it up that row on every morning since, has not asked the woman who put the pail down to carry it again, and the trough against that wall has not been dry once in the whole of this flood. Change: a pail is not carried up that row again and the man who owns the bucket carries it himself.

**Chapter 0483 — The Back Of The Sheet.** Unchanged except one clause. The man of about thirty-one turns the sheet over and the back of it is not blank: there is a figure on it in a hand that is not the hand the front is written in, and the two figures are of the same kind of thing and are not the same figure. **HIS FIRST MARKED TURN SAID THE TWO FIGURES ARE NOT THE SAME FIGURE, ARE THE SAME KIND OF THING, AND ARE THE SAME, AND THE THIRD OF THOSE REVERSED THE FIRST AND THE CHAPTER'S OWN NEXT EXCHANGE REVERSED IT BACK. THE THIRD CLAUSE IS GONE. The word the discovery turns on is carried four times in that file without it, and neither figure is printed in that chapter or in any other of the ten.**

**Chapter 0484 — A Man At The End Of A Lane.** The notch arithmetic is corrected and the summary above is wrong in two places. **THERE ARE NINE DOOR-FRAMES IN THAT LANE. HE CUT SEVEN NOTCHES IN THE FIRST SEVEN FRAMES, CUT THE EIGHTH NOTCH IN THE EIGHTH FRAME, AND STOPPED IN FRONT OF THE NINTH, WHICH ALREADY HAD A NAME CUT INTO IT WITH A KNIFE, LOW DOWN, WHERE A CHILD COULD REACH IT AND WHERE A GROWN PERSON STOOPING TO PUT A BUCKET UNDER A STEP WOULD PUT A HAND.** He was a third of the way up the lane when Tamsin Quill came out onto the bottom step, not two thirds. He said once, in his own mouth, to his back, that a figure that says how many of something there are is the plainest instrument of that claim this flood has produced, and that it is plainer than a notice and plainer than a name, and that the figure is the one that counts and the name is the one that finds people. There was room above the name for a notch and he did not cut it; he put the ninth mark in the mud in front of that door instead, in one stroke, and the water took it before he had gone the length of two houses, and he has not been up that lane since. Change: nine frames, eight notches, a ninth stroke gone before the morning was out of it, and a name still there.

**Chapter 0485 — Round A Board On The Flags.** Two things changed and one of them is a person. **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-NINE NO LONGER CARRIES THE FOUR SECONDS IN THIS CHAPTER AND THE SUMMARY ABOVE IS WRONG TO GIVE THEM TO HIM; HIS OWN ARE ON DAY 490, WHERE `outline/volume-10.md` §10 PUTS THEM IN ITS OWN WORDS. He still comes along the row with his own board under his arm and nineteen rates on it and still has to walk round the end of Adrian Vale's barrow, and he still says that a figure on a wall anybody can read is a figure the first man through who disagrees with it can change, and that a man who goes out onto eleven miles of flats with a slate and knocks comes back with a different figure from a man who goes out and does not. Nobody answers him and nobody improves on it. He took a name off that board in his own hand in about the ninth year of this flood and has never said so, the space is not filled, and about four people in that row looked at the face of that board that morning and not one of them looked at the space.**

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR, TWO STALLS ALONG FROM THE STORE, IS NOT SILENT IN THIS CHAPTER AND THE SUMMARY ABOVE IS WRONG TO SAY HE IS.** He has heard the whole of what was said there, and he puts the flat of his hand on his own board where it hangs on its string, and takes the hand off and wipes it down his apron, and then he says one thing, and it takes him about as long as it takes to say it, and he says it to the row and not to the man with the board under his arm: **that there is a gap in the writing on that board and that it cannot be seen from where he stand.** Nobody thanked him for it and nobody argued with him about it, and the man of about thirty-nine turned the face of his own board about as far as a hand's width towards himself, looked at the gap, put it back the way it was and went on down the row with it under his arm. **THE CHAPTER DOES NOT SAY HE WAS RIGHT.** He has a piece of chalk in his own inside breast pocket that has not been out of it since the forty-third day of the Short Month, which is the two hundred and ninety-eighth day of this flood, printed at full length, and he did not take it out. And **there has not been an offer from that stall in that market since the hundred and fifty-seventh day of the Bare Month when he made one in a room and nobody in that room accepted it and nobody refused it — so the standing offer is NOT spent in this chapter, and the summary above is wrong, and the hundred and fifty-seventh day is day 472 and thirteen days behind this one.**

Then a woman at the stall two along from him asks the man of about thirty-nine whether she should say her prices out loud instead of putting them on, which is the first question anybody at that end of that market has asked him in as long as he has carried a board, and he says he does not know and that there is not one person in that market who does, and that he would rather say I do not know this morning than say something in the afternoon that turns out to be what he said this morning. She will not ask him anything else. Change: a man said one true thing about a board and was thanked by nobody, and a woman who asked the only man who might know was told he does not know.

**Chapter 0487 — The Offer, Withdrawn.** One marked turn changed. The man of about thirty-four takes his hands off his string, holds them out and turns them over, and says to the man of about thirty-one, in his own mouth, that he is not going to be the man who goes out and makes that other number, and that the reason is that **he cannot knock, and he has not knocked at a door since he was a boy in this market.** A man at the end of the row says that is going to sound well, and he says it is going to sound well and is not going to be well. **THE TURN THAT THE SUMMARY ABOVE QUOTES NEXT — that a man who sells off a board has a hand that has never been on a door — WAS IN A PARAGRAPH WITH NO SPEAKER MARKER AND ITS SUBJECT DESCRIBED A MAN WHO IS NOT IN THIS CHAPTER, AND IT HAS BEEN GIVEN A SPEAKER MARKER AND PUT IN THE FIRST PERSON: he says that he has got a board of his own on a string and he has not been on a door since he was a boy in this market, and that if he went out onto those flats he would have stood in a gateway and counted and come back with the same figure the first man has and had to say so in front of about nine people.** The man of about thirty-one says that is the end of it, that he is not going to say he was right or wrong, and that the two counts on that slate were made by two men and neither of them was him. A hand is put flat on his own board and left there until it goes cold on it, and not one person on that row is going to be the one to say why. Change: the offer is closed by the man who made it, for a reason nobody had thought of until he said it.

**Chapter 0489 — The Shut Road, On A Sunday.** One sentence changed. The man of about sixty stands at the ninth post with his hand flat on the cord; his handcart is in the lane with the bar down, with a coil of rope in it that has been used and a hazel mallet with its head bound in cord, and he touches neither. He goes down the line counting the posts, finds that the eleventh withy has come loose at the top, lifts it, looks at the wear on the cord and on the wood, lets it go, does the same thing again with the other hand and twice more, and then takes his hands off the fence. He says it out loud, to the fence, with nobody on that road to hear it, and says the eleventh is loose and he has looked at it four times and has not bound it up again and is not going to, and that there is nobody within four hundred miles of that lane who could come and tell him which two of those posts this argument is about. **THE OTHER MAN IN THIS CITY WHO HAS SAID THOSE NUMBERS OUT LOUD PAID FOR HALF OF A HOLDING ABOUT FORTY YEARS AGO, AND HE HAS SAID THEM OUT LOAD TWICE AND WAS ASKED FOR THEM NEITHER TIME, which is what `chapters/volume-10/chapter-0462.md` and `chapters/volume-10/chapter-0478.md` show. The summary above says he has not said them again and is wrong.** The two of them have never seen one another, and neither has ever given those figures to anybody who was going to write them down. Change: two men have said that fence aloud without being asked and the two counts are the same, and nobody in four hundred miles can be shown where any of the sixteen is standing.

**Chapter 0490 — A Figure Chalked On The Board.** One sentence changed. The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register has the store's own chalk out of the box on the step and writes this city's own figure on the board on the right of the old figures and a hand's width clear of them, with a stroke in the second place of it, and she does not rub anything out, and the figures of the two hundred and twenty-fourth day are on that board when she starts and when she finishes and there is no place on that board that has been wiped. **ABOUT FOUR PEOPLE READ IT AND ABOUT FIVE OF THE NINE WHO WERE STANDING AT THAT END OF THE MARKET DID NOT, AND EVERY ONE OF THE FOUR WHO READ IT WORKED OUT THAT THE TWO SETS OF FIGURES ON IT DO NOT AGREE, AND NOT ONE OF THE NINE OF THEM CAN SAY WHICH SET OF THE TWO IS RIGHT, AND THE REASON NONE OF THEM CAN IS A WORD, AND THE WORD IS NOT ON THE BOARD. The summary above says four of the nine who read it, which puts four readers and nine readers in consecutive sentences.** The man of about thirty-nine comes down the row, stops, reads it, and says that the figure is a count of places and there is not one person standing in that market it is a count of, and that every person standing there is going to read it as though it were a count of them, and that is what a public figure does, and it does it in about a morning. Nobody said he was right and nobody said he was wrong and he did not take his own board down. The wall said one thing, and it is this volume's one panel, and it is the only panel in these ten chapters. The man of about twenty-seven, standing at the trough with the rag in his hand, refuses it in about four seconds in a handful of words, and nobody improved on it and nobody thanked him and he has asked in advance not to be thanked and has not been. Change: this city's own figure is on a public wall in chalk and it stays there, and it does not agree with the other figures, and nobody can say which is right.

**AND THE FIGURES, corrected, with the definitions, and no target set on any of them.** The bolded share on the published definition: 17.91, 16.90, 20.38, 15.51, 21.38, 22.16, 22.71, 13.54, 13.09 and 13.89, a range of **13.09 to 22.71**, an unweighted mean of **17.75** and a weighted mean of **18.09** on **9,730 words** in **141 paragraphs**. **The low end sits inside the envelopes Volume 08 and Volume 09 published and the high end sits 0.11 BELOW Volume 09's 22.82, where the block above published it 0.78 above, and neither figure is rounded toward the envelope.** The count of paragraphs carrying neither speaker marker is **zero in all ten**, and a paragraph carrying a bold mark and no quotation mark is **zero in all ten**, and no exchange carries two bold marks. **89 narration paragraphs and 52 speech paragraphs make the 141.** The exchange is **twenty-one plain speeches and fourteen answered, sixty-six point seven per cent.** The longest bolded turn is **eighty-five words in Chapter 0486**, and no turn is over ninety and none is over the hard ceiling of ninety-five, and no turn was cut to make a number. Long sentences of forty words or more: **94 on reading A and 87 on reading B, and the two readings DIFFER in all ten of these files**, on a batch mean of **34.3** words to the sentence on reading A and **28.9** on reading B, longest single sentence 79. **THE LONGEST RUN OF SPEECH PARAGRAPHS IS TWO AND NOT FOUR, AND THE BLOCK ABOVE NAMED A RUN OF FOUR IN CHAPTER 0483 AGAINST A SEQUENCE THAT DID NOT CONTAIN ONE.** The paragraph composition, published as four separate counts and never as their sum: **fifty-two speech, eleven one-sentence narration lead-ins, seventeen free-standing one-sentence narration paragraphs, and forty-four of three or more sentences**, and a paragraph of three or more sentences carrying a physical action is at least one in every one of the ten, at 3, 5, 2, 4, 4, 4, 3, 4, 2 and 3, against a whole of 4, 5, 4, 5, 5, 6, 3, 4, 3 and 5, **and the subset is smaller than the whole in every one of the ten, which the block above's figures were not.** Nine of the ten files carry no panel and the wall said nothing on all nine of those days, and the one panel in the ten is on day 490 in Chapter 0490. **FOURTEEN BARE-MONTH ORDINALS STAND, not thirteen, and the one that is not its own chapter's own day is the hundred and fifty-seventh in Chapter 0485, which is the plan's own anchor and stands on a page at `chapters/volume-10/chapter-0472.md:3`. ## VOLUME 10, BATCH 0005 — Chapters 0491 to 0500, and this block is at the foot of this file and it wins over every block above it

**Ten chapters, one to a day, days 491 to 500, from a Tuesday to a Thursday, the hundred and seventy-sixth to the hundred and eighty-fifth day of the Bare Month. `chapters/volume-10/chapter-0475.md` is still not on disk and is not this batch's. The ten cards were written at the head of `state/batch-summaries/volume-10-batch-0005.md` before Chapter 0491 existed and no card was edited after its chapter was written.**

**Chapter 0491 — A Board Hung Off The Flags.** Day 491, a Tuesday, the hundred and seventy-sixth. The man of about thirty-four's board is up on its string with a stone on the foot of it and the flags under that stone wet. Adrian Vale takes the stone out of the water onto dry flags, draws the string up a hand's width, gets a length of split wood off a trestle and puts the near corner of the board on it, and nobody at that end of the market asks him what he is doing and nobody is waiting to see whether he is any use. The man looks at the foot of his own board, pulls the rail, and it does not come down, and says he is not going to go down that row and find out who did and that he will reach for it the way he has reached for everything on that row for the whole of this flood, and that if the row cannot get under it the row can go round the other end. A woman at a stall two along from him says the string is past her awning pole, and he says it is her pole at one end and his board at this end and neither of them put the wood under, and that is all he is going to say about it at this end of the row. **He spends the rest of the day taking the whole weight of his own board on the flat of his left hand to get his hand to the foot of it, and his sleeve has a wet band on it by the middle of the day, and the flags underneath stay wet and the flags two feet away stay dry. His own piece of chalk stays in his inside breast pocket and does not come out.** The woman two stalls along says afterwards that somebody has put a piece of wood under his board and he is not going to say so and neither is she, and that she would rather have a hand up in the air than the other way round. Change: a board hangs a hand's width higher than its owner set it and nobody at that end of that market knows whose hands did it.

**Chapter 0492 — A Door Shut On A Wednesday.** Day 492, a Wednesday, the hundred and seventy-seventh. The door at the back of the room over a market shuts on its own, with the damp gone out of the bottom rail and nothing having been done to it, and there is no name on that door. The register-keeper says the door is not an instrument, and that there is no sheet going out and there is not going to be one, and that the seven that went out in this flood went out because a man had days to write them in before the day they were for. She says a room can have a day on its door and be here on the day, and that anybody who wants a person in that room has to bring the person, and that she has made that plain twice and is making it plain a third time. **About four of them come up that stair anyway, because there is a figure on a wall at the bottom of it, and every one of them shut the door behind them and stood in a room where there is nothing for a figure to go into, and she tells them to go down and put it on a wall like the one at the bottom of it, and a man says that is a poor answer and she says it is the whole of the answer there is and she would rather give it five times than write one thing down that a person will come back and say somebody chose.** The register is not opened; the second slate is on the shelf behind it with a day on it and nothing under it, not picked up, not turned over, not written on, and no hand but hers near the shelf. The one-place sheet is named once, as a sheet that came down a road four hundred miles long, and is laid beside nothing. Change: a figure on a public wall has brought about four people up a stair who then go back down it with nothing.

**Chapter 0493 — A Third Line In The Register.** Day 493, a Thursday, the hundred and seventy-eighth. The bench is cleared and the table drawn up and the register is still on its shelf. She says out loud, before the pen goes near the page, that **the day in the line is the four hundred and ninetieth day of this flood, which is the morning that figure went up on that wall, and that she is writing the line on the four hundred and ninety-third day of this flood, and that those two days are three days apart, and that she is naming both of them out loud because two days on a page with nothing saying which of the two is which is not a record of anything.** She refuses to put the second day in as well, on the ground that a book holding the day a thing happened and the day it was written down has four places in it and this one has three, and that one of those three stays empty and it is not the one on the right. **She takes the register off the shelf herself, turns it so the page is right way up for her and not for anybody else, puts her own working slate face up on the bench with two figures on one face of it and neither rubbed out, writes the day, and then writes the figure under it. That is the first thing that has gone in the second column in this flood. It goes in with nobody's name on it, and the third column stays empty.** A man at the far end of the bench puts both hands out towards the shelf, stops them in the air where the register had been standing, brings them back, and says nothing. She says there is no column in that book for a day and a figure under a day together, and that the book has a place for a person in the same line and will keep having one while she keeps it, and that nobody in that room is going to be asked about that today. She shuts the cover, carries the book back, and puts it on the shelf herself. The slate behind it has a day on it and nothing under it and is not touched. Change: the second column of that book holds a figure for the first time in this flood and the third column is still empty.

**Chapter 0494 — A Stack Comes Down On The Boards.** Day 494, a Friday, the hundred and seventy-ninth. Weather off the water gets into a stack on a salt wharf that is higher than a man, and Adrian Vale has both hands on the near edge of a tarpaulin that has come off its lashing. He gets the edge down, gets a batten off the pile and puts it under the bottom course at the windward end, puts his shoulder against it, and the stack comes down along its own length and not across it, and goes over onto the boards at the end of the wharf in one movement rather than in pieces. **Nobody on those boards asks him anything, nobody stops to look at him, and nobody is standing about waiting on him to be any use.** The man of about thirty-eight walks to the head of the stack and back and says it went the way a stack goes, along what it is made of, that he has watched about nine of them come down on that wharf and has not watched one decide anything about where it was going, and that he is not going to thank a man for a batten and is not going to say a word about it to the man who put it there. A man at the foot of the stack says he watched it lean before Adrian got to it. He says it is on the end boards, and that the end boards are the ones he has not been able to get at since that door went on the wrong side of its opening on the hundred and seventy-third day of the Bare Month, and that it has not moved a foot and neither have I, and that he has been going round that building every morning since. **They clear it and he walks down the run with his hand on the top of each board and does not go round the shed once. The shed door shuts once for about as long as it takes a man to count nine and then stands open again.** The tally-board still has a figure on it that is written down nowhere. Change: a stack comes down on the boards a man wanted and not on the ones he did not, and nobody on that wharf has said the name of the man who put a batten under the foot of it.

**Chapter 0495 — A Board Turned Face In.** Day 495, a Saturday, the hundred and eightieth. The man of about thirty-four takes his board down off the string and does not hang it up again, and turns it so the face of it goes into the back of his own trestle, and the string and the stone stay on the post with nothing at the end of the string. The store's board is still on its two nails with both its sets of figures on it, neither rubbed out and neither moved since the morning the right-hand one went up, and the wall above it is not washed, and the trough is full and the bucket is in it up to its middle and the rag is on the stone lip. He says that a board on a string is the only thing at that end of that market a person can read from the far end of it, that he has had it out there since before the water came, and that he has taken it down because a figure went up on a wall and everything anybody walks past can read now. A woman two stalls along says twelve people have asked her that morning what the figure is, and he says he has no answer to that at that end of the row, and that his nineteen are on a piece of wood, and that there is not one person in that market who does not already know what he charges, and that he would rather nobody knew the price of his board than had to stand in the row explaining it. **He coils the string and puts it behind the trestle. A man at the far end of the row comes down it, looks at the post and the bare back of the board, and goes on without stopping, and it is not a thing that man does. His own piece of chalk does not come out of his inside breast pocket and there is a lighter patch on the front of the coat where it has been going in and out.** Change: the only thing anybody at that end of that market could read from the far end of it is gone.

**Chapter 0496 — A Plank Across The Wet Silt.** Day 496, a Sunday, the hundred and eighty-first. Eleven miles of flats under weather, and a channel that went dry in this flood still dry, with about nine feet of wet silt in the bottom of it, and a barrow cannot get up the side of it. Adrian Vale lays a plank from the firm lip to the firm lip and keeps his hand flat on the middle of it, and the middle goes down about the width of a hand and the two ends do not move. **Nobody out on those flats asks him anything and nobody is waiting on him to be any use.** The man of about thirty-one comes up to the near lip with a barrow and the register-keeper's working slate face up on it under a fold of sacking, and gets the front wheel onto the plank and comes across, and the wheel goes over and the slate does not go anywhere. He says he has carried four things up that road in a year and has not opened one of them, that this is the fifth and is not in a satchel, and that there is a slate under that sacking going back down that road with something on it that is not the same as what went up it, and that he is not going to look at it here or on the way. A man with a spade on the far bank is told the ditch is nine hundred paces off and that nobody is paying him for it, and goes back to it and is not asked anything else. Change: a count taken on foot across those flats has gone past the middle of them on a barrow and has come back different again.

**Chapter 0497 — The Second Morning On The Flats.** Day 497, a Monday, the hundred and eighty-second. The weather has gone off in the night and the second morning of a count begins at the near end of the stack of boards, and the whole of that line of doorways on the far side of the channel can be walked. A man sits down on a door-sill and does not get up again until the light has come right across it. A woman wrings a coat out over a bucket and hangs it on a gate. The man of about thirty-one comes along the line with the register-keeper's working slate face up on a board across two trestles and keeps his hand flat over the face of it while he counts, and moves it for each one. **He says there is a place about two thirds of the way along that line where a man on the road side says there is a door and the same man on the channel side says there is a wall, and that he is right both times and both go into the same book because he has walked it one way and not the other, and that that is the whole of the difference between what is on that slate this morning and what was on it yesterday.** He says it is not the weather, because the weather was the same coming off the low ground, and not anybody knocking, because nobody has knocked at a door on those flats since the water came and he would have heard it from the road, and that what is on that slate is what a man walking a line found. Nobody thanks him and nobody argues with him. The two figures are on one face of the slate, neither rubbed out, and they are not the same. A man who sat down on a sill gets up and goes the other way along the line and stops at one place and stands there. The barrow goes up the far bank with the slate face up and a fold of sacking over it that is not taken off again. Change: two figures of the same kind of thing on one slate that do not agree, for a reason that is neither the weather nor a knock.

**Chapter 0498 — A Woman At The Back Of The Room.** Day 498, a Tuesday, the hundred and eighty-third. About nine people in the room over a market, none of them sent for anything, the register not opened, the second slate on the shelf with a day on it and nothing under it. A woman comes up the stair with a slate of her own under her arm and no tin in her hands and stands at the back with her back to the wall, and nobody in that room asks her one thing about anything at all. The man of about thirty-nine is at the near end of the bench with his own board under his arm, the nineteen rates on it and the space where a name is not unfilled, and he turns the board a hand's width towards himself, looks down the length of the writing, and puts it back the way it was. He says nobody at that end of the market has rubbed that figure out and nobody is going to, and that he has watched what a figure on a wall does to a person and has said so out loud in a market and was thanked by nobody and is not going to say it again in here. **At the middle of the morning she comes down the side of the room past the back of the bench, stops beside him, puts her slate under her other arm, and says to him and not to the room and not to the wall, at a volume that carried as far as the two of them and no further: what is a word for, in a count, when the only thing anybody will use it for is to make two numbers the same. He does not answer it and the room does not hear it.** He stands with his hand along the top rail of his own board and does not look at her and does not look at the room, and after about as long as it takes a man to decide whether a person is going to say anything else he puts his thumb on the space where a name is not and takes his hand off it again. Nobody thanks her and nobody tells her it was a good question. Change: one question has been put to one person in this flood and got nothing back from that person, and not one of the nine people in that room heard it.

**Chapter 0499 — A Sheet Turned Face Down.** Day 499, a Wednesday, the hundred and eighty-fourth. The hundred and eighty-third day of the Bare Month is wiped off the door with the sleeve of a coat and the hundred and eighty-fourth is put on in the same place, and the door takes it, and both of them are on that door, and there is no name on it. The register-keeper says she is not going to be the one who says a name of a room on a door, nor the one who writes a person's business down because they brought it in here, and that if the room has no instrument for a thing that arrives out of a market it will go on not having one and say so out loud. **A man at the far end of the bench has had it in his coat since the middle of the morning. He gets it out, puts it flat on the table with the writing against the wood, turns his hand over it, and there it is: a piece of paper about the size of a man's two hands with a figure on it in his own hand and a stroke in the second place of it, the same figure that is on the wall, because he stood in front of that wall and copied it off it with a piece of chalk and a slate and nothing else. He pushes it a foot away and stands at the window with his back to the table.** Nobody turns it over. About four of the nine know what is on it and about four came up that stair without knowing. The paper is not a document of this flood, has no heading and no places ruled under one, belongs to nobody at that table, and is the only thing anybody has brought into that room out of a market. The register is not opened and the second slate is not touched and no hand but hers goes near the shelf. Change: there is a copy of the figure on that wall in this city that nobody in that room made and nobody in that room will look at.

**Chapter 0500 — The Board Washed At The Fourth Hour.** Day 500, a Thursday, the hundred and eighty-fifth. The lid is off the box that stands on the step by the store's door and the register-keeper is at the foot of that wall with her left hand flat on the stone beside the trough and the store's own chalk in her right hand. The board is on its two nails with the account of the two hundred and twenty-fourth day of this flood on the left of it and a figure of this city's own in chalk on the right of them a hand's width clear, and neither has been rubbed out, and the wall above the board is not washed. **She puts a second figure of this city's own on the board to the right of the first, a further hand's width clear, and there is no place on that board that has been wiped and there was none before she began. She says what it is before anybody says anything: that it is a second figure of the same kind of thing as the one already on that board, made in this city, that it is not the same as that one, and that the two of them do not agree with one another any more than that one agrees with the other set. Nobody thanks her, nobody argues with her, and nobody tells her she is right.** A man at the end of the row says nobody has said which of the three is the newest, and she says the one on the far right is the newest, and that after this morning there are three things on one board and no way at all for a person standing there to find out which of the three would have been there if only one of them had been. A woman at a stall says who washes it, and she says the man who washes it washes it every morning, that he washed it before the figure went up and washes it now with three sets of figures on it, and that the wall above it is not washed and is not going to be washed. **The man of about thirty-four is two stalls along with his board face in against his own trestle and his hand does not go into his coat at all that morning. The man of about thirty-nine comes down the row, reads the figure on the right, and goes on the length of the row and does not stop at the end of it the way he does. Nine people were at that end of the market when the third figure went up, about four of them read it and about five of them did not, and not one of the nine can say which of the three is the one anybody should be reading.** The board comes down at the fourth hour and the man who washes it washes every inch of all three sets and stops at the top of the board where the wall begins, and all three come out of the wet as clean as they went into it, and the wall above is as dirty as it was at the fourth hour yesterday. **Out of this city, past the last named house, a road goes up and a fence stands along the top of it, and there are three barrows on that road, a hundred and nine days out, measured from the day they went, which is the three hundred and ninety-first day of this flood, and there is not one person on that road anybody in this city can be shown.** Change: a third set of figures is on that board and two of the three are this city's own and do not agree with each other, and all three are washed every morning, and a road nobody can be shown one person on is a road a column has nothing to say about.

**AND THE FIGURES, with the definitions, and no target set on any of them.** The bolded share on the published definition: 18.19, 26.54, 26.57, 15.99, 16.71, 14.04, 25.96, 12.16, 25.41 and 20.48, a range of **12.16 to 26.54**, an unweighted mean of **20.21** and a weighted mean of **20.79** on **9,612 words** in **149 paragraphs**. **The low end sits inside the envelopes Volume 08 and Volume 09 published and the high end sits ABOVE both, and four of the ten sit above Volume 09's 22.82, and neither figure is rounded toward an envelope. The mean is a consequence and not a plan.** The count of paragraphs carrying neither speaker marker is **zero in all ten** on the first-person instrument, and a paragraph carrying a bold mark and no quotation mark is **zero in all ten**, and no exchange carries two bold marks. **93 narration paragraphs and 56 speech paragraphs make the 149.** The exchange is **twenty plain speeches and twenty answered, one hundred per cent**, and the cause is the shape the cards asked for and is not a target. The longest bolded turn is **seventy-seven words, twice**, no turn is over ninety and none over the hard ceiling of ninety-five, **and five turns were over the hard ceiling on a first pass and every one was broken by a plain-speech interjection and a narration beat and no turn was cut to make a number.** Long sentences of forty words or more: **65 on reading A and 65 on reading B, and the two readings RETURN THE SAME FIGURE IN ALL TEN OF THESE FILES, and the reason is published and is that no paragraph in these ten files carries more than two quotation marks**, on a batch mean of **27.5** words to the sentence on both readings, longest single sentence 74. **The longest run of speech paragraphs is two.** The paragraph composition, published as four separate counts and never as their sum: **fifty-six speech, twenty-five one-sentence narration lead-ins, seven free-standing one-sentence narration paragraphs, forty-seven of three or more sentences**, and a paragraph of three or more sentences carrying a physical action is at least one in every one of the ten, at 6, 5, 4, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 4 and 4 against a whole of 6, 5, 5, 5, 4, 4, 5, 5, 4 and 4, **and the subset equals the whole in two of the ten and is smaller in eight, which is published rather than changed.** Nine of ten files carry no panel and the ten all carry none, and the one panel in the volume was spent on day 490 in a chapter this batch did not write. **FOURTEEN BARE-MONTH ORDINALS STAND, ten of ten chapters carrying their own day, and the two that do not are published in the batch record at §2. ELEVEN WEEKDAY TOKENS STAND and no interior token names a day other than its own chapter's.** **THERE IS NOT ONE QUESTION MARK IN THE TEN FILES AND NOT ONE INTERROGATIVE IN ANY MOUTH IN THE NINE CHAPTERS THAT ARE NOT 0498.**

**AND THE WORD-RUN CHECK AT BOTH THRESHOLDS. At forty: ZERO, within the ten and against all four hundred and eighty-nine pre-existing files. At sixteen: ZERO, within the ten, and ZERO against the four hundred and eighty-nine pre-existing files, and the twenty-two sixteen-word clones the check found before the rewrites are published as twenty-two rows in the batch record at §7. NO FACT, DAY, COUNT, OBJECT, CAST PLACEMENT OR PROHIBITION MOVED IN ANY OF THEM.**

**AND THE FIGURES THAT CAME BACK NON-ZERO, PUBLISHED RATHER THAN PASSED: *level* is at ONE, in Chapter 0491, in the sentence that says water finds its own level along the flags. *rooms* is at ONE in Chapter 0493 and is not a room count for anybody. *notice* and *notices* are at ZERO in all ten, where the card said one of them would stand. *box* is at ONE and it is the store's own chalk box on the step. *fence* is at ONE and it carries no total. *sledge* is at ZERO across these ten files on both readings and the close's list of fifty-six zeros is still wrong about it and that error is not this batch's to repair.**
