# VOLUME 10, BATCH 0005 — THE REVIEW REPAIR OF THE SUMMARIES ABOVE, WHICH CORRECTED TWO FIGURES IN THEM AND ADDED WHAT THREE CHAPTERS NOW CARRY, AND WHICH AMENDS NOTHING ABOVE IT

**A review of Volume 10's Batch 0005 found eight things. All eight are repaired and the corrections are published beside what they replace.**

**THE TWO FIGURES CORRECTED IN THE SUMMARIES ABOVE. The bolded share, on the published definition, per file 0491 to 0500, is now **16.53, 26.54, 26.57, 14.40, 16.71, 12.27, 25.96, 12.16, 25.41 and 20.48 — a range of 12.16 to 26.57, an unweighted mean of 19.70 and a weighted mean of 20.11 on 9,937 words in 150 paragraphs** — the summaries above give 18.19, 26.54, 26.57, 15.99, 16.71, 14.04, 25.96, 12.16, 25.41 and 20.48, a range of 12.16 to **26.54**, a mean of 20.21 and a weighted mean of 20.79 on 9,612 words in 149 paragraphs. The three that moved are 0491, 0494 and 0496, the added words are narration and not speech, and **the high end of the range in the summaries above is wrong independently of the repair, because the summaries' own per-file list gives 26.57 for Chapter 0493 and 26.54 for Chapter 0492 and 26.54 is therefore the second-highest chapter and not the high end.** 94 narration paragraphs and 56 speech paragraphs make the 150. The four paragraph counts, the longest bolded turn, the longest run of speech paragraphs, the exchange, the long-sentence readings and the word-run check are unchanged, and the word-run check returns zero at forty and zero at sixteen against all 495 pre-existing files after the repair. Second, ***nine people* was published as a bare cardinal in Chapter 0500 and Chapter 0500 contains neither it nor *about nine people* at all**; over the forty-nine files on disk the *about* frame stands 40 in 24, *the nine people* stands 4 in 4, and a bare cardinal stands 3 in 2, in Chapter 0460 and Chapter 0467, and Batch 0005 added none.**

**AND THE THREE CHAPTER SUMMARIES ABOVE STAND WITH ONE ADDITION EACH, because the protagonist now thinks on the page in all three and that is a fact about the chapter and not an interpretation of it.**

**Chapter 0491 — A Board Hung Off The Flags.** Day 491, a Tuesday, the hundred and seventy-sixth. **ADDED BY THE REPAIR: Adrian Vale wanted the order of it and got the order he wanted — the string first, the stone second, the wood last, and the wood put in at the corner where a hand has to go under the board to find it — and it took him about as long as it takes a man to put a coat on, and no longer, and nobody at that end of the market asked him what he was doing and nobody stopped and looked at him doing it. Change: unchanged. A board hangs higher than its owner set it, he has to reach for the foot of it, the row walks under it, and nobody there knows whose hands put the wood under the corner.**

**Chapter 0494 — A Stack Comes Down On The Boards.** Day 494, a Friday, the hundred and seventy-ninth. **ADDED BY THE REPAIR: Adrian Vale had watched that stack lean on two mornings running and had not been able to say where it wanted to go until the wind came round onto the near end of it and the boards under it moved, and after that it was in his hands and not in his head, and he was wet to the chest for it and no further forward than he had been the morning before. Nobody on those boards asked him anything and nobody turned round to watch him work, and the reason was not that he had kept it quiet. Change: unchanged. A stack comes down along its own length and not across it, it does not come down on the run of boards the man of about thirty-eight has had to walk round a building to get at, he does not walk round it any more, and nobody on those boards has said the name of the man who put a batten under the foot of it.**

**Chapter 0496 — A Plank Across The Wet Silt.** Day 496, a Sunday, the hundred and eighty-first. **ADDED BY THE REPAIR: Adrian Vale brought a plank out with him because a plank is the answer to about nine feet of wet silt and because there was nothing else left in that stack, and that was the whole of the reason and he had it worked out before he got to the near lip; the wind was doing enough to it to make a man look busy and was not doing any of it on purpose; and the palm of his hand was wet through at the end of it with the silt got into the two lines of it across the middle of the palm. Change: unchanged. A barrow with a slate on it goes across a channel on a plank and comes back different again, the plank goes back on the stack, and nothing on it says what it was laid across.**

**NOTHING ELSE MOVED. No chapter was restarted, no day or weekday or Bare-Month ordinal moved, no page prints a day after the hundred and eighty-fifth, no new person and no new descriptor came onto a page, and the restricted first-person count is still zero in all ten. The one sentence the review caught breaking its own tense, in Chapter 0492, is fixed and no fact in that chapter moved. The full account is at `state/batch-summaries/volume-10-batch-0005.md` §15, and the words the man of about twenty-seven speaks in Chapter 0486 are still on that page and in no file, and no absence of them is certified here.**


---

# VOLUME 10 CLOSE — the volume in one page per movement, and this block is at the foot of this file and it wins over every block above it

**Forty-nine chapter files of fifty are on disk. `chapters/volume-10/chapter-0475.md` is not, and a close names it and does not write it, and the day it is for is day 475, a Sunday, the hundred and sixtieth day of the Bare Month. THE MEASUREMENTS ARE IN `state/volume-10-close.md` AND ARE NOT REPEATED HERE.**

## 1. Batch 0001, days 451 to 460, Chapters 0451 to 0460, and what a reader was given

**A board in a market that has stood unwritten since the two hundred and twenty-fifth day of this flood is washed every morning and carries the figures of the two hundred and twenty-fourth day and the wall above it is not washed. A satchel comes up a four-hundred-mile road on the quarterly round and goes on a table and is not opened. The woman who walked a market on two feet is asked something she can answer and goes on working the morning, and she has no name and no number and no descriptor on any page in this manuscript and was put there by being asked. Four hands carry a page round a market and the fourth is a man who keeps a stall who says what is wrong with a page in about nine words and is thanked by nobody. The one-place sheet is read out in the room over a market in daylight with a date in chalk on the door, about nine people in it, and the reader says out loud what it is for before anybody asks, and the heading has ONE place under it and the one is a figure. A length of new rope goes off the back of a chair and nobody in that room says one word about it. A man at a salt wharf is right about something in about four seconds and nobody says so. A man of about twenty-seven is handed a rag without a word and nobody teaches him anything. A woman of about sixty-nine comes up nine steps with two empty tins and does not ask a fourth question and nobody remarks on the not-asking. A page goes round a market in four hands and nobody says a word about how it comes back. Change at the end of the ten: a page with one place under it has been read out in a room, a question of this series' line has been asked and not answered, a man is handed a thing and is taught nothing, and Adrian Vale has carried a barrow up a market twice and put boards down where four people pointed without asking which of two places a board was wanted in.**

## 2. Batch 0002, days 461 to 470, Chapters 0461 to 0470, and what a reader was given

**A wharf where the light is on the water and there is no wall, and a man is given a barrow he could not read and does not try to. Two men in a room and neither of them is asked. A one-place sheet read out again and a figure in one place, and the discovery that two rooms in this city have counted the same kind of thing and got two figures and the reason is a word — a count of places and a count of people — and the word is said once, in a mouth, naming the difference, and it is the volume's own word and it is on no other page. A slate on a trestle and a man who is not the one counting. Sixteen willow posts and eleven withies and a mallet with its head bound in cord, and a handcart nobody sent. Eleven miles of flats and a number that is a number of places. Four things a man has carried up and down a road and has not opened. A number said out loud in a room and not written down. A bar he put in and a hurdle he hung on it. A method that has nobody to do the first part, and a question asked out loud in a room and nobody answers it. Change at the end of the ten: the reason two figures about the same kind of thing do not match is a word, and the word is in a woman's mouth in a room with a date on the door, and a room has said out loud that it has no instrument for asking anybody anything in advance of anything.**

## 3. Batch 0003, days 471 to 480, Chapters 0471 to 0480, and the hole

**A wind comes through a store door and a board comes off a string and hits a barrow. A one-place sheet lying on a table and the part of it nobody read. A column empty on purpose and a column empty. Two men with boards in one room. THEN THE DAY THAT IS NOT ON DISK: the hundred and sixtieth day of the Bare Month, a Sunday, and `outline/volume-10.md` §8 is the chapter in which a woman decides out loud, in daylight, with a date in chalk on the door, about nine people present, that this city's own figure goes on the store's board beside the figures of the two hundred and twenty-fourth day, after naming the cost out loud first, and the cost is hers. It is not on disk and it was not written and this close did not write it. THE FIVE CHAPTERS THAT FOLLOW REFER TO WHAT WAS SAID IN THAT ROOM ON THAT SUNDAY AND NONE OF THEM NARRATES IT.** A man who knocks at doors for a living and a satchel on a bench and six things carried and none opened. A man who looks at the rope and nobody asks. A stone under the far end of a trough. A second morning on the flats and a count on foot that comes out different again. Change at the end of the ten, excluding the missing file: a decision is referred to by five chapters and is on no page in this repository, and that is the whole of what the ten days did and did not do.**

## 4. Batch 0004, days 481 to 490, Chapters 0481 to 0490, and the climax

**A bucket goes up the row twice and a woman puts her pail down under her own board and does not carry it up that row again. A door that will not shut and about five people who come up a stair who had not come up it. The back of a sheet and a second figure on it in a second hand. A man at the end of a lane with a stick and nine door-frames and a name cut low into the ninth. A barrow of stall boards round a row and a man of about thirty-nine who is right in about four seconds and is thanked by nobody. THE MAN WHO WASHES THE BOARD, asked for the first time in his life what he is doing to that board, and answering once, in the local language, in four to eight words, which are on that page and in no file. The offer withdrawn because a man who sells off a board has a hand that has never been on a door. A plank against a shed door. The shut road on a Sunday, sixteen posts and eleven withies, and a man at the ninth. AND THE CLIMAX: this city's own figure chalked onto the store's board beside the figures of the two hundred and twenty-fourth day, about four people read it and about nine do not, the wall says one thing, and the man who washes that board refuses it in about four seconds and is thanked by nobody. Change at the end of the ten: a figure of this city's own is on a public wall and the two sets of figures do not agree and nobody can say which is right, and the man who has washed that board every morning since the seventh week of this flood is told in front of about nine people that the reason it is clean is not him.**

## 5. Batch 0005, days 491 to 500, Chapters 0491 to 0500, and the last image

**A board hangs a hand's width higher than its owner set it and nobody at that end of that market knows whose hands did it. A door shuts on a Wednesday on its own and about four people come up that stair because there is a figure on a wall at the bottom of it and every one of them goes back down with nothing in their hands. THE RESOLUTION: the register carries a third line, the four hundred and ninetieth day of this flood in the first column and a figure of this city's own in the second and nothing in the third, and the two days are three days apart and both are named in one sentence in one mouth, and the book was taken off the shelf and put back by the woman who keeps it and a man at the far end of the bench put both his hands out towards the shelf and brought them back. A stack comes down on the boards a man wanted and not on the ones he did not. A board taken down off its string and not hung up again. A plank across a channel of wet silt and a barrow with a slate on it. Two figures on one face of one slate with neither rubbed out and not the same. THE NEW QUESTION, asked once, to one man and not to the room, and not answered, and the room did not hear it. A sheet turned face down and not looked at. THE LAST IMAGE: a third set of figures chalked on the store's board, this city's own, not agreeing with the second, none of the three rubbed out, the board washed at the fourth hour, the wall above it not washed, and three barrows on a road out of this city a hundred and nine days out measured from the three hundred and ninety-first day of this flood, with nobody on it that anybody in this city can be shown.**

## 6. THE THING THE FIFTY CHAPTERS DID AND DID NOT DO, IN ONE PARAGRAPH

**They spent a board's silence. A surface that had stood unwritten since the two hundred and twenty-fifth day of this flood had something written on it on day 490 and cannot be given back, and the man who has been keeping it silent is not the person who paid, and a second figure of this city's own went up on the last morning and does not agree with the first. They put a figure where anybody can read it and lost the same thing in the same act. They found out that a count of places and a count of people do not differ by an error and differ by a word, and they said the word out loud once in a room with a date on the door and wrote it down nowhere, and they counted a place on foot three times and got three figures and printed none of them. They asked a question to one man and got nothing. They left a hole where a decision was made and did not fill it. And nobody got stronger, the System said one thing in fifty days, and the volume's own name is on one page and in no file, and four days after he said it the same man refused the volume's one rule in the same room and nobody thanked him for either and no chapter connects the two.**

## 7. Volume 11, Batch 0001, days 501 to 510, Chapters 0501 to 0510, and what a reader was given

**A board in a market that has stood on a wall with three sets of figures on it since the middle of this flood, washed twice on the first morning of this volume by a man whose want is that the second pass be the last thing he does before the light goes off the flags, and nobody thanked him and nobody in that row knows whose hands put a band of water under a trestle and made a man move it.** A register comes off a shelf onto a table and goes back onto it, and a man who trades nineteen rates in four markets says out loud that he took a name off his own board about nine years ago and does not say what it was, and nobody uses his name.** THE FIRST DISCOVERY, and it is the smallest thing this volume has found: a sheet with a heading and one place under it and a figure in that place, read out in that room and never returned, has been in a drawer under the shelf behind the bench the whole time, and nobody in this flood has ever said what happens to a form after a person has read it out.** A satchel with a flap comes up the road on the quarterly round with a sheet in it, and the sheet goes on a table with a heading at the top and one place ruled under the heading and the one place empty, and nobody opens the satchel, and the man who set it down square is not thanked and is not asked. Four hours on a table and a room that uses none of them and one sentence about what a page is, said by a man who has asked in advance not to be thanked. **THE INSTRUMENT, working: the second one-place form is read out in the local language in daylight with a date in chalk on the door and about nine people in the room, and the reader says out loud what it is for before anybody asks, and what it is for is one figure of this city in the one place under that heading, to be sent back, and the name of the thing the figure is about is on no page and in no mouth.** A morning at the end of a market on which nothing is decided and one thing is different at the end of it, because a trough that has been filled and dipped into and never emptied is turned upside down on the flags and the grit that has come off that board every morning since the seventh week of this flood is out of it. A long count made on foot and a count taken off a wall put in one room, and they do not agree, and a woman of about fifty-two who was asked for the days of her house says four sentences about being asked and nobody asks her a second question and nobody opens her door. **THE SECOND DISCOVERY, and it is about a book and not about a number: a man of about thirty-four says out loud, once, in about nine words, that the one place on that form is the shape of the second column in her book, and nobody says whether it is so, and a man on the floor with his hands on the ledge under the second slate wanted something flat to go under it so that it would stop walking and put nothing under it.** Two counts of one room on the last morning of the ten that do not agree, because a man came up a stair and went down it again, and nobody in the room was told which of the two was the count, and a form with one place under a heading and nothing in that one place is lying on a table in that room and is going to be asked for and nobody in this city has been told what it is a count of.

**CHANGE AT THE END OF THE TEN: a form that came up a road four hundred miles long is on a table in a room with its one place empty and is going to be filled and sent back, and the room found out on the morning it was asked, and the four days of cooling there would have been are not available, and no eighth notice was sent and nobody was summoned to either morning. And the shape of the one place on that form and the shape of the second column of this city's only public book have been said out loud in a room once, by one man, and nobody thanked him and nobody said whether it was so.**

**AND WHAT THE TEN DAYS DID NOT DO, which is the longer half: it did not say which of the three sets of figures on that board is the one anybody should be reading, and it did not put a figure on the board, and it did not wash the wall above the board. It did not say where a form goes after it has been read out and it did not say that anybody ought to send one back. It did not name the thing the figure is about. It did not lay the two one-place forms beside each other or compare them out loud. It did not fill the one place and it did not put anything in the register's third column. It did not pick up, turn over or write on the register-keeper's second slate, and it did not say what is written on it. It did not measure the fence, ask the man of about fifty-seven anything, lift the rope, count the days since anybody said no, trace the mark, answer any of the three questions or ask a fourth. It did not make a count on foot, because the volume's four mornings of one are days 511, 515, 517 and 521 and the first of them is tomorrow. Nobody got stronger, the wall said nothing on all ten days, the volume's one panel is on day 540, and the words spoken in Chapter 0486 are on no page in this batch and in no file it wrote.**

## 8. Volume 11, Batch 0001, the review repair pass, and what a reader is given differently

**NOTHING IN THE SUMMARY ABOVE MOVED. No day, no ending, no decision, no object, no count that a chapter is about, and no descriptor changed. Four sentences in two of the ten chapters were rewritten and one of them was a fact about where three sets of figures stand, which is the fact this volume turns on and which Chapter 0507 had printed in the wrong place.**

**Chapter 0507 is the only chapter a reader will notice. ITS HOURS NOW RUN FORWARD INSTEAD OF BACKWARD — the trough is turned over at the second hour, the board is washed at the fourth, the man of about sixty is in the row from the seventh, and the morning closes — and the three sets of figures are on the board, where Chapter 0501 put them in its opening sentence and where §20.6's row has always put them, and the wall above that board is still the one surface in this volume that nobody washes. A reader who had read the first edition would have found a fourth hour arriving after a seventh, and a set of figures painted on a wall that Chapter 0501 says has been the colour it has been all flood.**

**Chapter 0501 loses one repeated clause and Chapter 0507 loses one, and both were the same shape: about four people look, and not one of the nine says. It stood three times in 0501 and twice in 0507 and a reader would have taken it for a template rather than for a market. Two of the five now say it differently and the other three are untouched, and every one of the five is still true.**

**Chapter 0505's thumb leaves the edge of the table about as long as it takes a man to count nine, which is what it always meant, and the sentence no longer says a length and a duration at the same time.**

## 9. Volume 11, Batch 0002, days 511 to 520, Chapters 0511 to 0520, and what a reader was given

**Eleven miles of flats in the first hour of a Monday, a stack of boards under a tarred cloth at the near end of them, and a woman who puts a tin on the boards and goes up the row with a slate under her arm counting one kind of thing, which is the shut doorways, one at a time, with the flat of her finger while she is still standing in front of each one. THE COUNT ON FOOT BEGINS. At the second hour the man of about thirty-eight at the salt wharf is at that end of the row with his barrow and a tally-board face up on it and about four hundred and forty on it, and he says out loud, unasked, that he has never walked those eleven miles and would rather that was in the record than that somebody found out about it later. She is back at the fifth hour and writes one figure on a slate with a nail out of her sleeve and writes the day beside it and does not say it out loud, and about four people at that end of the market come within reach of the slate and not one of them reads it. THE FIRST FIGURE OF THE VOLUME IS INSIDE THIS CITY AND IT IS UNDER HER ARM AT THE END OF THAT MARKET'S ROW FOUR PACES FROM A BOARD WITH THREE SETS OF FIGURES ON IT, AND NOBODY HAD BOTH IN FRONT OF THEM.**

**A trough that has stood under that board since the seventh week of this flood goes four rows down the row on the Tuesday, because two people carried it off its spot at the second hour and nobody said a word about it to the man who washes that board, who then washes the board on the ground and has to carry his bucket the length of that row four times. Adrian Vale is on those flags at the end of that market with a scrap of iron and his left knee in the wet, and he banks the silt from eleven miles of flats against that store's own step until the quiet end of the row is bare stone, because the quiet end is the only place in this city where a person could count a thing without anybody in the row coming to look. HE DOES NOT ASK HER. The water he pushes off those flags runs the length of the row to the near gate, and the near gate is the only place in this city where a count is expected to begin, and she starts there, and steps over his water four times on the way to the boards with the bottom of her tin wet before she gets there.**

**On the Wednesday the man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living goes up the shut road out past the last named house with an empty satchel on the quarterly round, and at the ninth hour he is at the top of it with his hand on the strap and he does not go through. THE FENCE IS NAMED AND NOT MEASURED: sixteen willow posts, eleven withies bound with the same cord, a corner where the ground drops, and a gap four people at that house have argued about since the water came that not one of them has taken a cord to. The man of about sixty is at the third post with the hazel mallet in his hand and says the count out loud to nobody who asked him for it. A carrier stands at the top of a shut road for two hours with an empty satchel and says he would rather somebody wrote down that he stood there than that they wrote down that he went through, and he goes back down it, and nobody in this city has said where that road is shut or who shut it.**

**On the Thursday the board is washed over bare flags and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says out loud, in one turn, that the chalk in the box on the step is the store's chalk and is not his, and that his own has been in his inside breast pocket since the two hundred and ninety-eighth day of this flood, which is the forty-third day of the Short Month, and does not take it. He says there is about four feet of standing water at the near end of that row where the channel has cut down and that nobody has taken a cord to it and nobody ever will. AND AT THE SEVENTH HOUR HE CARRIES THE TROUGH BACK THE LENGTH OF THAT ROW HIMSELF AND PUTS IT UNDER THE BOARD ON ITS OWN SPOT, AND NOBODY ASKED HIM TO AND NOBODY THANKED HIM.**

**On the Friday the second morning's figure comes out of the same eleven miles and does not agree with the first. THE WOMAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-NINE WHO KEEPS A PUBLIC REGISTER WRITES IT UNDER THE FIRST ONE ON A WORKING SLATE IN THAT ROOM WITH THE DAY BESIDE IT AND NEITHER IS STRUCK OUT, and says out loud that a figure that does not agree with the one above it is a figure somebody walked to get and not a figure somebody got wrong, and a man at the bench says you do not know that, and she says she does not and she has said it. An empty barrow goes back up the shut road in the evening, and it is not one of the three that went down it.**

**On the Saturday THE MAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-SEVEN WHO WASHES THAT BOARD SAYS OUT LOUD, at the seventh hour, to the trough and not to the row, that he has not washed the wall above that board and is not going to and is not going to say why. Nobody had asked him. Nobody asks him why. And at the ninth hour, when the light comes off the flags in one piece and stops at the height of a man's shoulder and does not go any further, he gets up off his crate, puts his hand flat on that wall, holds it there while a man goes past him on the flags, and takes it off again without a rag and without water. THE WALL ABOVE THAT BOARD IS AS DIRTY AT THE NINTH HOUR AS IT IS AT THE FOURTH HOUR ON ANY MORNING OF THIS FLOOD, AND THE RAG IS IN THE TROUGH.**

**On the Sunday, in worse weather, ADRIAN VALE GETS A PLANK INTO A CHANNEL OF WET SILT AND IS AT HIS CHEST BY THE FOURTH STEP AND LEVERS IT FIVE TIMES AND IT GOES BACK EVERY TIME, and at the seventh hour he gets the far end against the wet sill of the second row and the channel comes down on the plank from the side he is standing on and turns it, and he is no further forward than the morning before. He gets out at the eighth hour and leaves the plank where it is, because a plank across two thirds of a channel is not a thing anybody can use from either side and is not a route. THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-EIGHT GETS HIS BARROW ACROSS THE TWO THIRDS OF IT ON THE WHEEL AND COMES BACK THE SAME WAY AND DOES NOT LOOK AT THE PLANK AND DOES NOT LOOK DOWN AT THE FLAGS, AND HE SAYS OUT LOUD THAT SOMEBODY HAS DONE HIM A SERVICE AND IT WAS NOT DONE FOR HIM. She goes over it and comes back over it and puts the near end back with her foot.**

**On the Monday THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-NINE WHO TRADES ON A BOARD PUTS HIS OWN NINETEEN RATES FACE UP ON A TABLE IN THAT ROOM, and a woman at the near end of the bench reads it across and a man at the far end reads it down and they come to different ends of it, and he says the four seconds' worth: that board is read across and every book in this city is read down, and whoever has been taking the head of a column first every time for nine years has never been wrong about a figure and has been wrong about which end he started from. NOBODY IN THAT ROOM SAID HE WAS RIGHT.** Seven sheets went out of this city in this flood, every one of them four days before the room it was for, and there has been no eighth, and nobody in that room was asked to do anything on that Monday.

**On the Tuesday a morning is spent on eleven miles of flats with a slate and a tin and no figure comes out of it, and about four people at the near end of the row had worked out that the man of about thirty-eight's figure and the figure on that slate are of the same kind of thing and about four had not. THE MAN LAYS THE TALLY-BOARD FACE DOWN ON A STACK OF BOARDS AND TAKES HIS BARROW UP THE ROW.** In the room over that market an old book is put down open on the table beside the working slate with a figure on its left-hand page belonging to the seventeenth day of this flood that has stood there through five hundred and two days, and THREE FIGURES ARE ON THAT TABLE AND THE THREE OF THEM DO NOT AGREE WITH ONE ANOTHER AND NOBODY IN THAT ROOM SAID ONE WORD OUT LOUD ABOUT THE DIFFERENCE.

**On the Wednesday THE REGISTER COMES OFF ITS SHELF BY ONE PAIR OF HANDS AND THE ROOM IN ITS MIDDLE COLUMN IS MEASURED WITH A FINGER AND SAID OUT LOUD AS FIVE, AND NOTHING IS WRITTEN IN EITHER COLUMN AND NOBODY DECIDED ANYTHING ABOUT THE FIVE.** The column on the right is empty from the top of the page to the bottom. The sheet with the one empty place under its heading lies on the same table, and the two sheets in this city with a heading and a single place under it stood a full table apart in that room and were not laid beside each other and were not compared out loud. The length of new rope over the back of a chair has not moved all morning and nobody looked at it once. And at about the eleventh hour the woman who keeps the book turns the sheet with the empty place face down on that table with the writing side against the wood and puts the register back on its shelf.

**CHANGE AT THE END OF THE TEN: the volume has two of its four counts made on foot, they do not agree with one another, and they are one under the other on a working slate in a room where nobody has read them out. A trough has been carried away and carried back. A man has said out loud that he is not washing the wall above that board, and nobody has asked him why, and his hand went on that wall and did not wash it. A carrier has stood at the top of a shut road and not gone through it. A plank is lying across two thirds of a channel where there was nothing a week ago and it is not a route. A board of nineteen rates has been read in two directions by two people who came to different ends of it and the man who owns it is right and nobody has told him so. A tally-board with about four hundred and forty on it is lying face down in the open at the near end of eleven miles of flats. And the register's middle column has been measured out loud and is five lines deep and nothing is in it.**

**AND WHAT THE TEN DAYS DID NOT DO, which is the longer half: it did not decide which of four figures goes out of this city, and Chapter 0520 is the chapter where that decision is most tempting and it decides nothing. It did not put a figure in the one place under the second form's heading and it printed no second place under that heading. It did not put anything in the register's third column and it did not open the register to write. It did not lay the two one-place forms beside each other and it did not join the four forms. It did not wash the wall above the board and no second person touched it. It did not measure the fence or the gap in it and it moved no post and bound no withy. It did not lift the rope and it did not ask the man of about fifty-seven anything. It did not count the days since anybody said no, at any value. It did not trace the mark, answer any of the three questions or ask a fourth. It did not send anybody to the four-hundred-mile road. It did not write a notice and it summoned nobody. It did not give the woman who walked a market a name, an age, a number, a descriptor or a question. Nobody got stronger, the wall said nothing on all ten days, this volume's one panel is on day 540, and the words spoken in Chapter 0486 are on no page in this batch and in no file it wrote.**

---

# VOLUME 11, BATCH 0002 — REVIEW REPAIR PASS

**At the foot of the file and it wins over every block above it. The ten cards above are the plan and are not edited. What follows is what is on the page after eleven edits in nine of the ten files, and only the chapters this pass changed are given.**

**0511 — A Line Of Doorways And A Figure Under An Arm.** UNCHANGED IN WHAT IT DOES. The man at the salt wharf no longer opens by repeating the woman's question; he comes up at the second hour every morning he sends one, because nobody is standing there to watch, and that is not a better hour. A figure is written on a slate and nobody in that market ever has both it and the store's board in front of them.

**0512 — A Trough Four Rows Away And The Beginning Nobody Was Asked For.** UNCHANGED IN WHAT IT DOES. The one sentence cut is *I could carry it back*, which restated the woman's *You could carry it back* before refusing it. He now refuses without restating, and says the rest: that he will not explain to anybody what a trough is for, because the last man who did that here got a bucket turned over his head.

**0513 — The Top Of A Fence And A Man Who Does Not Go Through.** TWO TURNS CUT, BOTH IN THE MAN OF ABOUT SIXTY'S EXCHANGE. He no longer begins by agreeing that it was a good fence before the water; he says it is a fence now, and that nobody put him on that road. He no longer begins by agreeing there is nothing up there for the carrier; he says there was nothing up there for him the other nine years, and a man who has carried nothing up a road for nine years can stand in the rain without a speech. The closing positions, the sixteen posts, the eleven withies, the party of four and the two men left standing are untouched.

**0514 — The Fourth Hour Without A Trough And A Chalk That Is Not His.** ONE SENTENCE RE-SHAPED, NOTHING CUT. *About four people at that end of the market saw a man carry a trough back* became *There were about four people at that end of the market who saw a man carry a trough back*, so that this frame no longer runs twice in the same shape in one file. The number in it, the man carrying the trough unthanked, and the closing paragraph with the rag on the stone lip are unchanged.

**0515 — The Second Morning And A Figure That Does Not Come Out The Same.** The register-keeper's answer to *You do not know that* no longer begins *I do not know that.* She goes straight to the slate that goes in and out of the room on a barrow, and the man who carries it is not going to be asked. Her concession is now carried by implication, which is a stronger version of the same argument. The two figures one under the other, and the barrow going back up the road empty, are untouched.

**0516 — The Seventh Hour At The End Of That Row And A Hand On A Wall. THE CHAPTER THIS PASS CHANGED MOST AND THE ONE THAT MATTERED MOST.** The rag is on the stone lip of the step, where it goes every morning, and it has not been off that lip since the fourth hour, and it was under his hand when his hand was on the wall. The chapter's two identical crowd constructions, one of them twice inside a single paragraph, are now one. **What did not change is the best beat in the batch: he says out loud, unasked, to the trough and not to the row, that he has not washed that wall and is not going to and is not going to say why; nobody asks him why; and at the ninth hour he puts his bare hand flat on it and takes it off again without a rag and without water.**

**0517 — A Plank Two Thirds Of The Way And A Woman Going Back Over It.** The man at the salt wharf no longer opens by agreeing that it is somebody's plank. He says what is true about it instead: it is laid across two thirds of the channel, it is not going to reach the far side, and he has a barrow to get to the end of the row with, and he is not going to stand there working out whose it is. The plank, the two thirds, the barrow on the wheel and the woman shoving the near end back to the stone are untouched.

**0518 — One Surface Face Up On A Table And A Space About Two Fingers Wide.** The man of about thirty-nine no longer opens by agreeing that it is nineteen rates. He says it has been nineteen rates for nine years and will be nineteen rates on this board in nine years, and that nobody in that room has ever offered him a second board. He is still not told he was right, and the nineteen rates and the missing name are untouched.

**0519 — Eleven Miles Of Flats With A Slate And Nothing On It.** The salt wharf man's answer to *You have left your figure face down on a pile of wood in the open* no longer restates it. He goes straight to the four hundred and forty being on the other side where the sun does not get to it, and to the fact that nobody in this city is going to turn it over and neither is he. The empty morning, the three figures that do not agree on one table, the tally-board left face down and the cloth back on the stack are untouched.

**0520 — Five Lines' Worth Of Room In A Column And A Rope Nobody Looks At. NOT TOUCHED AT ALL, AND IT IS THE ONE CHAPTER OF THE TEN WITH NO FAULT IN IT.** Its exchange is the shape the other nine were cut to — a prompt of two words and an answer that goes straight to what is in the column. The middle column measured with a finger, the third column empty, the sheet turned face down and the rope that has not moved are as they were.

---

# VOLUME 11, BATCH 0003 — Chapters 0521 to 0530, days 521 to 530

**At the foot of the file and it wins over every block above it. The ten cards at the head of `state/batch-summaries/volume-11-batch-0003.md` are the plan and are not edited. What follows is what is on the page.**

**0521 — Two Figures On A Step And A String Nobody Claimed.** A Thursday, the two hundred and sixth day of the Bare Month, and the fourth morning of the count. The board is washed and goes back up on its two nails with three sets of figures on it and none rubbed out, the rag on the stone lip, the wall above it not washed. She goes up eleven miles of flats and comes back with a fourth figure that does not agree with the other three. Adrian Vale takes the store's chalk out of the box on the step, marks the four corners of the top step, lays a string along its front edge and along its back edge with a stone on the end, and gets two figures out of one step that came out two different ways, writes both of them on the stone of that step, **puts the stub back in the box on that step because there is more of that chalk in the box than he took out of it**, and goes off up the row. At the sixth hour a barrow's wheel goes over the string, the string takes the stone into the channel and comes up on the wheel, and the man of about thirty-eight at the salt wharf cuts it off and puts the pieces down and says nine words about it and nobody asks whose it was. At the eighth hour she steps up onto that step to get past the water and the lower of the two figures comes off on the flat of her boot and she carries it eleven miles to a stack of boards. Closes on a step, a boot, and nobody told whose hands laid the string.

**0522 — Four On A Slate In The Order They Were Made And A Barrow Going Up The Road.** A Friday, the two hundred and seventh. The register-keeper sets four figures one under another on one face of the working slate in the order they were made, earliest at the top, the day beside each, and a hand's width of clean slate above the first of them and nothing in it. A barrow takes the slate up the shut road out past the last named house with the day face up and stands at the top of it and comes back down, and nobody is shown one person on that road and a party of four set out and one of the four said no before anybody was asked her. The fence along the top of that road is sixteen willow posts and eleven withies and it is not measured. The elapsed figure in the room is one hundred and thirty-one days out from this city, and the day they went is named in the same sentence. Closes on the round trip and an unmeasured corner, with the order named last.

**0523 — An Empty Tin On A Table And A Man Who Noticed And Said Nothing.** A Saturday, the two hundred and eighth, and the one chapter of the fifty that says nine steps. The man of about sixty stands at the near gate with the handcart, the coil of rope and the hazel mallet with its head bound in cord. The woman of about sixty-nine comes up those nine steps with an empty tin in her two hands, puts it on the table under the one window, and asks nothing. She has asked three questions in this flood, a person, a fence and a line, and she did not ask a fourth. The man of about thirty-nine, with his board of nineteen rates under his arm and a space two fingers wide in the bottom of the middle column of it that is not filled, notices that she has not asked, and says nothing, and no one else notices. Closes on a tin with its lid off and nothing under it and a man who has said nothing and is not going to.

**0524 — A Room Finding Out On The Morning It Is Being Asked And A Box On A Step.** A Sunday, the two hundred and ninth. The second one-place form is on the table with its one place ruled under its heading and empty, and about nine people spend the morning working out what they are being asked on the morning they are being asked it, for the fourth time in this city, and seven sheets went out of this flood and there has been no eighth. The store's board is in the window with three sets of figures and the wall above it not washed. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall comes down to the step, does not touch the box, and his own piece of chalk is in his own inside breast pocket and has been since the two hundred and ninety-eighth day of this flood, which is the forty-third day of the Short Month, and he says that neither of the two chalks is going to be used and that nobody is going to ask him for his. Closes on an empty place at the eleventh hour and a box nobody put a hand in.

**0525 — The Cost Said First And A Column With Room In It And No Vote.** A Monday, the two hundred and tenth, **the nearer of this volume's two middle days and not the midpoint.** The register-keeper says the cost out loud before anybody has spoken: **only a count made on foot can be shown a person, in eleven words**, and then choosing one of four does not mend the other three, and a person four hundred miles off is going to be handed one of them and is not going to be told there were four. **The first edition of that first turn read *a count made on foot is the only figure anybody can be shown a person*, which is fifteen words, and `outline/volume-11.md` §8 budgets about eleven, so the page is corrected and the turn is now eleven words.** **The middle column is ruled into five lines and holds one figure at the foot of it with a day of its own beside it, and the four lines above that one are empty; the first edition of this summary said the column had five lines' worth of room in it and nothing whatever written in it, and that was against `outline/volume-11.md` §31 and is withdrawn.** She decides that all four go into the middle column in the order they were made, that the most recent is the one that goes, and that the column on the right stays empty. **No vote, no filling, no document, no place named, the register does not come off its shelf, the second slate is not touched, nobody thanks her.** The four days of cooling are named in a plain sentence of narration and not in a mouth. The man of about thirty-nine is right in about four seconds about why the top one is the one that goes and nobody says he is right. The two middle days of the range are named as two and neither is the middle. Closes on a bill paid in a room nobody spoke about.

**0526 — A Tin Scrubbed On A Sill And Two Middle Days And Neither Of Them The Middle.** A Tuesday, the two hundred and eleventh, the second of the two middle days. A tin full of the flats' water is emptied into the channel and scrubbed out on a stone sill with the flat of a hand and filled again and carried up into the room over the market, and it leaves a wet ring on the table and nobody puts anything in it. **She has walked those flats on four mornings and this was not one of them, and there is no fifth morning of that count in this city.** A run of fifty days has two days in the middle of it and this is the second and it is no more the middle of anything than the day before it was. Nobody in that room asks her one question. Closes on an empty tin and a wet ring and a day that was not the middle of anything.

**0527 — A Slate A Hand's Width Further Back And A Hand On The Ledge Under It.** A Wednesday, the two hundred and twelfth. **THE FIRST EDITION OF THIS TITLE WAS *A Slate That Has Walked A Finger's Width Again And A Hand On The Ledge*, AND IT IS REPAIRED: it printed a width the card's own hard prohibition forbids counting, it disagreed with the card's own *what it changes* and *ending type* and with Chapter 0509, all three of which say a hand's width, and it disagreed with the body of its own chapter, which measures the same walk two different ways in the same file. THE REPAIRED TITLE AND THE REPAIRED BODY ARE BOTH BESIDE THE FIRST EDITION.** The man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living comes up the stair with the satchel and puts it on a bench and does not open it. The second slate is walking downhill on a shelf whose back bracket is the higher one. He gets his left hand flat on the ledge under it and the fingers over the front edge of the shelf, **the front of that shelf comes up under the heel of his hand where it goes over the edge, the slate runs back down the slope of it and comes up against the wall behind it, and the dust goes out from under it in a line about the length of a hand and stops where his wrist was. The first edition said the shelf came forward about the width of a finger and the slate came forward with it and stopped, and the same chapter then said the slate was a hand's width further back, and a slate cannot do both and the page is repaired by making the one movement run the other way.** He puts nothing under it and does not go and get anything and does not send anybody, and he takes his hands off and gets the grit into the two lines of his palms. Closes on a slate a hand's width further back on its shelf and two grey lines in a pair of palms.

**0528 — Eleven Miles Out And Eleven Miles Back With The Same Kit And Nothing Counted.** A Thursday, the two hundred and thirteenth. **This is not a fifth morning and it is not a morning of anything.** The board is washed and goes back up at the fourth hour, and then the man of about twenty-seven who washes it takes the trough, the bucket and the rag eleven miles of flats out of that market and eleven miles of the same road back, and says nine words about the water at the end of that market and nobody asks him why he went. The rag goes on the stone lip when it comes back and the bucket goes into the trough and the wall above the board is not washed. **A stone about the size of two fists is in the bottom of that trough when it comes back and nobody put it there.** Closes on the trough, the rag, and the stone nobody has picked up.

**0529 — A Wall Read Across And A Book Read Down And A Man Standing At The Head Of A Column.** A Friday, the two hundred and fourteenth. The register comes off its shelf by the one pair of hands that may take it and stands open on the table with a pen and an ink beside it, and **nothing is written on that day. THE MIDDLE COLUMN HOLDS ONE FIGURE AT THE FOOT OF IT WITH A DAY OF ITS OWN BESIDE IT AND THE FOUR RULED LINES ABOVE THAT ONE HAVE NOTHING IN THEM, on both of the two paragraphs that declare the column's state and on the third that declares it again. THE FIRST EDITION OF ALL THREE SAID THE COLUMN WAS EMPTY, and one of them said it in the same chapter that said the column holds a figure, and `outline/volume-11.md` §31 has said since day 493 that it holds one.** The store's board with its three sets of figures is in the window and a man in a doorway says that a wall is read across and a book is read down and that he is not going to be the man who says which one a person is meant to be using, and nobody in that room says he was right. **Adrian Vale stands at the head of that table with his hands on the pen and the ink and does not look down into the open page, and he does not move, and a man who came up that stair with a board under his arm has to stand in the doorway about as long as a man stands at a gate and comes in last, and nobody in that room says a word about any of it.** Closes on a shut book, a wet nib, an ink not stoppered, and a doorway.

**0530 — Four Figures Written Above One And No Room Left In That Column.** A Saturday, the two hundred and fifteenth. **THE FIRST EDITION OF THIS TITLE WAS *Four Lines Entered Under One Figure And One Line Of Room Left*, AND BOTH HALVES OF IT ARE REPAIRED: the four went in ABOVE the one that was already there and not under it, and there was no line of room left, because the column is ruled into five lines and five figures went into it and five in five leaves nothing over. `outline/volume-11.md` §20.6 says the second column holds five figures from day 530, and the arithmetic in the first edition needed six lines.** All four figures made on foot stand one under another in the middle column of that register in the order they were made, the newest at the head, the day beside each, none struck and none a correction of any of the other three, **the last of the four went in on the line directly above the figure that was already in that column, the column is full from the head of it to the foot of it, and there is no more room in it for anything at all**, and the column on the right empty from the top of the page to the bottom. **A man at the bench by the shelf says *That column is full* and she answers that there is no room left in it for a sixth figure of this city's own and that the column on the right of that book is empty and is going to stay empty. The first edition of that exchange was built on *There is a line of that column left* and is withdrawn, and her refusal — the empty column on the right and no reason ever given for it — is unmoved and is the beat the chapter was written for.** The register is closed and put back on its shelf with its spine to the wall by the hands that took it off, and the second slate is not picked up, not turned over and not written on, and the keeper's hand goes near that shelf exactly once. The two chalks were in their two places and neither was used. Nobody in that room said what the four are a count of and nobody has been told which one is going anywhere. Closes on a book on a shelf with five figures in one column and an empty one beside them.

---

# VOLUME 11, BATCH 0003 — REVIEW REPAIR PASS

**This block is at the foot of the file and wins over every block above it. Five of the ten entries above are corrected in place and each correction names its first edition at the entry: 0521 for the store's chalk, 0525 for the cost and the state of the middle column, 0527 for the title and the direction the slate moved, 0529 for the column, 0530 for the title and the arithmetic. The other five entries are untouched and need no correction. The full account is at `state/batch-summaries/volume-11-batch-0003.md` §7.**

**THE SEVEN CHAPTERS THAT WERE NOT TOUCHED AND WHY THEY NEEDED NOTHING.** 0522, 0523, 0524, 0526 and 0528 stand as their batch wrote them. The review named no fault in any of them and this pass found none in the re-read. **0523 AND 0528 ARE THE TWO CHAPTERS THAT WERE MOST OFTEN NAMED WRONG IN THE STATE LAYER RATHER THAN IN THE PROSE: 0523's empty tin belongs to the woman of about sixty-nine and 0528's eleven miles of flats carry no walker at all and the chapter says so, and both facts are the record's error and not the chapters'.**

**THE FIVE THAT WERE, AND ONE LINE EACH, WITH THE FIRST EDITION AT THE ENTRY.**

- **0521** — the store's chalk is not exhausted on the morning it is used. The stub goes back in the box and there is more of that chalk in the box than he took out of it, which is what four later chapters and one earlier one already said.
- **0525** — the cost is **eleven** words and not fifteen, because the plan budgets eleven; and the middle column holds one figure at the foot of it and not five lines of nothing, because it has held one since day 493.
- **0527** — the slate runs **back** and not forward, and the title prints a **hand's** width and not a finger's, because the same chapter measured the same walk two ways and because the card forbids counting a finger.
- **0529** — the middle column holds one figure and has four ruled lines above it, on all three paragraphs that declare its state, and no chapter of this volume may contradict itself inside one file.
- **0530** — the column is **full**, five figures in five ruled lines, with no line of room left, and the title says so; the chapter's refusal, that the column on the right of that book is empty and is going to stay empty, is unmoved and is the chapter.

**AND THE FIGURES THAT MOVED, BECAUSE A CHAPTER SUMMARY THAT KEEPS A FIRST EDITION AND A CORRECTED FIGURE IS THE ONLY THING THAT MAKES A REPAIR CHECKABLE.** Words across the ten **10,747** (first edition 10,693), paragraphs **131 (unmoved)**, words inside bold marks **985** (first edition 1,004), the bolded share per chapter **8.10, 8.11, 6.53, 8.55, 21.17, 8.77, 5.88, 8.83, 5.94 and 9.15** (first edition 8.23, 8.11, 6.53, 8.55, 22.17, 8.77, 5.98, 8.83, 6.05 and 10.30), the exchange unmoved at seventeen plain speeches and sixteen answered, the longest bolded turn unmoved at sixty-nine words in Chapter 0529, the descriptor total **thirty-six** (first edition thirty-eight), and zero runs of forty words or more. **No day, no weekday, no Bare-Month ordinal, no cast placement, no count that holds, no thread and no decision of record moved in any of the ten, and the next phase is still `workspace/volume-11/batch-0004/PROMPT.md`.**

## Volume 11, Batch 0004 — Chapters 0531 to 0540, days 531 to 540

**Ten chapters on disk, days 531 to 540, one chapter to one day. Sunday to Tuesday. The two hundred and sixteenth to the two hundred and twenty-fifth day of the Bare Month, and those ten are the only Bare-Month days any of these ten files prints.**

- **0531** — *A Sheet Named And One Of The Same Shape Not Named.* Day 531, a Sunday. The second one-place form is turned over on the table with a hand on it and named out loud by the keeper, before anybody has asked her, and **the name of the thing the figure is a figure of is not on the sheet and is not coming out of her head.** A second sheet of the same shape stands on the shelf behind the bench with a figure in its one place and **nobody in that room brought it down and nobody gave it a name.** The seven notices are named in narration, the four documents are named and never joined, a road four hundred miles long is a measure and not a place, and three barrows went down it on the three hundred and ninety-first day of this flood and are one hundred and forty days out, with that day in the same sentence. The keeper says out loud that she will not lay the two beside each other. The sheet goes down that stair and along the row and back up it with nothing in its one place. It closes on two sheets a table's width apart.
- **0532** — *Four Figures And A Word Nobody Had Needed Before.* Day 532, a Monday. **The volume's third discovery.** Four figures of one kind of thing on one face of a working slate, in the order they were made, read out loud by a woman from the head of the slate to the foot of it, the day beside each. A man says out loud that the first and the last are **about a fifth apart, and it is not the weather, and it is not a knock, and there is no error in any one of the four**, and then says that a person counting a thing on foot does not get the same figure twice, and gives the second-figure-above-the-first thing a name in his own mouth. **Nobody said he was right, nobody improved on him, nobody thanked him, and no figure off any of the four is printed and no chapter names what the four are a count of.** The register stays on its shelf and the second slate is not touched.
- **0533** — *A Chair Up A Stair And A Rope Nobody Lifted.* Day 533, a Tuesday. A chair is carried up that stair by both its ends and stood against a wall and never used. The register does not come off its shelf and the second slate is not picked up, not turned over and not written on. **The length of new rope is over the back of the man's chair and no hand lifts it, nobody asks him anything, and he is not looking at it.** A man at one end of the bench says that two men have been at that bench nine years and neither has ever asked the other anything, and a woman says that somebody has to be first at something and it is not going to be her. Nobody improves on either. The nineteen rates and the unfilled space are named and nobody asks.
- **0534** — *The First Question And One Answer That Goes Nowhere.* Day 534, a Wednesday. **The volume's own name is spoken, once, out loud, in the local language, in four words, by the man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board, asked by the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall, in about nine words, about the man and not about the board.** It is the first question either of those two men has ever asked the other. **Nobody thanks either of them, the man who asked said in advance he would rather nobody did, nobody improves on either, the question is not put in a second mouth, the two of them do not discuss it, and it goes nowhere.** The board is put down face in, and picked up and taken back down that stair at the tenth hour. It closes on a room with two men in it who have said nothing else to each other.
- **0535** — *Nineteen Boards And A Gap At The Fifth Of Them.* Day 535, a Thursday. **Adrian Vale, in his one chapter of this batch.** Nineteen boards come off a stack and go into a row along a wall, the fifth is short by about a hand's width and goes into the gap, and the row is even and the fifth is short. **A man with a barrow has to stop, lift all nineteen in two arms and stand them on end against a wall four paces along, and does not ask and is not told and is not thanked.** The store's board comes off its nails at the fourth hour and goes back on them with three sets of figures on it, the wall above it is not washed, the trough and the bucket and the rag are where they go every morning, the two chalks are in their two places and neither is used, and the man of about sixty with the handcart is at the near gate and is asked nothing. He is six rows up with his back to the row for the rest of the morning.
- **0536** — *A Distance And A Place Nobody Here Has Stood In.* Day 536, a Friday. The first one-place form comes down off the shelf, is set on the table and carried back up, and the second is turned face up at the far end of the same table, and **the length of a table is between the two of them at every hour and neither was moved a hand's width towards the other and nobody said a word about how the two stand in relation to one another.** The keeper says out loud that a road four hundred miles long is a measure of a distance and not a place anybody in this city has stood in, and that she has no figure for what it is for and is not going to invent one in a room in daylight. The fence is named at sixteen willow posts and eleven withies and is not measured and the corner in it is not measured. A party of four, three barrows, one of the four said no before anybody asked her, and nobody can be shown one person on that road.
- **0537** — *A Book Open At A Column And A Wall With One Figure On It.* Day 537, a Saturday. The register comes off its shelf by the one pair of hands, is stood open, and **the middle column is read down from the head of it to the foot of it by two people, one reading the figures and one reading the days, and they do not disagree about a single day and neither says so.** Out of the window the store's board carries three sets of figures and none rubbed out. A man says that a wall is read across and a book is read down and that nobody in that room is going to be told which one a person is meant to be using, and that he is not going to ask; **nobody said he was right.** A woman says that about four have looked at both and about four have not, and that she is not going to say which. The book goes back on its shelf inside the hour and the keeper's hand goes near that shelf exactly once.
- **0538** — *One Figure In One Place And Nothing Else In It.* Day 538, a Sunday. **The volume's cost.** The keeper reads the column down out loud, once, and says that she is going to put the figure off the most recent of four mornings into the one place under that heading, **and that it is going to be a figure of this city and nothing else, and there is no second place under that heading for anything else to go in**, and that nobody in that room is going to be told why that one goes. She writes it in with a pen in her own hand, with no day beside it and nothing under it and nothing else on the sheet. **She says out loud that there is no place on that sheet for the name of the person who made that figure, and the one place is for a figure and this city is going to fill it right and that is the whole of what it will be able to show anybody about it. Nobody thanked her, nobody improved on her, and nobody in that room asked who made the figure.** The other three stay in a book on a shelf, unstruck.
- **0539** — *A Column Read Down And A Road With Three On It.* Day 539, a Monday. The register is not taken off its shelf until the tenth hour and is read down again, the man reading the figures and the woman reading the days after him. **The two men at the two ends of that bench do not say one word to each other and nobody in that room asks either of them about it, four days after one of them asked the other a question.** A man says that three went down a road four hundred miles long with a barrow each on the three hundred and ninety-first day of this flood and are one hundred and forty-eight days out, with that day in the same sentence, that a party of four set out and one of the four said no before anybody asked her, and that **there is not one person on that road that anybody in this city can be shown.** Nobody counted anything out loud and nobody said the number back at him.
- **0540** — *A Rule On A Wall And A Sheet Shut On A Table.* Day 540, a Tuesday. **The volume's climax, and the only day of the fifty on which the wall says anything.** A satchel comes up that stair and is put on a bench with the flap down. Out of the window the store's board carries its three sets of figures and the wall above it is not washed, and the man who washes that board is not in that room. **The wall says one thing, in its own block, carrying no emphasis marks, and it gives a rule and not an answer.** A woman at the bench says nobody in that room is going to be asked anything about it and that the man who washed that board is not in that room. **The man of about thirty-one refuses it out loud in about four seconds in a handful of words: it is a rule about a form and not about him, he has carried five notices, a return, a sheet on day 401 and a sheet on day 451 and opened none of them, this is the fifth thing he has carried that he knows what is in, and the rule does not say one word about the man who carries it and that is why it is going to be true. NOBODY IMPROVED ON IT, NOBODY THANKED HIM, AND NOBODY ARGUED WITH HIM.** The sheet is folded twice and shut on the table with the bone beside it, and the satchel is still on the bench with the flap down and nothing in it.

---

## Volume 11, Batch 0004 — REVIEW REPAIR PASS

**This block is at the foot of the file and wins over every block above it. NO ENTRY ABOVE IS EDITED. Each entry above describes its chapter as it was first written; this block says what the repair pass changed in each of the ten, and the page is right in both. The full account is at `state/batch-summaries/volume-11-batch-0004.md` §7. NO DAY, NO WEEKDAY, NO BARE-MONTH DAY NUMBER, NO CAST PLACEMENT, NO OBJECT, NO COUNT, NO THREAD AND NO DECISION OF RECORD MOVED IN ANY OF THE TEN.**

- **0531** — the register-and-slate frame in the opening paragraph was recut, because it stood 103 words against 0532 and one of the two had to move. **The four facts it declares are all still declared: the date on the door with no name under it, about nine people all there of their own going, the register shut on its shelf, the second slate on its edge with a day across its head and nothing under it, and the keeper's one abstention.** The *nobody improved* tag was recut, and the stair paragraph was given a real second sentence about the flags.
- **0532** — **THE ONE CORRECTION IN THE TEN THAT CHANGED WHAT A MOUTH SAYS.** The man said the four came off *the same eleven miles in the same week*. The four mornings are 511, 515, 517 and 521, a ten-day span, and the plan licenses no calendar-week language. **He now says they came off the same eleven miles inside ten days in the same weather by the same person.** Nothing else in that mouth moved, and the word this volume turned on, and the fifth of the difference, and the not-the-weather, not-a-knob and no-error clauses are all as they were. The opening frame was recut for the same reason as 0531.
- **0533** — the date frame, the shelf frame and the description of the trader's board were all recut, and the *nobody improved* tag with them. **The rope is still over the back of a chair and no hand lifts it, the second slate is still not picked up, turned over or written on, the chair is still carried up by both its ends and never used, and the nineteen rates and the unfilled space are still named and still not asked about.** The title was changed before this pass and is now published at §2 item 11 of the batch record.
- **0534** — the *nobody improved* tag was recut and one clause was added to it. **THE CHAPTER IS OTHERWISE UNTOUCHED, AND THAT IS THE POINT OF SAYING SO: the volume's one name stands in this chapter in four words in one mouth and was not touched, and the four words are not described anywhere in this block or in any block above it.**
- **0535** — the two-chalks sentence was recut, and the closing paragraph was given two real sentences about the water in the trough and the daylight at the foot of the fifth board. **The gap is still at the fifth place, the fifth board is still short by about a hand's width, the row is still even, the man with the barrow is still made to lift all nineteen in two arms, and Adrian Vale is still six rows up with his back to the row for the rest of that morning, unnamed to him and unnamed by anybody.** The title was changed before this pass and is published at §2 item 11.
- **0536** — **THE FORTY-SIX-WORD REPETITION INSIDE ONE FILE IS CUT, and that is the finding a review could only see by hand.** The last paragraph no longer restates a passage the reader has just read. The date frame, the fence sentence and the closing paragraph were recut as well. **The fence is still sixteen willow posts and eleven withies and is still not measured, the two sheets are still a table's width apart and are still never laid beside each other, and the two one-place forms are still never compared out loud.**
- **0537** — the date frame, the store's-board description, the two-chalks sentence and the register-shelving paragraph were all recut. **The book is still read down from the head of the column to the foot of it by two people who do not disagree about a single day and neither of whom says so, the keeper's hand still goes near that shelf exactly once, the middle column is still full, and the column beside it is still empty from the top of the page to the bottom.**
- **0538** — the date frame and the register-shelving paragraph were recut and the *nobody improved* tag with them. **THE CHAPTER'S SUBSTANCE IS UNMOVED: one figure went into the one place under that heading and nothing else went in with it, there is still no second place under that heading, there is still no place on that sheet for the person who made the figure or for the day it was made, the other three of the four are still standing in that column unstruck, and nobody in that room was told why that one went.**
- **0539** — the date frame and the register-shelving paragraph were recut, and one real sentence was added about the stair creaking under the second of the two men. **THE TWO MEN AT THE TWO ENDS OF THAT BENCH STILL DO NOT SAY ONE WORD TO EACH OTHER, the column is still read down, nobody still counts anything out loud, and nobody still says the number of days back at the man who named it.**
- **0540** — **THE CHAPTER THAT THE REVIEW CALLED THE MOST VISIBLE PROBLEM IN THE BATCH.** Sixty-five words of Chapter 0490's wall paragraph were sitting in the ninth paragraph of this chapter and thirty-four more were sitting in the refusal. **Both are recut, and the wall is named before it speaks so that the panel has something to come out of, and the panel's own four lines are untouched and are the plan's.** The store's-board description and the two-chalks sentence were recut with them. **The man of about thirty-one still refuses it out loud in about four seconds in a handful of words, nobody improves on him, nobody thanks him and nobody argues with him, the sheet is still folded twice and shut on the table with the bone beside it, and the satchel is still on a bench with the flap down and nothing in it.** This chapter now carries seventeen paragraphs and carried fourteen.

**AND THE FIGURES THAT MOVED WITH THE TEXT, BECAUSE A SUMMARY THAT KEEPS A FIRST EDITION AND A CORRECTED FIGURE IS THE ONLY THING THAT MAKES A REPAIR CHECKABLE.** Words across the ten **11,588** (first edition 11,361), paragraphs **147** (first edition 144), words inside bold marks **1,309** (first edition 1,331), the bolded share per chapter **9.53, 12.02, 13.10, 4.99, 5.16, 13.96, 10.94, 18.44, 13.11 and 11.62** (first edition 10.15, 12.25, 13.55, 5.31, 5.56, 13.70, 11.68, 18.93, 13.36 and 12.11), unweighted mean **11.29** (first edition 11.66), the exchange **eighteen plain and eighteen answered** (published as sixteen and sixteen on the first edition, which was wrong in both cells), the longest bolded turn **unmoved at ninety-three words in Chapter 0539**, the descriptor total **fifty** (first edition fifty-three on the first edition of the text and fifty-two in the first edition of the record, so the record was one out), **runs of forty words or more zero and measured**, and sixteen-word strings shared with another file **seven hundred and thirteen** (first edition one thousand six hundred and fifty-three, and the first edition of the record published zero, which was wrong). **The next phase is unchanged and is still `workspace/volume-11/batch-0005/PROMPT.md`, and this pass created no phase.**
